\title{Thermodynamic Potentials, Ensembles, and Response Functions\\
\large A working guide for graduate students}
\author{}
\date{}

\begin{document}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
These notes follow one logical chain from the first and second laws of
thermodynamics to the statistical ensembles and to the measurable response
functions. Heat and work are defined first, and the first law fixes the internal
energy as a function of state. A short mathematical interlude collects what is
needed afterwards: convex and concave functions, the Legendre transform and its
geometric content, homogeneous functions, and Lagrange multipliers. The
thermodynamic potentials are then built from the fundamental relation
$U(S,V,N)$, together with the Euler and Gibbs--Duhem relations that extensivity
imposes. The second law is introduced next, and its consequences are derived in
order: the reversible heat $\dbar Q_{\rm rev}=T\,dS$, the concavity of the
entropy, the minimum principles of the potentials, and the stability
inequalities for every response function. Phase transitions are read off the
geometry of the potentials: a first-order transition is a flat segment of
$U(S,V,N)$ and a kink of $G(T,P,N)$, a continuous transition a loss of
analyticity in the second derivatives. The statistical ensembles compute the
potentials as logarithms of partition functions, and their equivalence is
stated precisely. The last two sections descend to the microscopic level: every
partition function is a Laplace transform of the many-body density of states,
so that thermodynamics is a filter on the spectrum, and the low-energy shape of
that density of states fixes the observed power laws; and the entropy and the
second law are traced to the part of the Hamiltonian that no description
contains. Peripheral derivations, worked mean-field examples and numerical
practice are collected in the appendices.
\end{abstract}

\tableofcontents

\section{Notation and conventions}
\label{sec:notation}

$k_B$ is Boltzmann's constant and $\beta=1/k_BT$. We write $\dbar Q$ and
$\dbar W$ (rather than $dQ$, $dW$) for infinitesimal amounts of heat and work,
which are not changes of any function of state (Appendix~\ref{app:exact}). Work
is counted positive when done \emph{on} the system, so $\dbar W=-P\,dV$ for
reversible compression. The three textbooks used throughout are Swendsen
\cite{Swendsen}, Schwabl \cite{Schwabl} and Plischke and Bergersen
\cite{Plischke}; they are cited where their treatment should be consulted.

Four notational collisions are standard and are flagged once. $\Omega$ is the
grand potential throughout, the microcanonical multiplicity being written
$W(E,V,N)$. $\Delta$ is the gap exponent in Section~\ref{sec:critical} and the
isothermal--isobaric partition function in Section~\ref{sec:ensembles}.
$\omega$ is the grand potential density $\Omega/V$ and the leading
correction-to-scaling exponent of Appendix~\ref{app:fss}. And $\beta$ is the
inverse temperature except in lists of critical exponents, where it is the
order-parameter exponent $m\sim|t|^{\beta}$; where that could be ambiguous the
inverse temperature is written out.

\section{Heat, work and the first law}
\label{sec:heat}

\subsection{Equilibrium states and state variables}
\label{sec:states}

Thermodynamics describes a macroscopic body by a handful of numbers. A body
left to itself, enclosed by walls that fix its volume and keep matter and heat
in or out, settles after a relaxation time into a state in which these numbers
stop changing: an \emph{equilibrium state}.\footnote{\label{fn:equilibrium}%
\textbf{Equilibrium.} A macroscopic state whose measurable properties are
constant in time and in which no macroscopic currents of energy, matter or
momentum flow. The definition is relative to an observation time: a variable
that relaxes much more slowly than the measurement is treated as a fixed
parameter rather than as a state variable (Section~\ref{sec:found_protocol}).}
The \emph{state postulate} asserts that the equilibrium state of a simple,
one-component system is fixed by its internal energy $U$, its volume $V$ and
its particle number $N$, and by nothing else: every other equilibrium property
is a function of $(U,V,N)$. Each additional way of doing work on the body
(magnetising it, straining it) adds one variable to the list, and each
additional chemical species adds one particle number.

A variable is \emph{extensive} if it doubles when two identical copies of the
body are put together ($U$, $V$, $N$, the entropy $S$, the magnetisation $M$)
and \emph{intensive} if it stays the same ($T$, $P$, the chemical potential
$\mu$, the field $\mathcal{H}$). Extensive variables can be prescribed by
independent physical operations, by walling off a volume, admitting particles
or supplying energy, and are therefore the natural independent variables of the
theory. Intensive variables will appear as derivatives.

The content of the state postulate is a separation of time scales. The
microscopic degrees of freedom, some $10^{23}$ of them, relax in
$10^{-13}$--$10^{-11}\,\mathrm{s}$, and their collective effect on a
macroscopic measurement is an average with relative fluctuations of order
$N^{-1/2}$. A macroscopic variable that relaxes on the time scale of the
measurement, an order parameter near a transition, the extent of a slow
chemical reaction, the configurational coordinate of a glass, must be added to
the list; one that relaxes much more slowly is frozen and acts as a constraint.
The postulate fails where neither holds, and the failure is visible: a
ferromagnet driven around a hysteresis loop returns to the same $(T,\mathcal{H})$
with a different magnetisation, so that no single-valued function of
$(T,\mathcal{H})$ describes it. On the equilibrium branch, reached by waiting or
by field cooling, everything below applies.

\subsection{Work}
\label{sec:work}

Work is energy delivered \emph{to the system} through a parameter that the
experimenter controls: the position of a piston, the strength of an applied
field, the strain imposed by a clamp. For a fluid compressed quasi-statically
by a piston the work done on the system is
\[
  \dbar W = -P\,dV ,
\]
positive when the volume decreases. Each further control parameter adds its own
term, and the pattern is always (intensive variable)$\times d$(extensive
variable):
\[
  \dbar W = -P\,dV + \mathcal{H}\,dM + \sum_{ij}V\sigma_{ij}\,d\epsilon_{ij}
  + \mathbf{E}\cdot d\mathbf{P} + \mu\,dN + \dots ,
\]
with $\mathcal{H}\,dM$ for magnetic work, $V\sigma_{ij}\,d\epsilon_{ij}$ for
elastic work against the stress $\sigma_{ij}$, $\mathbf{E}\cdot d\mathbf{P}$
for dielectric work, and $\mu\,dN$ the energy brought in with $dN$ particles
admitted from a reservoir at chemical potential $\mu$. The terms simply add
because the control parameters are taken to be \emph{independent}: each can be
moved while all the others are held fixed, the work for a small displacement
of all of them is then linear in the displacements $dV$, $dM$, $d\epsilon_{ij}$,
\dots, and the coefficient of each is the work for moving that parameter
alone. If two controls are tied to each other, as when the volume of a
magnetostrictive sample follows the applied field, the dependent one is
eliminated first, so that the list contains only parameters that can be set
independently. (The sign of the magnetic
term is opposite to that of the pressure term because magnetising a sample
against a field costs energy, whereas expanding it against a pressure releases
energy; Appendix~\ref{app:bookkeeping} discusses the conventions.) Everything
below is written for the pair $(-P,V)$ and goes through with it replaced by any
other conjugate pair.

The microscopic reading of this definition is useful later. Let the Hamiltonian
$\hat H(\lambda)$ of the system depend on the controlled parameters $\lambda$
(volume, field, strain). Moving $\lambda$ changes the energy of the system at
the rate $\sum_j\langle\partial\hat H/\partial\lambda_j\rangle\dot\lambda_j$,
and this is the work:
\[
  \dbar W = \sum_j\Big\langle\frac{\partial\hat H}{\partial\lambda_j}\Big\rangle d\lambda_j ,
  \qquad
  \Big\langle\frac{\partial\hat H}{\partial V}\Big\rangle = -P .
\]
Work is thus the energy that enters through the \emph{known} part of the
Hamiltonian, the part the experimenter writes down and moves.
Section~\ref{sec:foundations} returns to this.

\subsection{Heat and the first law}
\label{sec:firstlaw}

Joule's experiments established that the work needed to carry a thermally
insulated\footnote{\label{fn:adiabatic}%
\textbf{Adiabatic.} A wall is \emph{adiabatic} if it is impermeable to
matter and lets no heat through, so that the energy of the enclosed system
changes only by work (mechanical, electrical, stirring); a wall that lets heat
pass is \emph{diathermal}. Impermeability is part of the definition and not an
afterthought: particles that cross a wall carry energy with them, and of that
energy only the part $\mu\,dN$ is work, the remainder being heat
(Section~\ref{sec:clausius}), so a wall open to matter cannot be adiabatic. A
process is adiabatic if the system is enclosed by adiabatic walls throughout,
$\dbar Q=0$ at every stage. The word says nothing about the rate: an adiabatic
process may be violent (free expansion, stirring), and then the entropy
increases; only a \emph{reversible} adiabatic process keeps the entropy fixed.
The quantum-mechanical usage is different: a parameter of $\hat H$ is changed
\emph{adiabatically} if it is changed slowly enough for the system to follow
the instantaneous eigenstates (the adiabatic theorem).} system from one
equilibrium state to another is the same whatever the path. This permits a
function of state $U$, the \emph{internal energy}, to be defined up to an
additive constant by $U(B)-U(A)=W_{\rm adiabatic}(A\to B)$. When the walls are
not adiabatic the work done and the change of $U$ differ, and the difference is
called \emph{heat}:
\[
\boxed{\;dU = \dbar Q + \dbar W\;}
\]
This is the \emph{first law}. Heat is the energy transfer that is not
accounted for by the controlled parameters; it enters through the part of the
Hamiltonian that couples the system to its surroundings and that nobody
controls in detail, the collisions of gas molecules with the walls, the
exchange of photons and phonons with the environment. Without surroundings
there is no heat: an isolated system changes its energy only by work.

Neither $\dbar Q$ nor $\dbar W$ is the change of a function of state. For a
cyclic process $\oint dU=0$, whereas $\oint\dbar Q=-\oint\dbar W\neq0$ in
general; a heat engine is a device exploiting exactly this. Appendix~\ref{app:exact}
gives the differential criterion that distinguishes the two cases and the
notation $\dbar$ records the distinction. Only the sum $\dbar Q+\dbar W$ is a
differential, and only after the process is specified do the two parts acquire
separate meanings.

\subsection{Heat capacities}
\label{sec:heatcap}

The heat capacity along a given path is the ratio of the heat absorbed to the
temperature rise, $C=\dbar Q/dT$, and the subscript names what is held fixed.
At fixed volume and particle number no work is done, so the first law between
the initial and final equilibrium states reads $\Delta U=Q$ \emph{whatever the
process in between}: heat the sample from equilibrium at $T$ to equilibrium at
$T+\Delta T$ by any means, however irreversible, measure $Q$, and
\[
  C_V \equiv \lim_{\Delta T\to0}\frac{Q}{\Delta T}
  = \left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial T}\right)_{V,N} .
\]
The limit is a derivative of the equilibrium function $U(T,V,N)$, which exists
independently of how the heat was delivered. That is why constant-volume
calorimetry is well defined without any assumption of reversibility.

\section{Mathematical preliminaries}
\label{sec:math}

Thermodynamics uses a small amount of mathematics repeatedly: convexity,
the Legendre transform, homogeneous functions and constrained extrema. This
section collects these tools in the form in which they are used later, so that
the physical arguments can refer to them without interruption. The reader who
knows them may skip to Section~\ref{sec:potentials} and return as needed.

\subsection{Convex and concave functions}
\label{sec:convex}

A function $f$ defined on an interval (or on a convex set) is
\emph{convex}\footnote{\label{fn:convex}\textbf{Convexity.} The chord form,
the tangent form and the derivative forms below are equivalent for a $C^{1}$
function, and the chord form alone defines convexity when $f$ is not
differentiable. A function that is convex and concave at once is
\emph{affine}: a linear function plus a constant, $f(x)=ax+b$, whose graph
is a straight line and whose second derivative vanishes.} if the chord joining
any two points of its graph lies on or above the graph,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:chord}
  f\big(xa+(1-x)b\big)\;\leq\;x f(a)+(1-x)f(b)
  \qquad\text{for all } a,b \text{ and all } x\in[0,1] ,
\end{equation}
and \emph{strictly convex} if the inequality is strict whenever $a\neq b$ and
$0<x<1$, which excludes any straight segment in the graph. It is
\emph{concave} (strictly concave) if $-f$ is convex (strictly convex): the
chord lies on or below the graph. Figure~\ref{fig:convex}a shows the two
cases.

Three equivalent forms are used below.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Tangent form.} A $C^{1}$ function is convex if and only if its
graph lies on or above each of its tangent lines,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tangent}
  f(b)\;\geq\;f(a)+f'(a)\,(b-a) \qquad\text{for all } a,b ,
\end{equation}
and strictly convex if and only if the inequality is strict for $b\neq a$.
(From Eq.~\eqref{eq:chord} with the roles $a\leftrightarrow b$ exchanged and
$x\to0^{+}$: $[f(a+x(b-a))-f(a)]/x\leq f(b)-f(a)$, and the left side tends to
$f'(a)(b-a)$.)
\item \emph{First-derivative form.} A $C^{1}$ function is convex if and only if
$f'$ is non-decreasing, and strictly convex if and only if $f'$ is strictly
increasing. (Add the tangent inequalities at $a$ and at $b$.)
\item \emph{Second-derivative form.} A $C^{2}$ function is convex if and only
if $f''\geq0$ everywhere. (Both directions reduce to the first-derivative
form. If $f''\geq0$, the mean value theorem $f'(b)-f'(a)=f''(c)\,(b-a)$ with
$c$ between $a$ and $b$ shows that $f'$ is non-decreasing; conversely, if $f'$
is non-decreasing then $f''(a)=\lim_{h\to0^{+}}[f'(a+h)-f'(a)]/h$ is a limit
of non-negative numbers.) The condition $f''>0$ is sufficient for strict
convexity, since it makes $f'$ strictly increasing, but not necessary: $f'$
may be strictly increasing while $f''$ vanishes at isolated points, as for
$x^{4}$, which is strictly convex with $f''(0)=0$.
\end{itemize}
For concave functions every inequality is reversed: the graph lies below its
tangents, $f'$ is non-increasing, $f''\leq0$.

Two consequences are used constantly. First, a strictly convex $C^{1}$
function has a strictly increasing derivative, so the map $a\mapsto f'(a)$ is
one-to-one and can be inverted: \emph{the slope labels the point}. This is what
makes the Legendre transform possible. Second, a convex function of several
variables is convex along every straight line in its domain, because the chord
inequality only ever involves two points and the segment between them. For a
$C^{2}$ function $f(\mathbf{x})$ the second derivative along the line
$\mathbf{x}_0+t\,\mathbf{v}$ is
\[
  \frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\,f(\mathbf{x}_0+t\,\mathbf{v})
  =\sum_{ij}v_i\,\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x_i\,\partial x_j}\,v_j
  \equiv\mathbf{v}^{\mathsf T}\mathsf{H}\,\mathbf{v} ,
\]
with $\mathsf{H}$ the symmetric matrix of second derivatives (the Hessian).
Convexity along every line is therefore the statement that $\mathsf{H}$ is
\emph{positive semidefinite}: $\mathbf{v}^{\mathsf T}\mathsf{H}\mathbf{v}\geq0$
for every vector $\mathbf{v}$, equivalently every eigenvalue of $\mathsf{H}$
is non-negative. $\mathsf{H}$ is \emph{positive definite} if the inequality
is strict for every $\mathbf{v}\neq\mathbf{0}$, equivalently every eigenvalue
is positive. For a $2\times2$ matrix, positive definiteness is
$\mathsf{H}_{11}>0$ together with $\det\mathsf{H}>0$, and positive
semidefiniteness is $\mathsf{H}_{11}\geq0$, $\mathsf{H}_{22}\geq0$ and
$\det\mathsf{H}\geq0$; Section~\ref{sec:stability} uses this case.

\subsection{The Legendre transform}
\label{sec:legendre}

\paragraph{Definition.} Let $U(S)$ be strictly convex and $C^{1}$ on an
interval, and let $T\equiv U'(S)$ be its slope. Because $U'$ is strictly
increasing, each value of $T$ in the range of $U'$ is attained at exactly one
point $S(T)$, the solution of $U'(S)=T$. The \emph{Legendre transform} of $U$
is the function of the slope
\begin{equation}\label{eq:legendre_def}
  \boxed{\;F(T)\;\equiv\;U\big(S(T)\big)-T\,S(T)\;}
\end{equation}
The letters anticipate the thermodynamic use, $U$ the internal energy, $S$ the
entropy, $T$ the temperature and $F$ the Helmholtz free energy, but the
construction is purely mathematical.

\paragraph{The derivative of the transform.} Differentiate
Eq.~\eqref{eq:legendre_def} with respect to $T$, using the chain rule on
$S(T)$:
\[
  \frac{dF}{dT}
  = U'\big(S(T)\big)\,S'(T) - S(T) - T\,S'(T)
  = \big[U'(S(T))-T\big]\,S'(T) - S(T)
  = -S(T) ,
\]
because the bracket vanishes by the definition of $S(T)$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:legendre_deriv}
  \boxed{\;\frac{dF}{dT}=-S ,\qquad \frac{dU}{dS}=T\;}
\end{equation}
The two relations are symmetric: $S$ is recovered from $F$ by a
differentiation, just as $T$ is obtained from $U$. The transform is therefore
invertible by the same operation, $U(S)=F(T(S))+T(S)\,S$ with $T(S)$ the
solution of $F'(T)=-S$, and \emph{no information is lost}.

\paragraph{Geometric content: the envelope of tangents.} A convex curve can be
described in two equivalent ways, as the set of its points $(S,U(S))$ or as the
envelope of its tangent lines. The tangent at $S_0$, of slope $T=U'(S_0)$, is
the straight line
\[
  y=U(S_0)+T\,(S-S_0)=T\,S+\big[U(S_0)-T S_0\big] ,
\]
and the bracket is its intercept with the $y$ axis (Figure~\ref{fig:convex}b).
Labelling the tangent by its slope rather than by its point of contact, the
intercept is $F(T)$: \emph{the Legendre transform is the intercept of the
tangent of slope $T$}. Specifying all (slope, intercept) pairs specifies the
envelope and hence the curve, which is why nothing is lost.

\paragraph{What naive substitution loses.} The transform is not the same as
expressing $U$ in terms of $T$. Inverting $T=U'(S)$ to $S(T)$ and substituting,
$\tilde U(T)\equiv U(S(T))$, keeps only the slopes and discards the intercepts.
That the loss is real is seen by trying to go back: from $F$ one recovers $U$
by differentiation, $S=-F'(T)$ and $U=F+TS$; from $\tilde U$ one must solve the
linear differential equation $F-TF'=\tilde U$, whose general solution is a
particular solution plus $CT$ with $C$ an arbitrary constant. In thermodynamics
$C$ becomes an arbitrary function of the remaining variables and the ambiguity
is physical: the classical ideal gas has $\tilde U(T,V,N)=\tfrac32Nk_BT$, which
does not depend on $V$ and cannot yield the equation of state, whereas
$F(T,V,N)=-Nk_BT[\ln(V/N\lambda_T^{3})+1]$ gives $P=-\partial F/\partial V=Nk_BT/V$
at once.

\paragraph{The variational form.} For fixed $T$ consider the function of $S$
\[
  g_T(S)\equiv U(S)-T\,S .
\]
It is strictly convex, since $U$ is and $-TS$ is affine, and its derivative
$U'(S)-T$ vanishes exactly at $S(T)$; a stationary point of a strictly convex
function is its unique global minimum. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:legendre_inf}
  \boxed{\;F(T)=\min_{S}\big[\,U(S)-T\,S\,\big]\;}
\end{equation}
In the minimisation $T$ is a fixed parameter and $S$ the variable scanned; the
result depends on $T$ alone. This form says the same thing as the definition
but has three advantages. It is defined for any $U$, differentiable or not,
convex or not, provided the minimum (or the greatest lower bound, `infimum') exists;
it displays $S$ as a quantity that is \emph{chosen} so as to minimise $U-TS$ at
given $T$, which is exactly what a system in contact with a heat bath does
(Section~\ref{sec:minimum}); and it makes two properties of $F$ immediate,
which are derived next.

\paragraph{Convexity flips, and curvatures are reciprocal.} The first
property is qualitative: $F$ is concave in $T$. Read Eq.~\eqref{eq:legendre_inf}
with the roles of the two variables exchanged. For each fixed value of $S$ the
bracket
\[
  \ell_S(T)\equiv U(S)-T\,S
\]
is a \emph{straight line} in $T$, with slope $-S$ and intercept $U(S)$; the
transform $F(T)=\min_S\ell_S(T)$ is the lowest of these lines at each $T$,
their lower envelope. A lower envelope of straight lines is concave, and the
proof is three lines. Take $T=x\,T_1+(1-x)\,T_2$ with $0\leq x\leq1$, and let
$S^{\ast}=S(T)$ be the value of $S$ that attains the minimum at this $T$. Then
\[
  F(T)=\ell_{S^{\ast}}(T)
  =x\,\ell_{S^{\ast}}(T_1)+(1-x)\,\ell_{S^{\ast}}(T_2)
  \;\geq\;x\,F(T_1)+(1-x)\,F(T_2) ,
\]
the equality in the middle because $\ell_{S^{\ast}}$ is affine, the inequality
because $F(T_i)$ is the \emph{minimum} over $S$ of $\ell_S(T_i)$ and
$S^{\ast}$ is just one competitor. This is the chord inequality for a concave
function. Note what was and was not used: the argument never invoked the
convexity of $U$, so the variational transform of \emph{any} function is
concave. Convexity of $U$ is needed for something else, namely that the
transform can be undone and $U$ recovered (next paragraph). So
\[
  U \text{ convex in } S \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
  F \text{ concave in } T,\ \text{and } U \text{ is recovered from } F .
\]
The second property is quantitative and says how strongly $F$ curves. Start
from $F'(T)=-S(T)$, Eq.~\eqref{eq:legendre_deriv}, and differentiate once
more: $F''(T)=-dS/dT$. The function $S(T)$ is the inverse of $T(S)=U'(S)$,
and the derivative of an inverse function is the reciprocal of the derivative,
$dS/dT=1/(dT/dS)=1/U''(S)$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reciprocal}
  \frac{d^{2}F}{dT^{2}}=-\frac{dS}{dT}=-\left(\frac{dT}{dS}\right)^{-1}
  =-\left(\frac{d^{2}U}{dS^{2}}\right)^{-1} .
\end{equation}
The sign reproduces the qualitative statement, $U''>0$ giving $F''<0$, and
the magnitude adds to it. The picture is this: where $U(S)$ is nearly flat,
the slope $T$ changes very little over a wide range of $S$, so the inverse
function $S(T)$ swings through that wide range for a tiny change of $T$, and
$F$, whose slope is $-S$, bends sharply.
The curvatures\footnote{\label{fn:curvature}\textbf{Curvature.} In this text
the curvature of a function of one variable means its second derivative
$f''$, which fixes the sign (convex for $f''>0$, concave for $f''<0$) and the
size of the bending of the graph. It is not the geometric curvature
$f''/(1+f'^{2})^{3/2}$ of the curve, with which it shares the sign.} are not
merely of opposite sign but reciprocal: a function that is nearly flat in $S$
has a transform that is strongly curved in $T$, and conversely.
Section~\ref{sec:stability} reads this as a statement about fluctuations.

\paragraph{The double transform and the convex hull.} Applying the
variational form twice returns $U$ when $U$ is convex. When it is not,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hull}
  \sup_T\big[F(T)+TS\big]=\operatorname{conv}U(S) ,
\end{equation}
the \emph{convex hull}\footnote{\label{fn:hull}\textbf{Convex hull of a
function.} The largest convex function lying nowhere above $U$; equivalently,
the pointwise supremum of all affine functions lying below $U$. Pictorially,
stretch a string tight underneath the graph: where $U$ is convex the string
follows the graph, and across any non-convex stretch it is replaced by the
straight segment touching the graph at two points with a common tangent, the
\emph{double tangent}. The hull destroys everything above the segment: the
barrier between two wells, and with it every metastable or unstable state.}
of $U$ (Figure~\ref{fig:convex}c). The reason is that a transform built from
tangent lines can only see the part of the graph that is touched by a
supporting line from below, and that is precisely the hull. In thermodynamics
this is the Maxwell construction (Section~\ref{sec:transitions} and
Appendix~\ref{app:maxwell}): transforming a non-convex computed potential to its
conjugate variable and back replaces the non-convex stretch by a straight
segment, i.e.\ by a two-phase mixture.

\paragraph{Several variables.} Everything above applies to a function of
several variables transformed in one of them, the others being held fixed as
parameters. Successive transforms in different variables commute, and each
replaces one extensive variable by its conjugate slope. The mixed convexity
of the result is the point that needs care. Let $U(S,V)$ be jointly convex and
transform it in $S$ only,
\[
  F(T,V)=\min_S\big[\,U(S,V)-T\,S\,\big] .
\]
Then $F$ is \emph{concave in the transformed variable} $T$ at fixed $V$, by
the envelope argument above applied at each $V$, and \emph{convex in the
untransformed variable} $V$ at fixed $T$. The latter is the statement that
minimising a jointly convex function over one of its variables leaves a convex
function of the others. To see it, fix $T$, take $V=x\,V_1+(1-x)\,V_2$, and
let $S_1$, $S_2$ be the minimisers at $V_1$, $V_2$. The point
$(x\,S_1+(1-x)\,S_2,\,V)$ is a competitor in the minimisation at $V$, so
\[
  F(T,V)\;\leq\;U\big(xS_1+(1-x)S_2,\,V\big)-T\,\big(xS_1+(1-x)S_2\big)
  \;\leq\;x\,F(T,V_1)+(1-x)\,F(T,V_2) ,
\]
the second step by the joint convexity of $U$ and the linearity of $-TS$.
The rule, for a function convex in all its variables, is therefore:
\emph{the transform is convex in the variables that were not transformed and
concave in those that were}. In particular $F(T,V)$ is neither convex nor
concave jointly in $(T,V)$: its graph is a saddle, and its full Hessian is
indefinite. This is why the stability conditions of
Section~\ref{sec:stability} are stated one variable at a time for $F$, $G$
and $\Omega$, whereas for $U(S,V,N)$ they are a single matrix statement.

\subsection{Homogeneous functions and Euler's theorem}
\label{sec:homog}

A function is \emph{homogeneous of degree $k$} if scaling all its arguments by
$\lambda>0$ scales its value by $\lambda^{k}$,
\[
  f(\lambda x_1,\dots,\lambda x_n)=\lambda^{k}f(x_1,\dots,x_n) .
\]
Differentiating with respect to $\lambda$ and setting $\lambda=1$ gives
\emph{Euler's theorem},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:eulerthm}
  \boxed{\;\sum_i x_i\,\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_i}=k\,f\;}
\end{equation}
Two cases matter. An extensive quantity expressed through extensive variables
is homogeneous of degree one: doubling the sample doubles it. An intensive
quantity expressed through extensive variables is homogeneous of degree zero:
doubling the sample leaves it unchanged. Differentiating a degree-one function
with respect to one of its arguments gives a degree-zero function, which is why
the derivatives of $U(S,V,N)$ are intensive. Differentiating
Eq.~\eqref{eq:eulerthm} once more shows that the Hessian of a degree-one
function annihilates the vector $(x_1,\dots,x_n)$: it is singular. This is the
origin of the Gibbs--Duhem relation (Section~\ref{sec:euler}).

\subsection{Constrained extrema and Lagrange multipliers}
\label{sec:lagrange}

Thermodynamics is full of extremum problems with side conditions: maximise an
entropy at fixed total energy, minimise a free energy at fixed particle number.
To find the extremum of $f(x_1,\dots,x_n)$ subject to $g(x_1,\dots,x_n)=0$ one
forms
\[
  \mathcal{L}=f-\lambda\,g
\]
and demands $\partial\mathcal{L}/\partial x_i=0$ for every $i$, together with
$g=0$; the number $\lambda$ is the \emph{Lagrange multiplier}. The
justification is that at a constrained extremum the gradient of $f$ must have
no component along the constraint surface, so it must be parallel to the
gradient of $g$, $\nabla f=\lambda\nabla g$. Several constraints bring one
multiplier each.

The multiplier is not an auxiliary device to be forgotten: it is the rate at
which the extremum changes when the constraint is relaxed,
$\lambda=\partial f_{\rm ext}/\partial c$ if the constraint is written $g=c$.
In thermodynamics the multipliers are the intensive variables. The simplest
example fixes the pattern used in Section~\ref{sec:ensembles}. Maximise the
function $-\sum_ip_i\ln p_i$ of the probabilities $p_i$ subject to
$\sum_ip_i=1$ and $\sum_ip_iE_i=U$. With multipliers $\alpha$ and $\beta$,
\[
  \frac{\partial}{\partial p_i}\Big[-\sum_jp_j\ln p_j-\alpha\sum_jp_j-\beta\sum_jp_jE_j\Big]
  =-\ln p_i-1-\alpha-\beta E_i=0
  \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
  p_i\propto e^{-\beta E_i} ,
\]
and $\beta$ is fixed afterwards by the constraint $\sum_ip_iE_i=U$. The
multiplier of the energy constraint will turn out to be $1/k_BT$: the
temperature is the price, in entropy, of one unit of energy.

A stationary point is not yet an extremum, and an extremum is not yet a
maximum. The first-order conditions locate stationary points; which of them is
the maximum is decided by the second-order condition, the sign of the second
derivative along the allowed directions, or by comparing values. This
distinction recurs in Section~\ref{sec:stability} as the difference between
equilibrium and stability, and in Section~\ref{sec:transitions} as the
difference between a stable and a metastable phase.

\section{The fundamental relation and the thermodynamic potentials}
\label{sec:potentials}

\subsection{The fundamental relation}
\label{sec:totaldiff}

The second law, introduced in Section~\ref{sec:second}, supplies a further
function of state, the entropy $S$, which is extensive and which, for an
isolated system, is a strictly increasing function of the energy. The state
postulate of Section~\ref{sec:states} then allows the state to be labelled by
$(S,V,N)$ instead of $(U,V,N)$, and all equilibrium information about a simple
one-component system is contained in one function of extensive variables,
\[
  U=U(S,V,N) ,
\]
the \emph{fundamental relation}. Its first derivatives define the intensive
variables,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:defTPmu}
  T\equiv\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial S}\right)_{V,N},\qquad
  -P\equiv\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial V}\right)_{S,N},\qquad
  \mu\equiv\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial N}\right)_{S,V},
\end{equation}
so that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dU}
  \boxed{\;dU=T\,dS-P\,dV+\mu\,dN\;}
\end{equation}
The three terms are the thermal, mechanical and chemical channels of energy
exchange; comparison with the first law identifies $T\,dS$ with the reversible
heat and $-P\,dV+\mu\,dN$ with the reversible work (Section~\ref{sec:clausius}
makes this identification precise). Each additional work mode of
Section~\ref{sec:work} adds one natural variable and one term, $U=U(S,V,N,M,\dots)$
with $+\mathcal{H}\,dM$, and so on. That $T$ defined in this way is the
temperature measured by a thermometer, and $P$ the pressure measured by a
gauge, follows from the equilibrium conditions of Section~\ref{sec:secondlaw}.

The relations $T=T(S,V,N)$, $P=P(S,V,N)$, $\mu=\mu(S,V,N)$ obtained by
differentiation are the \emph{equations of state}. Each is a function on the
set of equilibrium states, not a restriction of it: every triple $(S,V,N)$ in
the domain is a state of the substance, and the equations of state say what
$T$, $P$ and $\mu$ are in that state. Any one equation of state is less than
the fundamental relation, since it is a single derivative of it; all three
together, or one potential in its natural variables, carry the full
information.

Three assumptions underlie Eq.~\eqref{eq:dU}, and each is physical.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{$U$ is single valued.} This is Joule's result of
Section~\ref{sec:firstlaw}, and it fails around a hysteresis loop.
\item \emph{$(S,V,N)$ are independent.} They can be prescribed by independent
operations, so the coefficients in Eq.~\eqref{eq:dU} are unique. The
intensive triple $(T,P,\mu)$ does \emph{not} have this property: Gibbs--Duhem
(Section~\ref{sec:euler}) is a relation among them, so a symbol such as
$(\partial\,\cdot/\partial T)_{P,\mu}$ has no meaning.
\item \emph{$U$ is continuously differentiable.} The partial derivatives
$T,-P,\mu$ must exist and be continuous for the differential to exist. This is
a condition on the equation of state, and Section~\ref{sec:transitions} shows
exactly where it holds and where it fails: at a first-order transition $U$ is
$C^{1}$ but not $C^{2}$, so Eq.~\eqref{eq:dU} survives while the second
derivatives (the response functions) do not.\footnote{\label{fn:Cn}%
\textbf{Differentiability classes.} $f$ is of class $C^{n}$ if all its partial
derivatives up to order $n$ exist and are continuous; $C^{0}$ is continuity
alone. The classes are nested: $|x|$ is $C^{0}$ but not $C^{1}$, and $x|x|$ is
$C^{1}$ but not $C^{2}$. Two facts are used: $f\in C^{1}$ guarantees that the
differential $df$ exists, and $f\in C^{2}$ guarantees that mixed second
derivatives are equal, which is what makes the Maxwell relations available
(Appendix~\ref{app:exact}).}
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Extensivity: Euler and Gibbs--Duhem}
\label{sec:euler}

$U$, $S$, $V$ and $N$ are extensive, so $U(\lambda S,\lambda V,\lambda N)=\lambda\,U(S,V,N)$
for $\lambda>0$: $U$ is homogeneous of degree one in its natural variables.
Euler's theorem, Eq.~\eqref{eq:eulerthm}, with the derivatives
Eq.~\eqref{eq:defTPmu}, gives the \emph{Euler relation}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:euler}
  \boxed{\;U=TS-PV+\mu N\;}
\end{equation}
Taking its differential and subtracting Eq.~\eqref{eq:dU},
\[
  (S\,dT+T\,dS)-(P\,dV+V\,dP)+(\mu\,dN+N\,d\mu)=T\,dS-P\,dV+\mu\,dN ,
\]
whence the \emph{Gibbs--Duhem relation}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gibbsduhem}
  \boxed{\;S\,dT-V\,dP+N\,d\mu=0\;}
\end{equation}
Dividing by $N$, with $s=S/N$ and $v=V/N$, it reads $d\mu=-s\,dT+v\,dP$: the
chemical potential of a one-component system is a function of $T$ and $P$
alone, and the three intensive variables are not independent. Fixing two of
them fixes the third. This is the failure of independence noted in
Section~\ref{sec:totaldiff}, and it is why there is no useful potential of
$(T,P,\mu)$: a quantity built from $U$, $S$, $V$, $N$ is extensive, but a
function of $(T,P,\mu)$ alone is unchanged when the sample is doubled, so it
must vanish identically. Indeed $U-TS+PV-\mu N=0$ by Eq.~\eqref{eq:euler}.
The potentials that survive each keep at least one extensive argument to carry
the scaling.

Extensivity is exact only in the thermodynamic limit of a system with
short-range interactions, where the boundary contributes a fraction
$\sim N^{-1/d}$ of the particles (Appendix~\ref{app:extensivity}). Euler and
Gibbs--Duhem hold at and arbitrarily close to a critical point, where the
potentials are extensive but not analytic (Section~\ref{sec:critical}).

\subsection{The family of potentials}
\label{sec:family}

An experiment fixes intensive variables: a sample sits in a bath at
temperature $T$, not at prescribed entropy, and under atmospheric pressure, not
at prescribed volume. The Legendre transform of Section~\ref{sec:legendre}
trades an extensive variable for its conjugate slope without loss of
information, and each choice of variables traded gives one potential. Each is
$U$ minus the product of the conjugate pair traded away, and its differential
follows from Eq.~\eqref{eq:dU} by the product rule.

\paragraph{Enthalpy, $H(S,P,N)$.}
\[
  \boxed{\;H=U+PV ,\qquad dH=T\,dS+V\,dP+\mu\,dN\;}
\]
At constant $P$ and $N$, $dH=T\,dS=\dbar Q_{\rm rev}$: the enthalpy is the
bookkeeping variable for calorimetry at fixed pressure, and
$C_P=(\partial H/\partial T)_{P,N}$.

\paragraph{Helmholtz free energy, $F(T,V,N)$.}
\[
  \boxed{\;F=U-TS ,\qquad dF=-S\,dT-P\,dV+\mu\,dN\;}
\]
with $S=-(\partial F/\partial T)_{V,N}$, $P=-(\partial F/\partial V)_{T,N}$ and
$\mu=(\partial F/\partial N)_{T,V}$. This is the potential of a closed system
in a heat bath, and the one computed by the canonical ensemble.

\paragraph{Gibbs free energy, $G(T,P,N)$.}
\[
  \boxed{\;G=U-TS+PV=F+PV ,\qquad dG=-S\,dT+V\,dP+\mu\,dN\;}
\]
with $S=-(\partial G/\partial T)_{P,N}$ and $V=(\partial G/\partial P)_{T,N}$.
By the Euler relation $G=\mu N$: the chemical potential is the Gibbs free
energy per particle, and for a one-component system $G$ is $N$ times a
function of $(T,P)$.

\paragraph{Grand potential, $\Omega(T,V,\mu)$.}
\[
  \boxed{\;\Omega=U-TS-\mu N=F-\mu N ,\qquad d\Omega=-S\,dT-P\,dV-N\,d\mu\;}
\]
with $S=-(\partial\Omega/\partial T)_{V,\mu}$, $P=-(\partial\Omega/\partial V)_{T,\mu}$
and $N=-(\partial\Omega/\partial\mu)_{T,V}$. By the Euler relation
$\Omega=-PV$: the grand potential is $V$ times a function of $(T,\mu)$, and
$P(T,\mu)$ is Gibbs--Duhem once more. This is the potential of an open system
and the one computed by the grand canonical ensemble; it is the natural
potential for quantum gases, because only at fixed $\mu$ does the many-body
problem of independent particles factorise orbital by orbital
(Appendix~\ref{app:quadratic}).

\paragraph{Homogeneity of the potentials.} Each product $TS$, $PV$, $\mu N$
scales like its extensive factor, so every potential is homogeneous of degree
one in its extensive arguments at fixed intensive ones,
\[
  F(T,\lambda V,\lambda N)=\lambda F(T,V,N),\qquad
  G(T,P,\lambda N)=\lambda G(T,P,N),
\]
\[
  \Omega(T,\lambda V,\mu)=\lambda\Omega(T,V,\mu) .
\]
A potential with a single extensive argument is that argument times a function
of the intensive ones, which is the content of $G=\mu(T,P)N$ and
$\Omega=-P(T,\mu)V$.

\paragraph{Maxwell relations.} Each potential is $C^{2}$ away from phase
transitions, so its mixed second derivatives are equal. From $dF$ and $dG$,
\[
  \left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial V}\right)_{T,N}
  =\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial T}\right)_{V,N},
  \qquad
  \left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial P}\right)_{T,N}
  =-\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}\right)_{P,N},
\]
and similarly from $dU$, $dH$ and $d\Omega$. These convert an unmeasurable
entropy derivative into a measurable equation-of-state derivative, and they
are used in Section~\ref{sec:stability} and Appendix~\ref{app:cmu}.

\paragraph{Which potential to use.} The natural potential is the one whose
natural variables are those the environment holds fixed: $U$ for an isolated
system at fixed $(S,V,N)$, $F$ for a closed system in a heat bath, $G$ for a
closed system in a heat bath under a fixed pressure, $\Omega$ for a system
exchanging energy and particles with a reservoir. As \emph{descriptions of the
substance} all potentials are equivalent: each is obtained from
$U(S,V,N)$ by Legendre transformation and $U$ is recovered from each by
differentiation. As \emph{labels of a state} they are not equivalent, and the
difference appears at a first-order transition, where the intensive variables
are constant across a whole family of states and the potentials with intensive
arguments cannot tell those states apart (Section~\ref{sec:firsttransition}).

\section{The second law and its consequences}
\label{sec:second}

\subsection{Statement}
\label{sec:secondlaw}

The first law conserves energy and says nothing about direction: it allows heat
to flow from a cold body to a hot one and a gas to collect spontaneously in
one half of its vessel. The second law supplies the direction. In the form
most useful for what follows (Callen's postulates, as presented by Swendsen
\cite{Swendsen}) it reads:
\begin{quote}
There exists an extensive function of state, the \emph{entropy} $S(U,V,N)$,
which is a continuous and strictly increasing function of $U$, with the
following property.
When an internal \emph{macroscopic} constraint of an isolated system is
released, the system evolves to the equilibrium state in which $S$ is as large
as it can be, given the constraints that remain. Consequently, for any process
that carries a thermally isolated system from one equilibrium state to
another,
\[
  \boxed{\;\Delta S\;\geq\;0 ,\;}
\]
with equality if and only if the process is reversible.
\end{quote}
The word \emph{macroscopic} in the statement is not decorative. The
constraint is a wall, a valve, a clamp, a switch: something described by a few
variables and operated without any knowledge of the microstate. A constraint
that acts on individual molecules is not of this kind. Maxwell's demon, a
trap door opened for fast molecules travelling one way and for slow ones
travelling the other, sorts a gas at uniform temperature into a hot half and a
cold half, and the entropy of the gas decreases. There is no contradiction,
because the demon is not a macroscopic constraint: to operate the door it must
measure and record the state of each molecule, and the record is a physical
system whose entropy grows by at least $k_B\ln2$ per bit when it is erased to
make room for the next (Landauer's bound), so that the entropy of gas plus
demon does not decrease. The second law bounds what can be achieved with
macroscopic manipulations; what a microscopic agent can do is accounted for
only when the agent is included in the system (Section~\ref{sec:found_info}).
Clausius' statement (heat does not flow spontaneously from cold to hot) and
Kelvin's (no cyclic process converts heat entirely into work) are consequences
and are derived below and in Section~\ref{sec:clausius}; the three are
equivalent (see Schwabl \cite{Schwabl}). The statement is deliberately written
with $\Delta S$ rather than $dS$: the entropy is defined for equilibrium
states, and during a violent process (a gas rushing into a vacuum) there is no
equilibrium state and no entropy to differentiate. All that is needed is that
$S$ exist at the two end points.

\paragraph{Reversibility and the creation of entropy.} The condition for
equality, \emph{reversible}, is defined operationally in
Section~\ref{sec:clausius}: quasi-static and without dissipation, so that the
reversed protocol restores system and surroundings. For a thermally isolated
system the postulate makes the two notions coincide. A reversible process has
$\Delta S=0$, because the reversed process is also allowed and would have
$-\Delta S\geq0$. Conversely, a process with $\Delta S=0$ is reversible,
because any irreversibility consists of the relaxation of some internal
variable from a value held by a released constraint to its equilibrium value,
and the postulate says that such a relaxation strictly increases $S$. For a
system that is not thermally isolated the two notions must be kept apart,
because $S$ then changes for two reasons. Entropy can be \emph{transferred}
across the boundary with the heat, and it can be \emph{created} inside; the
balance along any process is
\[
  dS=\frac{\dbar Q}{T_{\rm res}}+\dbar S_{\rm created} ,\qquad
  \dbar S_{\rm created}\geq0 ,
\]
with $T_{\rm res}$ the temperature of the reservoir supplying the heat
(Section~\ref{sec:clausius}, where this follows from the Clausius inequality).
Reversibility is equivalent to the vanishing of the \emph{created} part, not
of $\Delta S$: a reversible isothermal expansion has $\Delta S=Q/T>0$ and
creates nothing, the loss of entropy of the reservoir, $-Q/T$, compensating
exactly. The isolated system is the case $\dbar Q=0$, where transfer is
absent and the two statements become one.
\paragraph{The entropy representation.} Since $S$ is strictly increasing in
$U$, the relation $S=S(U,V,N)$ can be inverted to $U=U(S,V,N)$, the
fundamental relation of Section~\ref{sec:totaldiff}. The derivatives of the
two forms are related by solving Eq.~\eqref{eq:dU} for $dS$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dS}
  dS=\frac{1}{T}\,dU+\frac{P}{T}\,dV-\frac{\mu}{T}\,dN ,
  \qquad
  \left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial U}\right)_{V,N}=\frac{1}{T}>0 .
\end{equation}
Positivity of $T$ is the monotonicity of $S$ in $U$, and it is worth being
precise about where it comes from. It is \emph{not} a consequence of the
maximum principle: that principle, applied below to two bodies in thermal
contact, yields $1/T_1=1/T_2$ and the direction of heat flow, and it would do
so for negative temperatures as well. Monotonicity is a separate clause of the
postulate, and physically it is a property of the energy spectrum. For a
spectrum unbounded above the number of states available grows with $U$ without
limit, $S$ increases with $U$, and $T>0$ (Section~\ref{sec:microcanonical}).
For a system whose spectrum is bounded above, such as a set of nuclear spins
isolated from the lattice, $S(U)$ rises and then falls again as $U$ approaches
the top of the band, and on the falling branch $T=(\partial S/\partial U)^{-1}$
is negative. Such states are prepared in the laboratory by population
inversion; they are internally equilibrated, obey the maximum principle, and
give heat to any body at positive temperature, so that they are ``hotter than
infinite temperature''. Everything in these notes that uses $T>0$ (the
invertibility of $S(U)$ to $U(S)$, the signs in the stability inequalities)
is restricted to systems with spectra unbounded above, which includes every
system with kinetic energy.

\paragraph{Equilibrium between two bodies.} Let an isolated system consist of
two parts, $1$ and $2$, separated by a wall. The entropy is additive,
$S=S_1(U_1,V_1,N_1)+S_2(U_2,V_2,N_2)$, up to a contribution from the interface
that is negligible in the thermodynamic limit (Appendix~\ref{app:extensivity}).
If the wall conducts heat but is rigid and impermeable, the only internal
variable is the division of the fixed total energy, $U_1+U_2=U$, and
the second law says that equilibrium maximises $S$ over $U_1$:
\[
  \frac{\partial S}{\partial U_1}
  =\frac{\partial S_1}{\partial U_1}-\frac{\partial S_2}{\partial U_2}
  =\frac{1}{T_1}-\frac{1}{T_2}=0
  \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad T_1=T_2 .
\]
That $S$ has a maximum does not conflict with the monotonicity of each $S_i$
in its own energy: the total energy is fixed, so the function being maximised
is $S_1(U_1)+S_2(U-U_1)$, which increases with $U_1$ through the first term
and decreases through the second, and the maximum is where the two rates,
$1/T_1$ and $1/T_2$, balance. Monotonicity in $U$ says only that raising the
\emph{total} energy never lowers the entropy. That the stationary point is a
maximum, and the only one, follows from the concavity of each $S_i$
(Section~\ref{sec:whyconvex}).
If the wall is also movable, maximising over $V_1$ gives $P_1/T_1=P_2/T_2$,
hence $P_1=P_2$; if it is permeable, maximising over $N_1$ gives $\mu_1=\mu_2$.
The derivatives of $S$ with respect to the quantities exchanged are what is
equalised, and these are the \emph{intensive} variables: this is why $T$, $P$ and
$\mu$, defined as derivatives in Eq.~\eqref{eq:defTPmu}, are the quantities
that a thermometer, a manometer and a membrane measure. The direction of
approach follows from the same formula. Before equilibrium, a small transfer
$dU_1$ changes the entropy by $dS=(1/T_1-1/T_2)\,dU_1$, and the second law
requires $dS>0$: energy flows into the part with the larger $1/T$, i.e.\ from
the hotter to the colder body. That is Clausius' statement.

\subsection{Reversible heat and the Clausius inequality}
\label{sec:clausius}

\paragraph{Quasi-static processes.} A process is \emph{quasi-static} if it is
slow compared with all internal relaxation times, so that every intermediate
state is an equilibrium state and the path is a curve on the set of
equilibrium states. Only then is $dS$ meaningful along the path. A process is
\emph{reversible} if it is quasi-static \emph{and} dissipationless: no
friction, no finite temperature or pressure drop across a boundary, no
hysteresis. For a reversible process the time-reversed process is also
possible, so that running the protocol backwards restores both the system and
its surroundings.

\paragraph{The reversible heat.} Along a \emph{reversible} path at fixed $N$ the work
is $-P\,dV$, the pressure of the system being equal to the external pressure
at every stage. Comparing the first law $dU=\dbar Q_{\rm rev}-P\,dV$ with
Eq.~\eqref{eq:dU} at fixed $N$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:TdS}
  \boxed{\;\dbar Q_{\rm rev}=T\,dS\;}
\end{equation}
Nothing here requires $P$ to be constant along the path; it varies in an
adiabatic compression, and the argument is unaffected. What is required is
that at every stage the external pressure equal the system's own $P$, a
function of state, so that the work is expressed through state variables and
can be compared with Eq.~\eqref{eq:dU} term by term. In an irreversible
compression the external pressure exceeds $P$, the work $-P_{\rm ext}\,dV$
exceeds $-P\,dV$, and the heat falls short of $T\,dS$; this is the content of
the Clausius inequality below.
This is the hinge on which thermodynamics turns: it converts the unmeasurable
entropy into the measurable heat, and it is what makes $1/T$ an integrating
factor for $\dbar Q$ (Appendix~\ref{app:exact}). In particular the heat
capacities of Section~\ref{sec:heatcap} become entropy derivatives,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:CVCP}
  C_V=T\left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial T}\right)_{V,N}
  =-T\left(\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial T^{2}}\right)_{V,N},
  \qquad
  C_P=T\left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial T}\right)_{P,N}
  =-T\left(\frac{\partial^{2}G}{\partial T^{2}}\right)_{P,N},
\end{equation}
the second forms by $S=-\partial F/\partial T=-\partial G/\partial T$.

\paragraph{The Clausius inequality.} Let the system pass from an equilibrium
state $A$ to an equilibrium state $B$, by any process however violent, while
exchanging heat with one or more reservoirs. A reservoir is so large that the
heat it exchanges never drives it out of equilibrium, and it does no work, so
Eq.~\eqref{eq:TdS} applies to it at every instant: receiving $-\dbar Q$ when
the system receives $\dbar Q$, its entropy changes by $-\dbar Q/T_{\rm res}$.
The second law for the composite, $\Delta S+\Delta S_{\rm res}\geq0$, then
gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:clausius}
  \boxed{\;\Delta S\;\geq\;\int_A^B\frac{\dbar Q}{T_{\rm res}}\;}
\end{equation}
with the integral taken along the actual process and $T_{\rm res}$ the
temperature of the reservoir in contact with the system when $\dbar Q$ is
delivered. For a single reservoir, $\Delta S\geq Q/T_{\rm res}$; for a cycle,
$\oint\dbar Q/T_{\rm res}\leq0$, and with two reservoirs
$Q_h/T_h+Q_c/T_c\leq0$, from which Kelvin's statement and the Carnot bound
$W\leq Q_h(1-T_c/T_h)$ follow. If in addition the process is quasi-static for
the system, the differential form holds,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:clausius_diff}
  dS\;\geq\;\frac{\dbar Q}{T_{\rm res}} ,
\end{equation}
with equality if and only if the process is reversible. Note that the
system's entropy change is compared with the \emph{reservoir's} temperature.
Writing $dS=\dbar Q/T_{\rm res}+d\sigma$ defines the entropy \emph{produced},
$d\sigma\geq0$.

Two examples show where the inequality is strict although every body is in
equilibrium at every instant. Let heat $\dbar Q$ pass through a weakly
conducting wall from a reservoir at $T_{\rm res}$ to the system at $T\neq
T_{\rm res}$; then $dS=\dbar Q/T$ and $dS_{\rm res}=-\dbar Q/T_{\rm res}$, so
\[
  d\sigma=\dbar Q\Big(\frac{1}{T}-\frac{1}{T_{\rm res}}\Big)
  =\dbar Q\,\frac{T_{\rm res}-T}{T\,T_{\rm res}}>0 ,
\]
since $\dbar Q$ and $T_{\rm res}-T$ have the same sign: the entropy is produced
in the wall, across the temperature drop, and vanishes only as $T\to T_{\rm res}$.
And let a thermally isolated fluid be stirred arbitrarily slowly: $\dbar Q=0$
because the vessel is adiabatic, yet $dS>0$, since the work is degraded into
internal energy; here $T\,dS=T\,d\sigma$ entirely. Quasi-staticity is therefore
necessary for Eq.~\eqref{eq:TdS} and not sufficient. Hysteretic systems
(domain-wall depinning, martensitic transformations) dissipate a finite work
per cycle however slowly the field is swept, and are irreversible in the
quasi-static limit itself.

\subsection{Concavity of the entropy and convexity of the energy}
\label{sec:whyconvex}

Convexity of the fundamental relation is not an assumption; it follows from
the second law and extensivity in three lines. Take two arbitrary bodies of
the same substance, with extensive variables $(U_1,V_1,N_1)$ and
$(U_2,V_2,N_2)$, each in equilibrium, separated by a rigid, impermeable,
adiabatic wall. The composite is an equilibrium state with an internal
constraint, and its entropy is the sum $S(U_1,V_1,N_1)+S(U_2,V_2,N_2)$. Now
replace the wall by one that conducts heat, moves and lets particles through,
and let the composite relax. By the second law the final entropy is at least
the initial one, and the final state is the unconstrained equilibrium of a
body with the summed variables:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:superadd}
  S(U_1,V_1,N_1)+S(U_2,V_2,N_2)\;\leq\;S(U_1+U_2,\,V_1+V_2,\,N_1+N_2) .
\end{equation}
Nothing was assumed about how the final state divides the energy between the
parts; at a first-order transition it need not divide it evenly. Now choose
the two bodies as fractions $x$ and $1-x$ of two states $a$ and $b$ of the
substance, $(U_1,V_1,N_1)=x\,(U_a,V_a,N_a)$ and $(U_2,V_2,N_2)=(1-x)(U_b,V_b,N_b)$
with $0\leq x\leq1$. Extensivity, $S(\lambda U,\lambda V,\lambda N)=\lambda S(U,V,N)$,
turns Eq.~\eqref{eq:superadd} into
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Sconcave}
  x\,S(a)+(1-x)\,S(b)\;\leq\;S\big(x\,a+(1-x)\,b\big) ,
\end{equation}
the chord inequality of Section~\ref{sec:convex} with the inequality reversed:
\emph{the entropy is a concave function of $(U,V,N)$ jointly}. The
inequality is strict in a single homogeneous phase and becomes an equality
along the straight segments that describe two-phase mixtures
(Section~\ref{sec:firsttransition}).

\begin{remark}
Concave and strictly increasing in $U$ are compatible, and together they say
something definite. Concavity requires the slope $\partial S/\partial U=1/T$
to be non-increasing in $U$; strict monotonicity requires it to stay
positive. A function with a positive slope that keeps decreasing without ever
reaching zero does both: $\ln U$ and $\sqrt U$ are examples, and so is the
entropy of the classical ideal gas, $S=\tfrac32Nk_B\ln U+\dots$, whose slope
$1/T=\tfrac32Nk_B/U$ falls towards zero as $U\to\infty$ but never vanishes.
Nothing in concavity forces the function to turn over. In thermodynamic terms
the two properties combined say that $1/T$ decreases with $U$, i.e.\ that
$T$ increases with $U$, which is the positivity of $C_V$ derived in
Eq.~\eqref{eq:USconvex} below. The case in which a concave $S(U)$ does turn
over is the bounded spectrum of Section~\ref{sec:secondlaw}, where the slope
passes through zero and $T$ becomes negative; the postulate excludes it by
requiring strict monotonicity.
\end{remark}

\paragraph{From $S$ to $U$.} Since $S$ is concave and strictly increasing in
$U$, its inverse $U(S,V,N)$ is convex. In one variable: with $S'(U)=1/T>0$,
\[
  U''(S)=\frac{d}{dS}\frac{1}{S'(U)}=-\frac{S''(U)}{S'(U)^{3}}
  =-T^{3}S''(U)\;\geq\;0 ,
\]
and the same holds in every direction of $(S,V,N)$ (the argument of
Section~\ref{sec:convex} applied along a line). Evaluating the one-variable
case with $\partial^{2}S/\partial U^{2}=\partial(1/T)/\partial U=-1/T^{2}C_V$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:USconvex}
  \left(\frac{\partial^{2}S}{\partial U^{2}}\right)_{V,N}=-\frac{1}{T^{2}C_V}\leq0 ,
  \qquad
  \left(\frac{\partial^{2}U}{\partial S^{2}}\right)_{V,N}=\frac{T}{C_V}\geq0 :
\end{equation}
concavity of $S$ in $U$, convexity of $U$ in $S$ and positivity of $C_V$ are
one statement. By the rules of Section~\ref{sec:legendre}, $F$ is concave in
$T$ and convex in $V$ and $N$; $G$ is concave in $T$ and $P$ and convex in
$N$; $\Omega$ is concave in $T$ and $\mu$ and convex in $V$. Convexity of $U$
in $S$ is also what makes the map $S\mapsto T=\partial U/\partial S$ invertible
on every single-phase branch, so that $S(T,V,N)$ exists and the Legendre
transform to $F$ is well defined; invertibility fails exactly where strict
convexity fails, on the flat segments of a first-order transition.

\subsection{The minimum principles}
\label{sec:minimum}

\begin{quote}
At fixed values of its natural variables, the appropriate thermodynamic
potential is a minimum with respect to every unconstrained internal
macroscopic variable.
\end{quote}
The \emph{internal variables} are the macroscopic quantities left undetermined
by the constraints: the position of an internal piston, the amount of substance
transferred through a valve, the extent of a chemical reaction, the fraction of
a coexisting phase, an order parameter. Prepare the system in equilibrium with
such a variable pinned, release it while the walls and reservoirs keep the
natural variables fixed, and the system moves spontaneously until the potential
is as low as it can be; from there no \emph{spontaneous} change remains.

\paragraph{Derivation.} Let the system pass, by any process, from an
equilibrium state $A$ (with an internal constraint) to an equilibrium state $B$
(constraint released). Its surroundings act only through the values they
impose at the boundary: a bath at temperature $T_0$ through which heat passes,
a fixed external pressure $P_0$ against which the volume moves, a reservoir at
chemical potential $\mu_0$ from which particles are admitted. These are
constants throughout the process, which is what baths and reservoirs are for;
the system's own $T$, $P$, $\mu$ need not even exist at intermediate times.
The first law reads
\[
  \Delta U=Q-P_0\,\Delta V+\mu_0\,\Delta N ,
\]
and the Clausius inequality~\eqref{eq:clausius} with a single bath temperature
reads $\Delta S\geq Q/T_0$. Eliminating $Q$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:availability}
  \boxed{\;\Delta\big(U-T_0S+P_0V-\mu_0N\big)\;\leq\;0\;}
\end{equation}
since $T_0$, $P_0$, $\mu_0$ are constants and can be taken inside the
difference. The bracket, the \emph{availability} $\Psi$, is defined for any
equilibrium state of the system; it contains the system's own extensive
variables and the surroundings' intensive ones. When the system is in
equilibrium with its surroundings its temperature equals $T_0$, and, where the
walls permit the exchange, its pressure equals $P_0$ and its chemical potential
$\mu_0$. (If $T\neq T_0$, a heat flow in the direction from hot to cold lowers
$\Psi$, is permitted by Eq.~\eqref{eq:availability}, and would occur: the state
was not equilibrium.) Then $\Psi$ reduces to a thermodynamic potential: with
rigid closed walls $\Delta V=\Delta N=0$ and $\Delta\Psi=\Delta(U-TS)=\Delta F$;
with closed movable walls $\Delta\Psi=\Delta G$; with rigid permeable walls
$\Delta\Psi=\Delta\Omega$. For the isolated system the second law gives
$\Delta S\geq0$ directly, and at fixed $S,V,N$ no work is done and
$\dbar Q\leq T\,dS=0$ gives $dU\leq0$.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
Held fixed & Second law becomes & Equilibrium condition\\
\midrule
$U,V,N$ (isolated) & $dS \geq 0$ & $S$ \emph{maximum}\\
$S,V,N$ & $dU \leq 0$ & $U$ minimum\\
$S,P,N$ & $dH \leq 0$ & $H$ minimum\\
$T,V,N$ & $dF \leq 0$ & $F$ minimum\\
$T,P,N$ & $dG \leq 0$ & $G$ minimum\\
$T,V,\mu$ & $d\Omega \leq 0$ & $\Omega$ minimum\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The sign flip relative to the entropy maximum comes entirely from the term
$-T_0S$: at fixed bath temperature, raising the system's entropy lowers
$U-T_0S$. The potential can only decrease, so it decreases until it cannot;
equilibrium is its minimum over the internal variables, stationarity
$\partial\Phi/\partial\xi=0$ is the first-order condition and
$\partial^{2}\Phi/\partial\xi^{2}\geq0$ the second-order one.

The minimum principle is the thermodynamic reading of the variational form
Eq.~\eqref{eq:legendre_inf} of the Legendre transform: a system in a bath is
free to exchange energy and thereby to choose its entropy, and it settles at
the $S$ that minimises $U-T_0S$, the stationarity condition
$\partial U/\partial S=T_0$ saying that system and bath have reached a common
temperature.

\begin{warning}
Stationarity alone is not equilibrium; one also needs stability, i.e.\ that the
extremum be a minimum. And a local minimum is a metastable state, not the
equilibrium state: supercooled liquids, retained austenite and diamond at
ambient conditions all sit in local minima with lifetimes exceeding any
experiment. Thermodynamics identifies the global minimum; whether the system
reaches it is a question of kinetics.
\end{warning}

The statistical-mechanical counterpart of the minimum principle, in which the
potential is minimised over all density matrices and the minimum is the Gibbs
state, is the Gibbs variational principle; it is stated in
Section~\ref{sec:ensembles} and proved in Appendix~\ref{app:gibbsvar}.

\subsection{Stability and response functions}
\label{sec:stability}
\label{sec:convexity}

Equilibrium requires the potential to be stationary; \emph{stability} requires
it to be a minimum. The second-order condition is the source of every
inequality obeyed by a response function, and one construction produces them
all.

\paragraph{The construction.} Divide a homogeneous body conceptually into two
equal halves that can exchange a single extensive quantity $X$ (energy,
volume, magnetic moment) through an internal wall, the total $2X_0$ being
fixed. The split
\[
  X_1=X_0+x,\qquad X_2=X_0-x
\]
is an internal variable, so the potential $\Phi$ selected by the remaining
constraints, regarded as a function of $x$,
\[
  \Phi_{\rm tot}(x)=\Phi(X_0+x)+\Phi(X_0-x) ,
\]
must be a minimum at the equilibrium split. The homogeneous split $x=0$ is
stationary for every $\Phi$, by symmetry: $\Phi'(X_0+x)-\Phi'(X_0-x)$ vanishes
at $x=0$, which is the equality of the intensive variable conjugate to $X$
between the halves. Whether it is a minimum is decided by the second order.
Expanding, the odd powers cancel between the halves,
\[
  \Phi_{\rm tot}(x)-\Phi_{\rm tot}(0)=\Phi''(X_0)\,x^{2}+O(x^{4}) ,
\]
so $x=0$ is a minimum for small $x$ if and only if
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stab}
  \boxed{\;\Phi''(X_0)\geq0\ \text{ for a minimised potential},
  \qquad S''(X_0)\leq0\ \text{ for the entropy}\;}
\end{equation}
Imposed for all $X_0$ this is the convexity already derived in
Section~\ref{sec:whyconvex}; here it is read locally. If $\Phi''<0$ the
homogeneous state is unstable: a half that acquired slightly more $X$ would go
on acquiring it, and the body separates into two phases. The first-order
condition carries no information about stability; the entire content is second
order, and in each case the second derivative is a response function.

\paragraph{Heat capacity.} Isolated composite, rigid impermeable diathermal
wall: $X=U$, the entropy is maximised, and by Eq.~\eqref{eq:USconvex}
\[
  \left(\frac{\partial^{2}S}{\partial U^{2}}\right)_{V,N}=-\frac{1}{T^{2}C_V}\leq0
  \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
  \boxed{\;C_V\geq0\;}
\]
With $C_V<0$ the half that gave energy away would become the hotter of the two
and go on giving.

\paragraph{Isothermal compressibility.} Composite in a bath at $T$, movable
diathermal impermeable wall: $X=V$, the potential is $F$, and with
$(\partial F/\partial V)_{T,N}=-P$ and
$\kappa_T\equiv-V^{-1}(\partial V/\partial P)_{T,N}$,
\[
  \left(\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial V^{2}}\right)_{T,N}
  =-\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial V}\right)_{T,N}=\frac{1}{V\kappa_T}\geq0
  \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
  \boxed{\;\kappa_T\geq0\;}
\]
With $\kappa_T<0$ a region that contracted slightly would find its pressure
fall below that of its surroundings and be compressed further: that runaway is
the interior of the van der Waals loop, and phase separation is how the system
escapes it (Appendix~\ref{app:maxwell}).

\paragraph{Magnetic susceptibility.} Fix $T$, $V$, $N$ and the total moment
$2M_0$, and let the halves exchange moment: $X=M$, the potential is
$F(T,V,N,M)$ with $(\partial F/\partial M)_{T,V,N}=\mathcal{H}$, and
\[
  \left(\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial M^{2}}\right)_{T,V,N}
  =\left(\frac{\partial\mathcal{H}}{\partial M}\right)_{T,V,N}=\frac{1}{\chi_T}\geq0
  \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
  \boxed{\;\chi_T\equiv\left(\frac{\partial M}{\partial\mathcal{H}}\right)_{T,V,N}\geq0\;}
\]
Diamagnets are not counterexamples: the inequality concerns the response of
the variable that couples linearly to the field, $\hat H=\hat H_0-\mathcal{H}\hat M$,
whereas orbital magnetism enters through $(\hat{\mathbf p}-q\mathbf{A})^{2}$,
quadratically in the field, and contributes a negative term
$-\langle\partial^{2}\hat H/\partial\mathcal{H}^{2}\rangle$ to the measured
susceptibility. What stability of matter plus field bounds is the permeability
$1+\chi_{\rm vol}\geq0$, which a superconductor saturates rather than
violates.

\paragraph{Two quantities exchanged at once.} Letting the halves exchange $U$
and $V$ together promotes Eq.~\eqref{eq:stab} to positive semidefiniteness of
the $2\times2$ Hessian of $U(S,V)$, whose determinant condition, worked out
with the Maxwell relations, gives the cross inequalities
\begin{equation}\label{eq:CPCV}
  \boxed{\;C_P-C_V=\frac{TV\alpha_P^{2}}{\kappa_T}\geq0 ,\qquad
  \kappa_T-\kappa_S=\frac{TV\alpha_P^{2}}{C_P}\geq0\;}
\end{equation}
with $\alpha_P=V^{-1}(\partial V/\partial T)_P$ the thermal expansion
coefficient and $\kappa_S$ the adiabatic compressibility (Schwabl
\cite{Schwabl}, Plischke \cite{Plischke}). Hence $C_P\geq C_V\geq0$ and
$\kappa_T\geq\kappa_S\geq0$. The full Hessian of $U(S,V,N)$ is only
semidefinite, never definite: by Euler's theorem it annihilates the vector
$(S,V,N)$ (Section~\ref{sec:homog}), so one combination of the response
functions is fixed by extensivity alone.

\paragraph{The general pattern.} Applying the construction variable by
variable gives the whole list: $U$ is convex in each of $S,V,N$; $F$ is
concave in $T$ and convex in $V$; $G$ is concave in $T$ and $P$; $\Omega$ is
concave in $T$ and $\mu$. In every case a response function is a second
derivative of a potential, and Eq.~\eqref{eq:reciprocal} says that the
curvature of a potential in an extensive variable and the curvature of its
transform in the conjugate intensive variable are reciprocal:
\[
  \left(\frac{\partial^{2}U}{\partial S^{2}}\right)_{V,N}=\frac{T}{C_V},
  \qquad
  \left(\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial T^{2}}\right)_{V,N}=-\frac{C_V}{T} .
\]
A large heat capacity makes $U(S)$ nearly flat (adding entropy hardly raises
the temperature) and $F(T)$ strongly curved (a small change of temperature
changes the entropy a lot). Section~\ref{sec:ensembles} identifies the
curvature of the transformed potential with the variance of the fluctuating
extensive quantity, $k_BT^{2}C_V=\Var(E)$, which is the statistical reason why
the stability inequalities hold automatically: a variance is non-negative.
Equality, a vanishing curvature with a diverging response and a diverging
fluctuation, is what marks a critical point (Section~\ref{sec:critical}).

\section{Phase transitions}
\label{sec:transitions}

A phase transition is a point, line or surface in the space of intensive
variables at which a thermodynamic potential is not analytic. Everything about
the thermodynamics of a transition can be read off the geometry of the
potentials established in Section~\ref{sec:second}: the concavity of $S$, the
convexity of $U$ and the Legendre duality between extensive and intensive
coordinates. This section does so, first for discontinuous (first-order)
transitions and then for continuous ones, and ends with the question of where
the non-analyticity can come from at all.

\subsection{Coexistence and first-order transitions}
\label{sec:firsttransition}

\paragraph{Coexistence conditions.} Consider an isolated sample of fixed
$(U,V,N)$ that has separated into two homogeneous parts, $\ell$ and $g$,
through an interface that conducts heat, moves and lets particles pass. The
division $(U_\ell,V_\ell,N_\ell)$ is an internal variable, and by the argument
of Section~\ref{sec:secondlaw} the entropy maximum requires
\begin{equation}\label{eq:coex}
  T_\ell=T_g\equiv T_t,\qquad P_\ell=P_g\equiv P_t,\qquad \mu_\ell=\mu_g\equiv\mu_t .
\end{equation}
The interface contributes to $S$ a term proportional to its area, and the
argument neglects it. The neglect is justified in the thermodynamic limit: the
particles whose environment differs from the bulk lie within a correlation
length of the interface, their number is $\sim L^{d-1}$ against $N\sim L^{d}$
in the bulk, and their entropy differs from the bulk value by $O(k_B)$ each, so
the interface term is of relative order $L^{-1}\sim N^{-1/d}$
(Appendix~\ref{app:extensivity}).

Equations~\eqref{eq:coex} hold between any two parts of a system in
equilibrium. What makes a \emph{phase transition} is that two \emph{distinct}
homogeneous states satisfy them at the same $(T_t,P_t,\mu_t)$. Two states of a
one-component substance are distinct if they differ in at least one density per
particle, $u=U/N$, $v=V/N$ or $s=S/N$. Each density is computed for the
homogeneous phase in question with that phase's own particle number,
$v_\ell=V_\ell/N_\ell$ and $v_g=V_g/N_g$; being intensive, the densities do
not depend on how much of either phase is present, and $N$ here is not the
total number of particles in the sample. Generically the two phases differ in
all three densities: the volumes $v_\ell\neq v_g$, and the entropies
$s_\ell\neq s_g$, whose difference defines the \emph{latent heat} per
particle,
\[
  Q_L\equiv T_t\,(s_g-s_\ell) ,
\]
the heat that must be supplied reversibly at $T_t$ to convert one particle
from phase $\ell$ to phase $g$, by $\dbar Q_{\rm rev}=T\,dS$. The two
differences are not independent, and the relation between them is the
\emph{Clapeyron equation}. Its derivation uses three things. (i)~Each phase
is a homogeneous one-component system, so Gibbs--Duhem,
Eq.~\eqref{eq:gibbsduhem} divided by $N$, holds for each phase separately
with that phase's own densities,
\[
  d\mu_\ell=-s_\ell\,dT+v_\ell\,dP ,\qquad d\mu_g=-s_g\,dT+v_g\,dP .
\]
(ii)~The chemical potential of each phase is a differentiable function
$\mu_\ell(T,P)$, $\mu_g(T,P)$ on a neighbourhood of the coexistence line, not
only on the line itself; this is the statement that each single-phase branch
continues smoothly a little past the transition, as the metastable
continuation of Figure~\ref{fig:firstorder}c, and it is what gives meaning to
$d\mu$ for displacements off the line. (iii)~Along the coexistence line
$\mu_\ell(T,P)=\mu_g(T,P)$ at every point, by Eq.~\eqref{eq:coex}, so for a
displacement $(dT,dP)$ \emph{along} the line the two functions change by the
same amount, $d\mu_\ell=d\mu_g$. Equating the two Gibbs--Duhem expressions,
\[
  -s_\ell\,dT+v_\ell\,dP=-s_g\,dT+v_g\,dP
  \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
  (v_g-v_\ell)\,dP=(s_g-s_\ell)\,dT ,
\]
and when $v_g\neq v_\ell$ this gives the slope of the coexistence line,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:clapeyron}
  \boxed{\;\frac{dP_t}{dT_t}=\frac{s_g-s_\ell}{v_g-v_\ell}=\frac{Q_L}{T_t\,(v_g-v_\ell)}\;}
\end{equation}
The degenerate cases are read off the unboxed form. A transition with
$v_\ell\neq v_g$ and no latent heat has $dP=0$ along the line: the
coexistence line is horizontal, which is possible but non-generic. A
transition with $v_g=v_\ell$ and a latent heat has $dT=0$: the coexistence
line is vertical, $T_t$ is independent of pressure to first order, and the
transition is not seen in the volume at all. If both differences vanish the
two states are not distinct in the fluid densities, and a transition, if
there is one, shows up in some other density (the magnetisation per site of a
magnet, say); the same derivation then goes through with the conjugate pair
$(h,m)$ in place of $(P,-v)$ in the Gibbs--Duhem relation.

\paragraph{The tie line.} Fix $N$ and a point $(T_t,P_t)$ on the coexistence
line. Let $(S_\ell,V_\ell,U_\ell)$ be the entropy, volume and energy of the
whole sample when all $N$ particles are in phase $\ell$ at $(T_t,P_t)$, and
$(S_g,V_g,U_g)$ the same for phase $g$. A two-phase state with a fraction $x$
of the particles in phase $\ell$ consists of $xN$ particles of $\ell$ and
$(1-x)N$ of $g$, each in its bulk state, so by extensivity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tieline}
  S=xS_\ell+(1-x)S_g,\qquad V=xV_\ell+(1-x)V_g,\qquad U=xU_\ell+(1-x)U_g .
\end{equation}
As $x$ runs over $[0,1]$ the point $(S,V)$ traces the straight segment joining
$(S_g,V_g)$ to $(S_\ell,V_\ell)$: the \emph{tie line}. Since $U$ is linear in
the same $x$, $U$ is an \emph{affine} function of $(S,V)$ along the tie line.
The two-phase part of the $U$ surface is therefore ruled by straight segments,
one for each point of the coexistence line, flat along each segment and curved
transverse to them (Figure~\ref{fig:firstorder}b). Solving
Eq.~\eqref{eq:tieline} for $x$ is the \emph{lever rule},
\[
  x=\frac{V_g-V}{V_g-V_\ell}=\frac{v_g-V/N}{v_g-v_\ell} ,
\]
and the same with $S$ in place of $V$: the point $V/N$ divides the segment
$[v_\ell,v_g]$ in the ratio of the two phase fractions, as a balance is divided
by its fulcrum.

\paragraph{What is continuous and what jumps.} Along a tie line $T$ and $P$
are constant, being the slopes of an affine function, and they equal the
single-phase values at the two ends, so the flat piece joins the curved
single-phase pieces with a common tangent. The first derivatives of $U(S,V,N)$
are therefore continuous across the transition: $U$ is $C^{1}$ and
Eq.~\eqref{eq:dU} survives intact. What $U$ loses is \emph{strict} convexity:
its second derivatives vanish on the segment and jump at its ends, so $U$ is
not $C^{2}$, and the response functions, which are second derivatives,
misbehave. Nothing jumps as a function of $(S,V,N)$. What jumps is $S$ and $V$
regarded as functions of $(T,P)$, the \emph{inverse} map: since $T$ and $P$
are constant along the whole tie line, the map $(S,V)\mapsto(T,P)$ collapses a
segment to a point, its inverse cannot be continuous, and as $T$ crosses $T_t$
at fixed $P$ the realised state jumps from one end of the segment to the
other. A jump in $S(T,P)$ and a $C^{1}$ function $U(S,V)$ are two readings of
one non-invertibility. Read in the Gibbs free energy, $G(T,P,N)$ is continuous
at the transition (both phases have the same $\mu$) but its first derivatives
$S=-\partial G/\partial T$ and $V=\partial G/\partial P$ jump: $G$ has a kink
(Figure~\ref{fig:firstorder}c,d).

This is \emph{Ehrenfest's classification}. A transition is of \emph{first
order} if a first derivative of the potential in the controlled variables,
here $G(T,P)$, is discontinuous, which is the same as a jump in the extensive
variables, a latent heat and a tie line. It is \emph{continuous} if the first
derivatives are continuous and the singularity sits in the second or higher
derivatives (Section~\ref{sec:critical}). The two statements, $U$ is $C^{1}$
but not $C^{2}$ and $G$ is $C^{0}$ but not $C^{1}$, are Legendre duals.

\paragraph{Which variables label a state.} Every equilibrium state of the
substance, including every two-phase state, corresponds to exactly one point
$(S,V,N)$: by the state postulate, $(U,V,N)$, equivalently $(S,V,N)$, fixes the
state, and the division into phases is fixed by the entropy maximum, which by
Eq.~\eqref{eq:tieline} is in one-to-one correspondence with the position on
the tie line. Each Legendre transform replaces an extensive coordinate by its
conjugate slope, and on a tie line the slopes are constant. $F(T,V,N)$ still
labels two-phase states uniquely, because $V$ fixes the phase fraction by the
lever rule; $G(T,P,N)$ assigns the whole tie line to the single point
$(T_t,P_t)$ and cannot tell the states on it apart. The more intensive
variables a potential has, the more convenient it is in the laboratory and the
less robust it is at a transition. The fundamental relation in extensive
variables is the most robust representation available.

\subsection{Order parameters and the Landau picture}
\label{sec:landau}

At a transition between a liquid and a gas the two phases differ in density
only; at a transition between a paramagnet and a ferromagnet they differ in
symmetry, the ordered phase having a spontaneous magnetisation $M$ that the
Hamiltonian's symmetry does not single out. A quantity that vanishes in one
phase and not in the other is an \emph{order parameter}, written $\phi$ (the
magnetisation per site $m$, the density difference $\rho-\rho_c$).

The potential adapted to the question is the \emph{constrained free energy}
$F(T,V,N;\phi)$, the free energy of the system with the internal variable
$\phi$ held at a prescribed value by an external constraint. (Its
statistical-mechanical definition as a restricted sum over microstates is given
in Section~\ref{sec:constrained}.) By the minimum principle the equilibrium
value of $\phi$ at given $(T,V,N)$ is the global minimum of
$F(T,V,N;\phi)$ over $\phi$, and the change of the number and position of its
minima as $T$ is varied \emph{is} the phase transition. Landau's theory expands
$F$ in powers of $\phi$ near the transition, in the terms allowed by symmetry,
\[
  \frac{F(T;\phi)}{V}=f_0(T)-h\phi+\frac{a}{2}\,t\,\phi^{2}+\frac{b}{4}\,\phi^{4}+\dots ,
  \qquad t=\frac{T-T_c}{T_c} ,
\]
with $h$ the field conjugate to $\phi$. For $b>0$ and $h=0$ the single minimum
at $\phi=0$ for $t>0$ splits continuously into two minima at
$\phi=\pm\sqrt{a|t|/b}$ for $t<0$: a continuous transition with
$\phi\sim|t|^{1/2}$. If symmetry allows a cubic term, or if $b<0$ and a
positive sextic term stabilises $F$, a second minimum at $\phi\neq0$ becomes
degenerate with the one at $\phi=0$ while the latter is still locally stable:
a first-order transition, with a jump in $\phi$, a latent heat, and metastable
branches on either side. Appendices~\ref{sec:appising} and~\ref{sec:apppotts}
work out both cases in closed form for mean-field models, and
Appendix~\ref{app:mfchi} the susceptibility and its fluctuation reading.

Two cautions belong here. First, the exact $F(T,V,N;\phi)$ of a finite system
below $T_c$ really has two wells and a barrier between them; the barrier is the
free energy of the interface between two ordered domains, of order $\sigma L^{d-1}$,
and it is the whole content of nucleation and metastability. The potential
obtained by controlling the \emph{field} instead, $F_h=\min_\phi[F(\phi)-hV\phi]$,
and transforming back to fixed $\phi$ returns only the convex hull of $F$
(Eq.~\eqref{eq:hull}): fixing the field performs the Maxwell construction
automatically and is blind to the barrier, which is why the constrained free
energy, not the field route, is the right definition. Second, a non-convex
\emph{equilibrium} free energy $F(T,V,N)$ in $V$, as produced by the van der
Waals equation or by any mean-field theory, is an artefact of forbidding phase
separation; the exact $F$ is convex by Section~\ref{sec:whyconvex}, and the
repair is the double-tangent construction, equivalently Maxwell's equal-area
rule (Appendix~\ref{app:maxwell}). The curve where a uniform state ceases to
be locally stable, $\partial^{2}F/\partial\phi^{2}=0$, is the \emph{spinodal}
and lies inside the coexistence curve (\emph{binodal}); between the two the
uniform state is metastable and decays by nucleation.

\subsection{Continuous transitions and critical exponents}
\label{sec:critical}

\emph{Euler homogeneity} is scaling in the extensive variables
at fixed intensive ones; it needs only short-range interactions and a
correlation length small compared with the sample, and it holds at and
arbitrarily close to a critical point. \emph{Analyticity}\footnote{\label{fn:analytic}%
\textbf{Analytic versus differentiable.} $f$ is \emph{analytic} at $x_0$ if
its Taylor series about $x_0$ converges to $f$ in a neighbourhood of $x_0$.
This is strictly stronger than $C^{\infty}$: $e^{-1/t^{2}}$ (with $f(0)=0$)
has all derivatives zero at the origin, so its Taylor series is identically
zero and represents the function nowhere but at $t=0$. \emph{Non-analytic} at
$x_0$ covers three situations: $f$ may fail to be differentiable (a kink, as
$G$ at a first-order transition); it may be $C^{k}$ but not $C^{k+1}$ (as
$|t|^{2-\alpha}$ at a continuous transition, $C^{1}$ but not $C^{2}$ for
