$0<\alpha<1$); or it may be $C^{\infty}$ with a Taylor series that
misrepresents it. Phase transitions realise the first two.
Appendix~\ref{app:analytic} collects what is needed about analytic functions
and analytic continuation.} of the potential in its intensive variables is
what fails, and with it the smoothness of second derivatives.

\paragraph{The two-dimensional Ising specific heat.} Onsager's exact solution
gives a free energy per site whose singular part behaves, with
$t=(T-T_c)/T_c$, as $f_{\rm sing}\sim A\,t^{2}\ln|t|$ with $A>0$. For $t\neq0$,
$d\ln|t|/dt=1/t$ on both branches, so $df_{\rm sing}/dt=A(2t\ln|t|+t)\to0$ and
$d^{2}f_{\rm sing}/dt^{2}=A(2\ln|t|+3)$: $f_{\rm sing}$ is $C^{1}$ at $t=0$ but
not $C^{2}$, and the specific heat $c=-T\,\partial^{2}f/\partial T^{2}$
diverges as $-(2A/T_c)\ln|t|$. Throughout, extensivity remains exact; what
$f(t)$ lacks is a Taylor series about $t=0$.

\paragraph{Exponents and the scaling form.} For a lattice magnet the natural
variables are $(T,h)$, with $m$ conjugate to $h$ as $n$ is to $\mu$, so the
free energy per site $f(T,h)$ is the lattice analogue of the grand potential
density $\omega(T,\mu)$; the specific heat at fixed field, $c_h$, is the
analogue of $c_{V,\mu}$ and not of $c_{V,N}$ (Appendix~\ref{app:cmu}). The
analogy is one of structure, not of particle number. The number of sites of
the magnet is fixed, but it plays the part of the \emph{volume} $V$ of the
fluid, not of $N$. The extensive variable that fluctuates in the magnet is
the total magnetisation $M=\sum_i\sigma_i$, controlled through its conjugate
field $h$ exactly as $N$ is controlled through $\mu$ in the grand ensemble;
the lattice-gas map $n_i=(1+\sigma_i)/2$ makes this literal, turning $M$ into
$N$, $h$ into $\mu$ and the number of sites into $V$. A specific heat
measured at fixed $h$ therefore lets $M$ fluctuate, as $c_{V,\mu}$ lets $N$
fluctuate; the analogue of $c_{V,N}$ would be the specific heat at fixed
magnetisation, $c_M$, which is rarely measured. The
\emph{critical exponents} are defined by the leading behaviour along the axes,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:exponents}
  c_h\sim|t|^{-\alpha},\qquad
  m\sim(-t)^{\beta}\ \ (h=0,\ t\to0^{-}),\qquad
  \chi\sim|t|^{-\gamma},\qquad
  m\sim|h|^{1/\delta}\ \ (t=0),
\end{equation}
with $m=-\partial f/\partial h$ and $\chi=\partial m/\partial h$, and by the
correlation length $\xi\sim|t|^{-\nu}$ and the decay $G(r)\sim r^{-(d-2+\eta)}$
of the order-parameter correlation function at $t=0$. Near the critical point
the singular part of $f$ takes the \emph{generalised homogeneous} form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:scaling}
  \boxed{\;f_{\rm sing}(t,h)=|t|^{2-\alpha}\,\Phi\!\left(\frac{h}{|t|^{\Delta}}\right)\;}
\end{equation}
in which $t$ and $h$ are dimensionless ($h=\mu_0\mathcal{H}/k_BT_c$ for a
lattice magnet with moment $\mu_0$ per site), $\Delta$ is the \emph{gap
exponent}, and $\Phi$ is a \emph{scaling function}, analytic on each branch
$t\gtrless0$ away from the origin. The scaling hypothesis is the assumption
that all the non-analyticity has been collected into the prefactor and the
argument. \emph{Universality} is the statement that $\Phi$, and with it the
exponents and the ratios of amplitudes built from it, depend only on the
dimensionality, the symmetry of the order parameter and the range of the
interactions, and not on microscopic details, up to two non-universal scale
factors for $t$ and $h$ and the value of $T_c$ (Appendix~\ref{app:cusp}
discusses what universality does and does not deliver).

Each derivative with respect to $h$ brings down one factor $|t|^{-\Delta}$,
\[
  \frac{\partial^{k}f_{\rm sing}}{\partial h^{k}}\bigg|_{h=0}
  =\Phi^{(k)}(0)\,|t|^{2-\alpha-k\Delta} ,
\]
so the exponents of the successive field derivatives form a ladder with
equally spaced rungs, and $\Delta$ is the gap between them. Reading off $k=1$
and $k=2$, $\beta=2-\alpha-\Delta$ and $-\gamma=2-\alpha-2\Delta$. On the
critical isotherm $t\to0$ at fixed $h\neq0$ the argument of $\Phi$ diverges,
and $m$ stays finite only if $\Phi'(y)\sim y^{\beta/\Delta}$ at large $y$,
giving $m\sim h^{\beta/\Delta}$, i.e.\ $1/\delta=\beta/\Delta$. Together,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rushbrooke}
  \boxed{\;\Delta=\beta\delta=\beta+\gamma ,\qquad \alpha+2\beta+\gamma=2\;}
\end{equation}
the second being the Rushbrooke identity. For the three-dimensional Ising
class $\beta\simeq0.326$ and $\gamma\simeq1.237$, so $\Delta\simeq1.563$.
With the hypothesis that the singular free energy per correlation volume is
of order $k_BT$, $f_{\rm sing}\sim\xi^{-d}$ (\emph{hyperscaling}), one obtains
in addition $2-\alpha=d\nu$, and the correlation-function form gives
$\gamma=\nu(2-\eta)$ (Appendix~\ref{app:fss}). Two exponents then fix all the
others.

\paragraph{Response functions at a critical point.} The divergence of $\chi$
and of $c_h$ at $t=0$ is the limiting case of the stability inequalities of
Section~\ref{sec:stability}: the curvature of the potential in the intensive
variable diverges, so the curvature of its transform in the conjugate extensive
variable vanishes, $\partial^{2}F/\partial M^{2}=1/\chi\to0$. The body is
infinitely soft against a change of its order parameter, and the fluctuations
of the order parameter, which Section~\ref{sec:ensembles} identifies with
$k_BT\chi$, become correlated over the diverging length $\xi$. What goes wrong
in a mean-field treatment, and when it goes wrong, is decided by comparing
these fluctuations with the order parameter itself, the Ginzburg criterion of
Appendix~\ref{app:mfchi}.

\subsection{Where the non-analyticity comes from}
\label{sec:analyticity}

For any \emph{finite} system the partition function
$Z=\sum_ie^{-\beta E_i}$ is a finite sum of positive exponentials. It is an
entire function of $\beta$ (and of the field, and of the fugacity), it has no
zeros on the real axis, and $F=-k_BT\ln Z$ is therefore analytic in every
real variable. A finite system has no phase transition: its specific heat and
susceptibility have finite, rounded maxima at every size, and there is no
sharp $T_c$. Non-analyticity can arise only in the thermodynamic limit, in
which the number of terms becomes infinite and the complex zeros of $Z$, the
\emph{Lee--Yang zeros} in the fugacity or field plane and the \emph{Fisher
zeros} in the temperature plane, can accumulate on a curve that pinches the
real axis (Yang and Lee \cite{YangLee}). Where the zeros pinch the axis the
limit of $\ln Z/N$ is continuous but not analytic, and the two sides of the
pinch are two phases. For the ferromagnetic Ising model the zeros lie on the
unit circle in the plane of $e^{-2\beta h}$, and the transition at $h=0$
occurs exactly when their density at the real axis becomes nonzero.

Three consequences follow. A phase transition is a property of the limit,
never of a finite sample, which is why finite-size scaling is needed to
extract critical behaviour from a simulation (Appendix~\ref{app:fss}). The
free energy of one phase is an analytic function in the interior of that phase
and cannot in general be continued analytically through the transition into
the other phase: for short-range models the free energy at a first-order
transition has an essential singularity on the coexistence line (Isakov
\cite{Isakov}), so the ``metastable branch'' of a mean-field theory, which
is an analytic continuation, is a construct and not a property of the exact
potential (Appendix~\ref{app:analytic}). And for a system whose Hamiltonian is
quadratic in creation and annihilation operators the grand potential is a sum
of analytic one-orbital terms (Appendix~\ref{app:quadratic}), so independent
fermions have no transition at any temperature; the one transition of an ideal
gas, Bose--Einstein condensation, arises from the constraint of fixed particle
number when $\mu$ reaches the bottom of the spectrum, not from the one-orbital
terms themselves. A transition in an interacting system is a property of the
many-body spectrum, and Section~\ref{sec:filters} says what it requires of it.

\section{Statistical ensembles and their equivalence}
\label{sec:ensembles}

Statistical mechanics computes the potentials of Section~\ref{sec:family}
from the microscopic Hamiltonian. Each ensemble corresponds to a choice of
which variables the environment fixes, and computes the corresponding
potential as $-k_BT$ times the logarithm of a partition function.

\subsection{The microcanonical ensemble}
\label{sec:microcanonical}

For an isolated system with energy in a narrow window $[E,E+\delta E]$, let
$W(E,V,N)$ be the number of microstates (energy eigenstates, or cells of
classical phase space) in the window. The fundamental postulate is that in
equilibrium every one of them is equally probable, and Boltzmann's definition
of the entropy is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:boltzmann}
  \boxed{\;S=k_B\ln W\;}
\end{equation}
This $S$ is extensive, because the number of microstates of two independent
parts is the product of the numbers; it is increasing in $E$ for a spectrum
unbounded above; and $(\partial S/\partial E)_{V,N}=1/T$ identifies the
temperature with the growth rate of the level density, as the argument of
Section~\ref{sec:secondlaw} requires. The width $\delta E$ affects $S$ only by
a term $O(\ln N)$, negligible against the extensive $S$. For a general
probability distribution over microstates the same quantity is the
\emph{Gibbs entropy}\footnote{\label{fn:gibbsS}\textbf{Gibbs, Shannon and
von Neumann entropy.} For a probability distribution $\{p_i\}$ over
microstates, or more generally a density matrix $\rho$ (Hermitian, $\rho\geq0$,
$\Tr\rho=1$), $S[\rho]\equiv-k_B\Tr(\rho\ln\rho)=-k_B\sum_ip_i\ln p_i$, the
second form in the eigenbasis of $\rho$, with $0\ln0\equiv0$. Gibbs (1902)
introduced it for the classical phase-space distribution and von Neumann
(1927) for the density matrix; Shannon (1948) introduced the same functional,
without $k_B$ and usually with $\log_2$, as the measure of the information
missing when only the distribution is known. The three are one quantity in
different units and contexts. It is defined for any state, in or out of
equilibrium; it vanishes for a pure state and is largest, $k_B\ln D$, for the
uniform distribution over $D$ states, where it reduces to
Eq.~\eqref{eq:boltzmann}. For the equilibrium state of any ensemble it equals
the thermodynamic entropy (Section~\ref{sec:partition}).}
of that distribution, which reduces to Eq.~\eqref{eq:boltzmann} for the
uniform distribution.

\subsection{Reservoirs, Boltzmann weights and Lagrange multipliers}
\label{sec:reservoir}

Every other ensemble is obtained from the microcanonical one by the same step.
Let the system exchange with a large reservoir an extensive conserved quantity
$X$ (energy, particle number, volume), the composite being isolated, so that
$X+X_{\rm res}=X_{\rm tot}$ is fixed. The composite is microcanonical, and
every one of its microstates is equally likely. The probability of one
particular microstate $i$ of the system, with value $X_i$, is therefore
proportional to the number of reservoir microstates compatible with it,
$W_{\rm res}(X_{\rm tot}-X_i)=\exp[S_{\rm res}(X_{\rm tot}-X_i)/k_B]$. Because
the reservoir is large, $X_i\ll X_{\rm tot}$ and its entropy may be expanded to
first order,
\[
  \frac{S_{\rm res}(X_{\rm tot}-X_i)}{k_B}\simeq\frac{S_{\rm res}(X_{\rm tot})}{k_B}
  -\frac{X_i}{k_B}\,\frac{\partial S_{\rm res}}{\partial X_{\rm res}} .
\]
The derivative is an intensive property of the reservoir alone, read off from
Eq.~\eqref{eq:dS}: $1/T$ for $X=E$, $-\mu/T$ for $X=N$, $P/T$ for $X=V$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:weights}
  p_i\propto e^{-\beta E_i}\ \ (X=E),\qquad
  p_i\propto e^{-\beta(E_i-\mu N_i)}\ \ (E,N),\qquad
  p_i\propto e^{-\beta(E_i+PV_i)}\ \ (E,V) .
\end{equation}
The rule is general: each extensive quantity that the system exchanges with a
reservoir, and which is therefore fixed only on average, enters the weight
through one factor $e^{\pm\beta fX}$, with $f$ the conjugate intensive variable
of the reservoir and the sign that of the term $f\,dX$ in $dU$. A magnet in an
applied field is an ensemble with the magnetisation fixed on average, weight
$e^{-\beta(\hat H_0-\mathcal{H}\hat M)}$. The second-order term of the expansion
is smaller by the ratio of the system's heat capacity to the reservoir's and
vanishes for an ideal reservoir.

The same weights follow without any reservoir from the maximum-entropy
construction of Section~\ref{sec:lagrange}: maximising the Gibbs entropy
subject to prescribed averages $\langle E\rangle$ and $\langle X\rangle$ gives
$\ln p_i=\text{const}-\beta E_i-\lambda X_i$, with one Lagrange multiplier per
constraint, and comparison with Eq.~\eqref{eq:weights} identifies
$\lambda=\mp\beta f$. In both readings the intensive variable is not a property
of the system: it is the price, in entropy, of moving one unit of $X$ from the
reservoir to the system, and its value is whatever makes $\langle X\rangle$
come out right. This is the sense in which $\mu$ ``must be chosen'' in the
grand ensemble: a hard constraint on $N$ has been traded for a soft one,
enforced on average.

\paragraph{The size of the fluctuations.} The trade is harmless for a
macroscopic system. In the grand ensemble $N=V\,n(T,\mu)$ with $n$ intensive,
so $(\partial N/\partial\mu)_{T,V}=V\,\partial n/\partial\mu$ is extensive,
of order $N$; and Section~\ref{sec:canonical} shows that
$\Var(N)=k_BT(\partial N/\partial\mu)_{T,V}$. Hence
\[
  \frac{\sqrt{\Var(N)}}{N}\;\propto\;\frac{\sqrt N}{N}=N^{-1/2} :
\]
the relative width of the particle-number distribution vanishes as
$N^{-1/2}$, and likewise for the energy in the canonical ensemble, with
$\Var(E)=k_BT^{2}C_V\propto N$. The absolute fluctuations are extensive and do
not vanish; Section~\ref{sec:equivalence} shows that this is exactly where
ensembles can still differ.

\subsection{The ensembles and their potentials}
\label{sec:partition}

Normalising the weights of Eq.~\eqref{eq:weights} gives the partition
functions, and the thermodynamic potential in each case is $-k_BT$ times the
logarithm. For a system exchanging energy with a bath at temperature $T$, at
fixed $V$ and $N$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Z}
  Z(T,V,N)=\sum_ie^{-\beta E_i}=\Tr\,e^{-\beta\hat H},\qquad
  p_i=\frac{e^{-\beta E_i}}{Z},\qquad
  \boxed{\;F=-k_BT\ln Z\;}
\end{equation}
For a system exchanging energy and particles with a reservoir at $(T,\mu)$,
at fixed $V$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Xi}
  \Xi(T,V,\mu)=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}\sum_ie^{-\beta(E_{i,N}-\mu N)}
  =\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}z^{N}Z(T,V,N),\qquad z=e^{\beta\mu},
\end{equation}
with $z$ the fugacity, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Omega}
  \boxed{\;\Omega=-k_BT\ln\Xi\;}
\end{equation}
and since $\Omega=-PV$ this says $\ln\Xi=\beta PV$. For a system exchanging
energy and volume with a reservoir at $(T,P)$, at fixed $N$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Delta}
  \Delta(T,P,N)=\frac{1}{V_0}\int_0^\infty\!dV\,e^{-\beta PV}Z(T,V,N),\qquad
  \boxed{\;G=-k_BT\ln\Delta\;}
\end{equation}
where the reference volume $V_0$ makes the integral dimensionless and
contributes a subextensive $O(\ln V)$ to $G$. The table collects the
correspondence.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{llll}
\toprule
Ensemble & Fixed & Fluctuating & Potential \\
\midrule
Microcanonical & $E,V,N$ & --- & $S=k_B\ln W$ \\
Canonical & $T,V,N$ & $E$ & $F=-k_BT\ln Z$ \\
Grand canonical & $T,V,\mu$ & $E,N$ & $\Omega=-k_BT\ln\Xi$ \\
Isothermal--isobaric & $T,P,N$ & $E,V$ & $G=-k_BT\ln\Delta$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The pattern is a Legendre transform on the thermodynamic side matched by a
Laplace transform on the statistical side: $Z=\sum_EW(E)e^{-\beta E}$,
$\Xi=\sum_NZe^{\beta\mu N}$, $\Delta=\int dV\,Ze^{-\beta PV}$. Evaluating each
Laplace transform by steepest descent in the thermodynamic limit reproduces the
Legendre transform, with the saddle point at the equilibrium value of the
fluctuating variable (Section~\ref{sec:equivalence}).

\paragraph{The identification with thermodynamics.} That $-k_BT\ln Z$ is the
Helmholtz free energy, and not merely a function with its name, rests on two
checks. First, the Gibbs entropy of the canonical distribution is the
thermodynamic one: substituting $\ln p_i=-\beta E_i-\ln Z$ into
$S=-k_B\sum_ip_i\ln p_i$ gives the exact identity
\[
  S=k_B\big(\ln Z+\beta U\big),\qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad F=U-TS .
\]
Second, the reversible heat is $T\,dS$. Let the Hamiltonian depend on the
controlled parameters $\lambda$ through its levels $E_i(\lambda)$, so that
$\ln Z$ depends on $\beta$ and $\lambda$ and
$d\ln Z=-U\,d\beta-\beta\sum_ip_i\,dE_i=-U\,d\beta-\beta\,\dbar W$, the last
step by the definition of work in Section~\ref{sec:work}. Differentiating the
identity above,
\[
  \frac{dS}{k_B}=d\ln Z+U\,d\beta+\beta\,dU
  =-U\,d\beta-\beta\,\dbar W+U\,d\beta+\beta\,dU=\beta\,(dU-\dbar W) ,
\]
that is, $T\,dS=dU-\dbar W=\dbar Q$ along a quasi-static sequence of Gibbs
states, which is Eq.~\eqref{eq:TdS}. The only inputs are that the state is
Gibbsian and that work is the energy delivered through $\lambda$.

\paragraph{The Gibbs variational principle.} The minimum principle of
Section~\ref{sec:minimum} has an exact statistical counterpart. For an
arbitrary density matrix $\rho$ define
$\mathcal{F}[\rho]=\Tr(\rho\hat H)+k_BT\Tr(\rho\ln\rho)=U[\rho]-TS[\rho]$. Then
\[
  \mathcal{F}[\rho]-F=k_BT\,D(\rho\,\|\,\rho_{\rm eq})\;\geq\;0 ,
  \qquad \rho_{\rm eq}=e^{-\beta\hat H}/Z ,
\]
with $D(\rho\|\sigma)=\Tr[\rho(\ln\rho-\ln\sigma)]$ the relative entropy, which
is non-negative and vanishes only for $\rho=\sigma$ (Klein's inequality). The
Gibbs state is the global minimiser of the free energy over all states, and
$F=-k_BT\ln Z$ is the minimum value. Restricting the trial states to the Gibbs
states of a family of solvable Hamiltonians $\hat H_0$ gives the
Gibbs--Bogoliubov inequality $F\leq F_0+\langle\hat H-\hat H_0\rangle_0$, the
basis of variational mean-field theory. Appendix~\ref{app:gibbsvar} proves
both.

\subsection{Response functions as variances}
\label{sec:canonical}

Differentiating $\ln Z$ twice with respect to $\beta$ at fixed $V$, $N$ gives
$\partial\ln Z/\partial\beta=-\langle E\rangle$ and
$\partial^{2}\ln Z/\partial\beta^{2}=\langle E^{2}\rangle-\langle E\rangle^{2}=\Var(E)$.
With $S=-\partial F/\partial T$ and $d\beta/dT=-k_B\beta^{2}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:CVvar}
  \boxed{\;C_V=T\left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial T}\right)_{V,N}
  =k_B\beta^{2}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\ln Z}{\partial\beta^{2}}\right)_{V,N}
  =\frac{\Var(E)}{k_BT^{2}}\;}
\end{equation}
The heat capacity is the energy variance: a system that absorbs much heat per
kelvin is one whose energy fluctuates much at fixed temperature. The same
computation in every ensemble gives, for every conjugate pair,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fluctresp}
  \left(\frac{\partial N}{\partial\mu}\right)_{T,V}=\frac{\Var(N)}{k_BT},\qquad
  V\kappa_T=\frac{\Var(V)}{k_BT}\ \ \text{(isothermal--isobaric)},\qquad
  \chi_T=\frac{\Var(M)}{k_BT},
\end{equation}
and, at fixed $\mu$, $C_{V,\mu}=\Var(E-\mu N)/k_BT^{2}$. In every case
\[
  \text{curvature of potential}=\text{response function}=\text{variance of the conjugate quantity},
\]
the elementary form of the fluctuation--dissipation theorem. Because the right
side is a variance, statistical mechanics delivers the stability inequalities
of Section~\ref{sec:stability} automatically. (For a non-conserved $\hat M$ the
variance becomes the Kubo canonical correlation, likewise non-negative;
Section~\ref{sec:filters}.)

A response function is labelled not only by what is differentiated but by
everything held fixed while differentiating. Fixed $\mu$ and fixed $N$ give
genuinely different heat capacities, $C_{V,\mu}$ and $C_{V,N}$, related by a
conversion formula whose correction term is of the same order as the heat
capacities themselves; Appendix~\ref{app:cmu} derives it and checks it on the
ideal gas.

\subsection{The constrained free energy as a histogram}
\label{sec:constrained}

The constrained free energy of Section~\ref{sec:landau} has a precise
definition as a partial trace. Let $\hat\phi$ be the operator whose value is
the order parameter, for instance $\hat\phi=V^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{V}\hat\sigma_i^{z}$
for the magnetisation per site of a lattice of $V$ sites. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:constrainedF}
  \boxed{\;e^{-\beta F(T,V,N;\phi)}=\Tr\Big[\delta\big(\hat\phi-\phi\big)\,e^{-\beta\hat H}\Big]
  \equiv Z(\phi)\;}
\end{equation}
one sums the Boltzmann weight over only those microstates whose order parameter
has the prescribed value. Dividing by the full $Z=\int d\phi\,Z(\phi)$ gives
the probability density of $\phi$ in the unconstrained ensemble,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Pphi}
  P(\phi)=\frac{Z(\phi)}{Z}=\big\langle\delta(\hat\phi-\phi)\big\rangle ,
  \qquad
  F(\phi)=-k_BT\ln Z(\phi)=-k_BT\ln P(\phi)+F_{\rm eq} ,
\end{equation}
the first equality for $F(\phi)$ being Eq.~\eqref{eq:constrainedF} itself and
the second following from it by inserting $Z(\phi)=P(\phi)\,Z$ and
$F_{\rm eq}=-k_BT\ln Z$.
This makes $F(\phi)$ measurable: a Monte Carlo or molecular-dynamics run
samples the Boltzmann distribution, and $-k_BT$ times the logarithm of the
histogram of $\phi$ is $F(\phi)$ up to a constant. In any other ensemble the
weight is replaced by that ensemble's weight.

The minimum principle follows from the extensivity of $F(\phi)=Vf(\phi)$.
The sum $Z=\sum_\phi e^{-\beta Vf(\phi)}$ over the $M$ attainable values of
$\phi$ ($M=V+1$ for Ising spins) is bounded by its largest term and by $M$
times it, and the largest term is the one with the \emph{smallest} $f$:
\[
  e^{-\beta Vf_{\min}}\leq Z\leq M\,e^{-\beta Vf_{\min}}
  \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
  F_{\rm eq}=V\min_\phi f(\phi)-O(k_BT\ln M) .
\]
The correction is subextensive, so per unit volume
$f_{\rm eq}=\min_\phi f(\phi)$ and $\partial f/\partial\phi=0$ at the minimum,
as the minimum principle states.

\paragraph{The field route gives the convex hull.} Coupling a source $h$ to
$V\hat\phi$ gives the unconstrained free energy
$F_h(h)=-k_BT\ln\Tr\,e^{-\beta(\hat H-hV\hat\phi)}$. Organising the trace by
the values of $\hat\phi$ (assume $[\hat\phi,\hat H]=0$, as for classical
spins), $e^{-\beta F_h}=\int d\phi\,e^{-\beta V[f(\phi)-h\phi]}$, and the same
largest-term argument gives, per unit volume as $V\to\infty$,
\[
  f_h(h)=\min_\phi\big[f(\phi)-h\phi\big] ,
\]
the Legendre transform of Eq.~\eqref{eq:legendre_inf} with $(S,T)\to(\phi,h)$,
up to sign. Transforming back, $\hat f(\phi)\equiv\max_h[f_h(h)+h\phi]$ is by
Eq.~\eqref{eq:hull} the convex hull of $f$: the two constructions agree
wherever $f$ coincides with its hull and differ exactly on the interval where
$f$ lies above it, which contains every region where $f$ is locally concave.
Below $T_c$ the hull is the straight common tangent across the two wells, so
every value of $h$ selects a state outside $(-\phi_0,\phi_0)$, and the states
inside, which are reached only as mixtures, are never selected by any field.
This is the statistical form of the statement made in
Section~\ref{sec:landau}, and of the Maxwell construction of
Appendix~\ref{app:maxwell}.

\subsection{Ensemble equivalence and its limits}
\label{sec:equivalence}

\paragraph{Agreement of the potentials.} Write
$\Xi=\sum_Ne^{-\beta[F(T,V,N)-\mu N]}$ and evaluate the sum by steepest
descent.\footnote{\textbf{Saddle-point (steepest-descent, Laplace)
approximation.} To evaluate $I=\int dN\,e^{-g(N)}$ when $g$ is extensive:
replace the sum over integer $N$ by an integral, the summand varying on the
scale $\sqrt N\gg1$; find the minimum $N^\ast$ of $g$; expand
$g(N)=g(N^\ast)+\tfrac12g''(N^\ast)(N-N^\ast)^{2}+\dots$; and do the Gaussian
integral, $I\simeq e^{-g(N^\ast)}\sqrt{2\pi/g''(N^\ast)}$. Each derivative of
the extensive $g$ with respect to the extensive $N$ costs a factor $1/N$, so
over the width $\sqrt N$ of the peak the cubic term is $O(N^{-1/2})$ and
corrects $\ln I$ only at $O(1/N)$.} The exponent is stationary at $N^\ast$
with $(\partial F/\partial N)_{T,V}=\mu$, the condition for particle
equilibrium with the reservoir, and the Gaussian integral gives
\[
  \Omega=-k_BT\ln\Xi
  =\underbrace{F(N^\ast)-\mu N^\ast}_{\text{Legendre transform}}
  \;-\;\frac{k_BT}{2}\ln\frac{2\pi}{\beta F''},
  \qquad F''=\left(\frac{\partial\mu}{\partial N}\right)_{T,V}=\frac{1}{\beta\Var(N)} .
\]
Since $\Var(N)=O(N)$ the correction is $-\tfrac12k_BT\ln N+O(1)$: subextensive,
and invisible in the densities. That is the whole content of ensemble
equivalence: \emph{the ensembles produce the same thermodynamic potentials up
to subextensive terms}, hence the same surface of equilibrium states.

\paragraph{Response functions still differ.}
\label{sec:respdiffer}
Equivalence states that the function $S(T,V,\mu)$ is the same in both
descriptions. A response function is a directional derivative, fixed by the
surface \emph{and} the direction of differentiation; fixing $\mu$ and fixing
$N$ define two different curves through the same point, so
$C_{V,N}=T(\partial S/\partial T)_{V,N}$ and $C_{V,\mu}=T(\partial S/\partial T)_{V,\mu}$
are two different numbers, exactly as $C_P\neq C_V$ within a single ensemble.
Their difference is not a $1/N$ correction: from Appendix~\ref{app:cmu},
\[
  C_{V,\mu}-C_{V,N}=T\,\frac{\big[(\partial N/\partial T)_{V,\mu}\big]^{2}}
  {(\partial N/\partial\mu)_{T,V}}=O(N) ,
\]
the same order as the heat capacities themselves. The slogan ``$N^{-1/2}$''
refers to relative fluctuations, whereas response functions are built from
absolute fluctuations, which are extensive. Equivalence is a statement about
potentials, never a licence to interchange derivatives.

\paragraph{Inequivalence at a first-order transition.}
\label{sec:firstorder}
Here equivalence genuinely fails, and the mechanism is the tie line of
Section~\ref{sec:firsttransition} seen in energy space. At a temperature-driven
first-order transition the ordered and disordered phases have different energy
densities $e_o\neq e_d$ at the same $T_t$ and equal free energies, so the
canonical energy distribution $P(E)\propto W(E)e^{-\beta E}=e^{[S(E)-E/T]/k_B}$
has two peaks of equal weight, at $E=Ve_o$ and $E=Ve_d$, separated by a
minimum. The dip is the free-energy cost of the interface between coexisting
phases, so
\[
  \ln\frac{P_{\max}}{P_{\min}}\;\sim\;\frac{2\sigma L^{d-1}}{k_BT_t} ,
\]
with $\sigma$ the interfacial tension (Appendix~\ref{app:histograms} derives
this). A barrier that grows with $L$ is the Lee--Kosterlitz criterion for a
first-order transition, and it is why multicanonical sampling was invented:
ordinary Metropolis dynamics cannot cross it. Microcanonically all energies
between $Ve_o$ and $Ve_d$ are accessible, as phase-separated states with an
interface; canonically they are exponentially suppressed. In the thermodynamic
limit $s(e)$ is affine across that interval, the Legendre transform $s\mapsto f$
collapses the whole interval onto the single temperature $T_t$, and the
canonical ensemble cannot resolve which $e$ the system has. The microcanonical
description is strictly finer. At finite $L$ the affine segment of $s(e)$ has
a convex intruder, i.e.\ a negative microcanonical specific heat, which the
canonical ensemble never shows.

At a continuous transition the ensembles agree on the free energy but can
disagree on the critical exponents: a hidden constraint such as fixed
composition renormalises them whenever $\alpha>0$ (Fisher renormalisation,
Appendix~\ref{app:fisher}). For systems with long-range interactions
(self-gravitating matter, unscreened plasmas, infinite-range spin models)
$s(e)$ can be genuinely non-concave even in the thermodynamic limit, and the
microcanonical ensemble then supports stable states with negative specific
heat that have no canonical counterpart.

\section{Microscopic view: thermodynamics as a filter on the spectrum}
\label{sec:filters}

Everything so far has been top-down: a potential is postulated, Legendre
transformed and differentiated, and the ensembles compute it as the logarithm
of a partition function. This section runs the chain in the opposite direction
and asks what a measurement at temperature $T$ actually \emph{sees} of the
spectrum. The starting point is one identity: every partition function is the
\emph{Laplace transform} of the many-body density of states. Everything
measured in equilibrium, the heat capacity, the entropy, the response
functions, is therefore the many-body spectrum seen through the Boltzmann
weight, and every response is a covariance. Two consequences are drawn. The
low-energy shape of the density of states fixes the low-temperature power laws
of the observables, and this is the origin of the scaling forms of
Section~\ref{sec:critical}; and a divergent response requires correlated
fluctuations of macroscopically many degrees of freedom,
which an additive spectrum (non-interacting) cannot provide.
The special case of a quadratic Hamiltonian,
where the many-body weight collapses onto the single-particle levels and each
observable sees the single-particle spectrum through its own window, is worked
out in Appendix~\ref{app:quadratic}; it is the elementary statistical
mechanics of metals and phonons, and it is irrelevant to phase transitions.

The representation used throughout is the eigenbasis of the full many-body
Hamiltonian, interacting or not, which is assumed to be known. The whole
construction is at fixed $\mu$; the conversion to fixed $N$ uses the formula
of Appendix~\ref{app:cmu}, which is not always a small correction.

\subsection{Exact eigenstates}
\label{sec:filt_exact}
Let $ \hat H $ be the full many-body Hamiltonian, interacting or not, of a system
whose total particle number is conserved, $ [\hat H, \hat N] = 0 $. We assume that it has
been solved: the commuting Hermitian operators $ \hat H $ and $ \hat N $ have a
common orthonormal eigenbasis $ \{|a\rangle\} $ of the Fock space,
\[
  \hat H\,|a\rangle = E_a\,|a\rangle , \qquad \hat N\,|a\rangle = N_a\,|a\rangle ,
\]
Nothing else is assumed.

\begin{remark}
The conservation law is required only for the species to which $ \mu $ is
attached. The electron--phonon Hamiltonian is the standard example: it
creates and destroys phonons freely, but it commutes with the electron number,
and $ \hat N $ is the electron number. Phonons, like photons and magnons, are
not a conserved species, they carry no chemical potential, and the ensemble is
simply canonical in them; their number in equilibrium is whatever minimises
the free energy, which is the statement $ \mu_{\rm ph} = 0 $. A Hamiltonian
with \emph{no} conserved number at all, such as the Bardeen--Cooper--Schrieffer
mean-field Hamiltonian with its pairing terms, is handled by the same
construction with $ \hat N $ dropped and $ \mu $ absent: $ \xi_a = E_a $ and
$ \Xi $ becomes $ Z $. In the pairing case the number is in fact conserved by
the underlying Hamiltonian and broken only by the mean-field approximation;
what is then diagonalised is $ \hat H_{\rm MF} - \mu\hat N $ with $ \mu $ fixed
afterwards by the condition $ \langle\hat N\rangle = N $, so that
$ \hat{\mathcal{H}} $ below is the object that has an eigenbasis even though
$ \hat H_{\rm MF} $ and $ \hat N $ separately do not share one.
\end{remark} In an interacting system the $ |a\rangle $ are in general
superpositions of very many Slater determinants (or permanents), and nothing
requires $ E_a $ to be related to single-particle energies.
We abbreviate
\[
  \hat{\mathcal{H}} \equiv \hat H - \mu\hat N , \qquad \xi_a \equiv E_a - \mu N_a ,
\]
so that $ \hat{\mathcal{H}}|a\rangle = \xi_a|a\rangle $. The hat distinguishes the
operator $ \hat{\mathcal{H}} $ from the applied magnetic field $ \mathcal{H} $, and
$ \xi_a $ is the many-body counterpart of the single-particle $ \xi_\lambda =
\varepsilon_\lambda - \mu $ of Appendix~\ref{app:quadratic}.

\paragraph{The partition function as a normalisation.} The grand canonical
equilibrium state of Section~\ref{sec:ensembles} is the density matrix
\[
  \hat\rho_{\rm eq} = \frac{e^{-\beta\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}{\Xi} , \qquad
  \Tr\hat\rho_{\rm eq} = 1
  \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
  \Xi(T,\mu) = \Tr e^{-\beta\hat{\mathcal{H}}} :
\]
the grand partition function is the normalisation of the equilibrium density
matrix. A trace may be evaluated in any orthonormal basis, and the eigenbasis turns
it into a sum. A function of an operator acts on each eigenvector as the same
function of the eigenvalue (the spectral theorem), $ e^{-\beta\hat{\mathcal{H}}}|a\rangle =
e^{-\beta \xi_a}|a\rangle $, so $ \langle a|e^{-\beta\hat{\mathcal{H}}}|a\rangle = e^{-\beta \xi_a} $ and
\[
  \Xi = \sum_a \langle a|e^{-\beta\hat{\mathcal{H}}}|a\rangle
  = \sum_a e^{-\beta(E_a - \mu N_a)}
  = \sum_N e^{\beta\mu N}\!\!\sum_{a\,:\,N_a = N}\!\! e^{-\beta E_a}
  = \sum_N e^{\beta\mu N}\,Z_N ,
\]
which is Eq.~\eqref{eq:Xi}. In the same basis the
density matrix is diagonal, $ \hat\rho_{\rm eq} = \sum_a p_a\,|a\rangle\langle a| $ with
$ p_a = e^{-\beta \xi_a}/\Xi $: the system is found in the eigenstate $ a $ with
probability $ p_a $, and $ \sum_ap_a = 1 $.

\subsection{Thermodynamics in the eigenbasis}
\label{sec:filt_mb}

\paragraph{The many-body filter.} Let $ g(\xi) \equiv \sum_a\delta(\xi - \xi_a) $ be the
many-body density of states of $ \hat{\mathcal{H}} $, and
$ P_T(\xi) \equiv g(\xi)\,e^{-\beta \xi}/\Xi $ the probability density of $ \xi $ in
equilibrium. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_mb}
  \Xi = \int\! d\xi\; g(\xi)\,e^{-\beta \xi} , \qquad
  S = -k_B\sum_a p_a\ln p_a = \frac{\langle\hat{\mathcal{H}}\rangle - \Omega}{T} , \qquad
  \boxed{\;C_\mu = \frac{\Var(\hat{\mathcal{H}})}{k_BT^{2}}\;}
\end{equation}
with $ \Omega = -k_BT\ln\Xi $. The first is the definition of $ g $; the thermodynamics
is the Laplace transform of the many-body spectrum. The second follows from
$ \ln p_a = -\beta \xi_a - \ln\Xi $. For the third, $ \hat{\mathcal{H}} $ does not depend on $ T $ at
fixed $ \mu $, so $ \partial\ln\Xi/\partial\beta = -\langle\hat{\mathcal{H}}\rangle $ and
$ \partial^{2}\ln\Xi/\partial\beta^{2} = \Var(\hat{\mathcal{H}}) $; differentiating $ TS = \langle\hat{\mathcal{H}}\rangle - \Omega $
with $ (\partial\Omega/\partial T)_\mu = -S $ gives $ T(\partial S/\partial T)_\mu =
(\partial\langle\hat{\mathcal{H}}\rangle/\partial T)_\mu = \Var(\hat{\mathcal{H}})/k_BT^{2} $, using
$ d\beta/dT = -1/k_BT^{2} $. This is the result of Appendix~\ref{app:cmu}, now
written in the eigenbasis:
\[
  C_\mu = \frac{1}{k_BT^{2}}\int\! d\xi\; P_T(\xi)\,\big(\xi - \langle\hat{\mathcal{H}}\rangle\big)^{2} ,
\]
the variance of the many-body spectrum seen through the Boltzmann weight. It is
non-negative for every Hamiltonian.

\paragraph{Responses as covariances.} Let $ \hat A $ be a Hermitian operator that is
conserved, $ [\hat A, \hat H] = [\hat A, \hat N] = 0 $; the eigenbasis may then be chosen
to diagonalise it as well, $ \hat A|a\rangle = A_a|a\rangle $. A field $ f $ coupled to it,
$ \hat{\mathcal{H}} \to \hat{\mathcal{H}} - f\hat A $, leaves the eigenvectors unchanged and shifts the
eigenvalues to $ \xi_a - fA_a $. For any $ \hat B $ diagonal in the same basis,
$ \langle\hat B\rangle = \sum_aB_a\,e^{-\beta(\xi_a - fA_a)}/\Xi(f) $, and differentiating the
numerator and the normalisation,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_mb_response}
  \frac{\partial\langle\hat B\rangle}{\partial f}\bigg|_{T,\mu}
  = \frac{\Cov(\hat A, \hat B)}{k_BT} ,
  \qquad
  \frac{\partial\langle\hat B\rangle}{\partial T}\bigg|_{\mu}
  = \frac{\Cov(\hat{\mathcal{H}}, \hat B)}{k_BT^{2}} ,
\end{equation}
the second because $ \partial_Te^{-\beta \xi_a} = (\xi_a/k_BT^{2})\,e^{-\beta \xi_a} $: a change
of temperature acts as a field whose charge on the state $ a $ is $ \xi_a/T $. Every
static response among conserved quantities is a covariance, exactly:
$ (\partial N/\partial\mu)_T = \Var(\hat N)/k_BT $ ($ \hat A = \hat B = \hat N $, $ f = \mu $);
the spin susceptibility $ \Var(\hat M)/Vk_BT $ whenever the total moment $ \hat M $ is
conserved; $ C_\mu $ itself ($ \hat B = \hat{\mathcal{H}} $); and the mixed response
$ (\partial N/\partial T)_\mu = \Cov(\hat N, \hat{\mathcal{H}})/k_BT^{2} $, needed for the
conversion to fixed $ N $ (Appendix~\ref{app:cmu}).

If $ \hat A $ is \emph{not} conserved, the eigenvectors move with $ f $, and the
derivative of $ e^{-\beta(\hat{\mathcal{H}} - f\hat A)} $ is given by Duhamel's formula,
$ \int_0^\beta d\tau\,e^{-(\beta-\tau)\hat{\mathcal{H}}}\hat A\,e^{-\tau\hat{\mathcal{H}}} $. Evaluating the trace
in the eigenbasis, the $ \tau $ integral is elementary and gives the static
\emph{Lehmann representation} of the isothermal susceptibility,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_mb_kubo}
  \chi_{AB} \equiv \frac{\partial\langle\hat B\rangle}{\partial f}\bigg|_{f=0}
  = \sum_{a,b}A_{ab}B_{ba}\,w_{ab} - \beta\langle\hat A\rangle\langle\hat B\rangle ,
  \qquad
  w_{ab} = \frac{p_b - p_a}{\xi_a - \xi_b} ,
\end{equation}
with $ A_{ab} = \langle a|\hat A|b\rangle $ and $ w_{ab} \equiv \beta p_a $ when $ \xi_a = \xi_b $ (the
limit of the quotient). For conserved $ \hat A $ only $ a = b $ survives and
Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_mb_response} is recovered. The weight $ w_{ab} > 0 $ is the
many-body filter for responses: it acts on \emph{pairs} of eigenstates connected by
the perturbation. Writing $ y = \beta(\xi_a - \xi_b) $, $ w_{ab} = \beta p_b(1 - e^{-y})/y $
while $ \tfrac12\beta(p_a + p_b) = \tfrac12\beta p_b(1 + e^{-y}) $, and
$ (1 - e^{-y})/y \leq \tfrac12(1 + e^{-y}) $ is the inequality $ \tanh(y/2) \leq y/2 $.
Hence, with $ \delta\hat A = \hat A - \langle\hat A\rangle $ and
$ \sum_{ab}|A_{ab}|^{2}p_a = \langle\hat A^{2}\rangle $,
\[
  0 \;\leq\; \chi_{AA} = \sum_{a,b}\big|\langle a|\delta\hat A|b\rangle\big|^{2}\,w_{ab}
  \;\leq\; \frac{\Var(\hat A)}{k_BT} ,
\]
with equality on the right if and only if $ \hat A $ connects only states of equal
$ \xi_a $, i.e.\ is conserved. A conserved quantity responds exactly as much as it
fluctuates; a non-conserved one responds less, because part of its fluctuation
is carried by transitions between states of different energy, which a static field
cannot exploit fully.

\paragraph{Exact limits.} Three statements follow from Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_mb} with no
assumption on $ \hat H $.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Low temperature.} Let $ \xi_0 $ be the lowest eigenvalue of $ \hat{\mathcal{H}} $, with
  degeneracy $ d_0 $. As $ T \to 0 $, $ p_a \to 1/d_0 $ on the ground manifold, and
  $ S \to k_B\ln d_0 $. If the next eigenvalue is $ \xi_0 + \Delta $, with degeneracy $ d_1 $,
  then $ \Xi = e^{-\beta \xi_0}\big[d_0 + d_1e^{-\beta\Delta} + \dots\big] $ and, to leading
  order in $ q = (d_1/d_0)\,e^{-\beta\Delta} $, $ \Var(\hat{\mathcal{H}}) = \Delta^{2}q $:
  \[
    C_\mu \;\simeq\; k_B\left(\frac{\Delta}{k_BT}\right)^{2}\frac{d_1}{d_0}\,e^{-\Delta/k_BT} .
  \]
  The heat capacity of a gapped system has an ``activated'' form, with the many-body gap of
  $ \hat{\mathcal{H}} $; at fixed $ \mu $ this includes the costs $ E_{N\pm1} - E_N \mp \mu $ of
  adding or removing a particle. (In a macroscopic system $ d_1 $ grows with $ V $ and
  the expansion holds while $ q \ll 1 $, i.e.\ while excitations are dilute.)
\item \emph{High temperature.} If the Fock space has finite dimension $ D $ (fermions
  on a lattice), write $ \langle\cdot\rangle_\infty = \Tr(\cdot)/D $ for the
  infinite-temperature average. The cumulant expansion of
  $ \ln\Xi = \ln D + \ln\langle e^{-\beta\hat{\mathcal{H}}}\rangle_\infty $ gives
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_mb_hiT}
    \ln\Xi = \ln D - \beta\langle\hat{\mathcal{H}}\rangle_\infty + \tfrac12\beta^{2}\Var_\infty(\hat{\mathcal{H}}) + O(\beta^{3}) ,
    \qquad
    C_\mu = \frac{\Var_\infty(\hat{\mathcal{H}})}{k_BT^{2}} + O(T^{-3}) ,
  \end{equation}
  so $ S \to k_B\ln D $ and $ C_\mu $ decays as $ T^{-2} $ with a coefficient fixed by the
  spread of the whole spectrum.
\item \emph{Sum rule.} Since $ C_\mu = T(\partial S/\partial T)_\mu $,
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_mb_sumrule}
    \int_0^{\infty}\! C_\mu(T)\,\frac{dT}{T} = S(\infty) - S(0) = k_B\ln\frac{D}{d_0} :
  \end{equation}
  the area under $ C_\mu/T $ counts the eigenstates, logarithmically.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{The thermodynamic limit: a filter on an energy shell.} For a macroscopic
system $ g $ is dominated by an extensive exponent, $ g(\xi) = e^{S_{\rm mc}(\xi)/k_B} $ up to
subextensive factors, with $ S_{\rm mc} $ the microcanonical entropy of $ \hat{\mathcal{H}} $. The
weight
\[
  P_T(\xi) \;\propto\; \exp\!\Big[\big(S_{\rm mc}(\xi) - \xi/T\big)\big/k_B\Big]
\]
is then sharply peaked where $ S'_{\rm mc}(\xi) = 1/T $, i.e.\ at the thermal value
$ \langle\hat{\mathcal{H}}\rangle = U - \mu N $, and, by the steepest-descent argument of
Section~\ref{sec:equivalence}, Gaussian with variance $ k_BT^{2}C_\mu \propto V $: its
relative width is $ O(V^{-1/2}) $. The many-body filter therefore does not select
levels near $ \mu $, nor levels near the ground state; it selects a thin
\emph{shell} of many-body states around the thermal energy, and the observables
measure the density and the curvature of the spectrum in that shell. The temperature
does not rescale the shape of the weight, as it will for single-particle levels; it
tilts $ \ln g $ by $ -\xi/T $ and so moves the shell.

\paragraph{Correlations.} The variance form says at once what a divergent response
requires. A response per unit volume can diverge only if the variance of an
extensive quantity grows \emph{faster} than the volume. Writing the extensive
quantity as a sum of local densities, $ \hat X = \sum_i\hat x_i $,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_corr}
  \frac{\Var(\hat X)}{V} = \frac{1}{V}\sum_i\Var(\hat x_i)
  + \frac{1}{V}\sum_{i\neq j}\Cov(\hat x_i, \hat x_j)
  \;\simeq\; \sum_{\mathbf{r}} G_x(\mathbf{r}) ,
\end{equation}
with $ G_x(\mathbf{r}) = \Cov(\hat x_0, \hat x_{\mathbf{r}}) $ the connected
correlation function. The first, ``diagonal'' sum is always finite per unit volume
when the local Hilbert space is finite; the covariances --- the \emph{correlations}
between fluctuations at different places --- are what can grow. With correlations of
range $ \xi $ the sum runs effectively over a correlation volume, and if
$ G(r) \sim r^{-(d-2+\eta)} $ up to $ r \sim \xi $ it gives
$ \chi \sim \xi^{2-\eta} \sim |t|^{-\nu(2-\eta)} $, which is Fisher's relation
$ \gamma = \nu(2 - \eta) $. A critical point is thus the place where the fluctuations
of macroscopically many degrees of freedom become correlated, so that they no
longer add as independent contributions.


\subsection{The low-energy density of states fixes the power laws}
\label{sec:filt_lowenergy}

\paragraph{Power laws in $T$ are power laws in the spectrum.} Suppose that,
per unit volume, the heat capacity at fixed $\mu$ behaves as $c_\mu=aT^{r+1}$
at low temperature, with $s(0)=0$. Then $s=aT^{r+1}/(r+1)$ by
$c_\mu=T\,\partial s/\partial T$, and the excitation energy per unit volume is
$k\equiv(\langle\hat{\mathcal H}\rangle-\xi_0)/V=\int_0^Tc_\mu\,dT'=aT^{r+2}/(r+2)$.
Eliminating $T$,
\[
  s(k)=\frac{a}{r+1}\left(\frac{(r+2)\,k}{a}\right)^{(r+1)/(r+2)} ,
\]
and since $k_B\ln g=S_{\rm mc}=Vs$ in the thermodynamic limit, the many-body
density of states grows above the ground state as
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_stretched}
  \boxed{\;\ln g(\xi_0+Vk)\;\simeq\;\frac{V}{k_B}\,s(k)\;\propto\;V\,k^{(r+1)/(r+2)}\;}
\end{equation}
A power-law heat capacity is equivalent to a stretched-exponential growth of
the many-body level density, with an exponent between $0$ and $1$. The
statement is exact for any Hamiltonian: it uses only the Laplace-transform
identity and the steepest-descent evaluation of the previous subsection. Two
instances: for a metal, $c=\gamma T$ gives $r=0$ and $g\propto e^{2\sqrt{\tilde aE}}$
at excitation energy $E$, Bethe's formula for the level density of a heavy
nucleus, which is the many-body statement of the Sommerfeld law; for Debye
phonons, $r=2$ gives $\ln g\propto V^{1/4}E^{3/4}$. In the additive case (non-interacting)
the exponent $r$ is that of the single-particle density of states near $\mu$,
$\rho(\varepsilon)\sim|\varepsilon-\mu|^{r}$ (Appendix~\ref{app:quadratic}),
but Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_stretched} does not need additivity.

\paragraph{Scaling forms come from a scale-free spectrum.} The scaling form
Eq.~\eqref{eq:scaling} was postulated in Section~\ref{sec:critical}; here its
mechanism can be seen in the simplest setting. Let the relevant density of
states be scale-free about some energy $\varepsilon_0$,
$\rho=A|\varepsilon-\varepsilon_0|^{r}$ (a band edge, a Dirac point, a van Hove
singularity), and let $\mu$ be detuned from it by $\delta\mu=\mu-\varepsilon_0$.
For independent particles the grand potential density is
$\omega=k_BT\int d\varepsilon\,\rho(\varepsilon)\,\varphi\big((\varepsilon-\mu)/k_BT\big)$
with $\varphi$ a fixed function of its argument (Appendix~\ref{app:quadratic},
Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_Omega}). Substituting $\varepsilon-\varepsilon_0=k_BT\,y$,
the temperature comes out of the integral entirely,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_scalingform}
  \omega_{\rm sing}(T,\delta\mu)=A\,(k_BT)^{r+2}\,\Phi\!\left(\frac{\delta\mu}{k_BT}\right),
  \qquad
  \Phi(z)=\int dy\;|y|^{r}\,\varphi(y-z) ,
\end{equation}
up to analytic terms. This is exactly the generalised homogeneous form of
Section~\ref{sec:critical}, with $T$ in the role of $t$, $\delta\mu$ in the
role of $h$, $2-\alpha\to r+2$ and gap exponent $\Delta=1$. Every derivative
inherits a power law: $c\sim T^{r+1}$, the excess density $\sim T^{r+1}\Phi'$,
the compressibility $\sim T^{r}\Phi''$. At $T=0$ the same form gives
$\omega_{\rm sing}\propto|\delta\mu|^{r+2}$ on one side of $\varepsilon_0$: a
genuine thermodynamic singularity as $\mu$ crosses a band edge, the Lifshitz
transition, of order $r+2$ in Ehrenfest's sense ($5/2$ for a three-dimensional
band edge, $r=\tfrac12$).

The lesson is general: \emph{power laws in thermodynamics are the image of a
spectrum with no intrinsic scale}, because the Boltzmann weight itself has no
scale other than $k_BT$ (it is unchanged when $\xi$ and $k_BT$ are rescaled
together), and a scale-free density of states then leaves $T$ nowhere to
enter except as an overall power. At a genuine critical point the scale-free
object is not a single-particle density of states but the many-body density
of states of the collective fluctuations, or equivalently the correlation
function of Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_corr}, and the exponents $\alpha$, $\Delta$
are no longer the simple numbers $r+2$ and $1$; but the mechanism by which a
missing scale is turned into exponents is the same, and it is why the scaling
hypothesis is more than a convenient fit.

\paragraph{What $c(T)$ determines.} Given $C_\mu$ at all temperatures, the
entropy and the excitation energy follow by integration,
$S(T)=S(0)+\int_0^TC_\mu\,dT'/T'$ and
$\langle\hat{\mathcal H}\rangle(T)=\xi_0+\int_0^TC_\mu\,dT'$, and with them
$\ln\Xi=S/k_B-\beta\langle\hat{\mathcal H}\rangle$. Hence
\[
  e^{\beta\xi_0}\,\Xi(\beta)=\int_0^\infty\!d\epsilon\;g(\xi_0+\epsilon)\,e^{-\beta\epsilon}
\]
is known for all $\beta>0$, up to the factor $e^{S(0)/k_B}=d_0$. A Laplace
transform known on the whole half-line determines its original uniquely
(Lerch's theorem), so the heat capacity fixes the many-body density of states
above the ground state exactly, up to the ground-state degeneracy, with no
assumption on $\hat H$. The inversion is severely ill-posed in practice: the
kernel $e^{-\beta\epsilon}$ smooths $g$ over a window of width $k_BT$, so
structure in $g$ narrower than $k_BT$ at the lowest temperature measured is
beyond the reach of thermodynamics and needs a spectroscopy. What survives
robustly is the low-temperature asymptotic series, each term of which fixes
one term in the low-energy expansion of $g$. The additive case, where $c(T)$
can be inverted for the single-particle density of states, is treated in
Appendix~\ref{app:quadratic}.

\subsection{From many-body levels to single-particle levels}
\label{sec:filt_additive}

The results above are exact but involve the many-body spectrum, with a number of
levels exponential in $ V $. They reduce to statements about single-particle levels
under one condition, \emph{additivity}: a complete set of single-particle orbitals
$ \lambda $ with energies $ \varepsilon_\lambda $ such that every eigenstate $ |a\rangle $ is a
Fock state of occupations $ \{n_\lambda\} $ and
\[
  E_a = \sum_\lambda \varepsilon_\lambda\,n_\lambda , \qquad N_a = \sum_\lambda n_\lambda .
\]
This holds exactly when $ \hat H $ is quadratic in creation and annihilation
operators, since then $ \hat H = \sum_\lambda\varepsilon_\lambda\hat n_\lambda $ in the
basis that diagonalises the one-body matrix; and conversely an operator that acts
as $ \sum_\lambda\varepsilon_\lambda n_\lambda $ on every Fock state \emph{is}
$ \sum_\lambda\varepsilon_\lambda\hat n_\lambda $, a quadratic operator.


\paragraph{Without additivity.} The Fock states of any complete set of orbitals
form a basis of the Fock space, so every eigenstate $|a\rangle$ of an
interacting $\hat H$ can be expanded in them. What fails for a generic
interacting $\hat H$ is that the expansion has a single term: each $|a\rangle$
is a superposition of many Fock states, and no choice of orbitals makes every
eigenstate a single one. Even where the eigenstates are Fock states, the
energies need not be additive: for one orbital with spin and an on-site
repulsion $U$ (the Hubbard atom), the eigenstates $|0\rangle$,
$|{\uparrow}\rangle$, $|{\downarrow}\rangle$, $|{\uparrow\downarrow}\rangle$
have $\xi_a=0,\ \xi,\ \xi,\ 2\xi+U$ with $\xi=\varepsilon-\mu$, and
$\Xi=1+2e^{-\beta\xi}+e^{-\beta(2\xi+U)}$ equals $(1+e^{-\beta\xi})^{2}$ only
for $U=0$. The addition energies $\xi_b-\xi_a$ then belong to \emph{pairs} of
many-body states and not to single orbitals, and the thermodynamics is not a
functional of the single-particle spectral function alone: the entropy, for
instance, requires the full $g(\xi)$. The single-particle filters of
Appendix~\ref{app:quadratic} therefore hold exactly for quadratic
Hamiltonians, and for interacting systems to the extent that the low-lying
many-body spectrum is additive: for the quasiparticles of a Landau Fermi
liquid at low temperature, and for self-consistent (mean-field) theories,
which are additive at each temperature with a temperature-dependent spectrum
(Appendix~\ref{sec:mfgeneric}).

\paragraph{What is exact and what is additive.}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{p{0.45\linewidth}p{0.45\linewidth}}
\toprule
Exact, for any $ \hat H $ (eigenbasis) & Additive spectrum only (Appendix~\ref{app:quadratic}) \\
\midrule
$ C_\mu = \Var(\hat{\mathcal{H}})/k_BT^{2} \geq 0 $, Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_mb} &
Fermi and Bose windows, Eqs.~\eqref{eq:filt_window} ff. \\
responses $ = $ covariances, Eqs.~\eqref{eq:filt_mb_response}, \eqref{eq:filt_mb_kubo} &
normalised weights and the delta limit, Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_kernels} \\
activated $ C_\mu $ of a gapped system; $ S(0) = k_B\ln d_0 $ &
Sommerfeld law, Wilson ratio $ R_W = 1 $ \\
high-$ T $ tail, Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_mb_hiT}; sum rule, Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_mb_sumrule} &
$ c \sim T^{r+1} $ from $ \rho \sim |\varepsilon - \mu|^{r} $; $ s/c = 1/(r+1) $ \\
divergent response requires correlations; stretched-exponential $g$, Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_stretched} &
per-orbital bound on $ c_\mu $, Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_bound} \\
inversion of $ c(T) $ for $ g(\xi) $ &
inversion of $ c(T) $ for $ \rho(\varepsilon) $ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}


\section{Microscopic foundations: heat, information and the second law}
\label{sec:foundations}

The preceding sections take the equilibrium ensembles as given. This section asks
where they come from. The starting point is a negative result. The microscopic
dynamics of a closed system --- a density matrix $ \rho $ evolving under a Hamiltonian
by the von~Neumann equation --- is reversible, and it conserves the Gibbs entropy
exactly. The entropy therefore cannot be obtained from the microscopic equations of
motion, however they are manipulated.

What enters instead is the part of the Hamiltonian that no description contains. The
Hamiltonian of a real body is
\[
  \hat H_{\rm tot} = \hat H + \hat H' ,
\]
where $ \hat H $ is the model, the part that is known and treated microscopically, and
$ \hat H' $ comprises everything else: the degrees of freedom of the body itself that the
model does not resolve --- strongly interacting states on energy and length scales
beyond its ultraviolet or infrared cutoffs --- and the couplings to the walls, to the
radiation field, to the molecules of a surrounding fluid, and to the contacts through
which particles enter and leave. The effects of $ \hat H' $ are not small, but $ \hat H' $ cannot be written down
in detail, either because it is too complex or because its mechanism is simply not
known. The ideal gas is the simplest instance: its model Hamiltonian
$ \sum_ip_i^{2}/2m $ contains no collisions, yet without collisions the gas would
never equilibrate. Boltzmann's kinetic theory restores them through the
\emph{Stosszahlansatz}, the assumption that the velocities of two molecules about
to collide are uncorrelated; this single assumption, not the mechanics of the
collisions, is what produces the $ H $-theorem and the approach to the Maxwell
distribution. Equilibrium statistical mechanics goes one step further and keeps
nothing of the collisions at all: their only trace is that the gas has a
temperature and an entropy. A Brownian particle is the other paradigm: it is
kicked by the molecules of the fluid, and nothing about the individual kicks is
known. Statistical mechanics does not
attempt to describe $ \hat H' $. It replaces it by \emph{devices} with very few
parameters. The entropy, with its conjugate $ T $, is the device for the unknown
exchange of energy; the term $ -\mu\hat N $ added to the Hamiltonian is the device for
the unknown mechanism of creation/annihilation or exchange of particles.

\subsection{Heat and work}
\label{sec:found_heat}

Heat and work are not two terms into which $ dU $ can be split by inspecting the state
of the system; they are distinguished by the \emph{channel} through which the energy
arrives. Let the Hamiltonian $ \hat H(\lambda) $ of the system depend on parameters
$ \lambda $ that the experimenter controls (volume, field, strain), let the system be
coupled, if at all, to a bath with Hamiltonian $ \hat H_{\rm B} $, and let
$ U = \Tr(\rho\hat H) $ with $ \rho $ the state of the system. System and bath together
are isolated except for the agent that moves $ \lambda $: their joint state evolves
unitarily under $ \hat H_{\rm tot} = \hat H(\lambda(t)) + \hat H_{\rm B} + \hat H_{\rm int} $,
whose only time dependence is through $ \lambda(t) $. Their energy therefore changes at
the rate
\[
  \frac{d}{dt}\Tr\big(\rho_{\rm tot}\hat H_{\rm tot}\big)
  = \Tr\big(\rho_{\rm tot}\,\partial_t\hat H_{\rm tot}\big)
  = \sum_j\Big\langle\frac{\partial\hat H}{\partial\lambda_j}\Big\rangle\dot\lambda_j ,
\]
the term $ \Tr(\hat H_{\rm tot}\,\dot\rho_{\rm tot}) = -(i/\hbar)\Tr\big(\hat H_{\rm tot}[\hat H_{\rm tot}, \rho_{\rm tot}]\big) $
vanishing by cyclicity of the trace. This is the \emph{work}, the energy delivered by
the agent, $ \dbar W = \sum_j\langle\partial\hat H/\partial\lambda_j\rangle\,d\lambda_j $; for
$ \lambda = V $ in equilibrium, $ \langle\partial\hat H/\partial V\rangle = -P $ and
$ \dbar W = -P\,dV $. The \emph{heat} is the energy the system receives from the bath,
$ \dbar Q = -d\langle\hat H_{\rm B}\rangle $ when the coupling energy
$ \langle\hat H_{\rm int}\rangle $ is negligible (weak coupling), and the first law
$ dU = \dbar W + \dbar Q $ is the conservation of the total energy. Without a bath there is
no heat at all: a thermally isolated system receives energy only as work. In the
language of this section, work is the energy that arrives through the known part of
the Hamiltonian, the controlled parameters $ \lambda $, and heat is the energy that
arrives through the part that is not known in detail,
$ \hat H' = \hat H_{\rm B} + \hat H_{\rm int} $, especially, $ \hat H_{\rm int} $.

Neither $ \dbar W $ nor $ \dbar Q $ is the differential of a function of the state
(Appendix~\ref{app:exact}); along a quasi-static sequence of Gibbs
states,\footnote{\label{fn:gibbsstate}\textbf{Gibbs state.} A density matrix of
the form $ \rho = e^{-\beta\hat K}/\Tr e^{-\beta\hat K} $ for some Hermitian
$ \hat K $: the canonical state for $ \hat K = \hat H $, the grand canonical state
for $ \hat K = \hat H - \mu\hat N $, and so on. It is the equilibrium state of
every ensemble of Section~\ref{sec:ensembles}.} Section~\ref{sec:partition}
shows that $ \dbar Q = T\,dS $.

\subsection{Reversibility of the microscopic dynamics}
\label{sec:found_unitary}

Consider an isolated system, $ i\hbar\,\partial_t\rho = [\hat H, \rho] $, so that
$ \rho(t) = \hat U\rho(0)\hat U^\dagger $ with $ \hat U = e^{-i\hat Ht/\hbar} $. The evolution
is reversible in two senses. It is \emph{invertible},
$ \rho(0) = \hat U^\dagger\rho(t)\,\hat U $: the state at any time determines the state at
every other time, so no information about the initial state is ever lost. And if
$ \hat H $ commutes with the antiunitary time-reversal operator $ \Theta $, then with
$ \rho(t) $ the time-reversed history $ \Theta\rho(-t)\Theta^{-1} $ is also a solution, so
that every process can, in principle, run backwards (Loschmidt's objection to
Boltzmann, 1876). Two exact statements follow.

\emph{The Gibbs entropy is constant.} $ \rho(t) $ and $ \rho(0) $ are unitarily similar,
so they have the same eigenvalues, and $ S[\rho(t)] = S[\rho(0)] $; more generally
$ \Tr f(\rho) $ is conserved for every function $ f $. The Gibbs entropy of an
isolated system never increases.

\emph{The energy distribution is constant.} Let $ P_t(E) = \Tr[\rho(t)\,\delta(\hat H - E)] $.
Since $ \hat U $ commutes with every function of $ \hat H $, cyclicity of the trace gives
\[
  P_t(E) = \Tr\big[\rho(0)\,\hat U^\dagger\delta(\hat H - E)\,\hat U\big]
  = \Tr\big[\rho(0)\,\delta(\hat H - E)\big] = P_0(E) .
\]
All moments of $ \hat H $ are constants of motion.

Both statements hold for \emph{any} Hamiltonian, and in particular for the full
$ \hat H_{\rm tot} $ of system and surroundings. No functional of $ \rho $ determined by
a completely known microscopic dynamics can therefore grow monotonically: a system
described exactly has no second law. The macroscopic irreversibility
(e.g., a gas filling a vessel, heat flowing from hot to cold, a stirred fluid warming up)
must come from what the description leaves out, i.e. from $ \hat H' $.

\subsection{The unknown part of the Hamiltonian}
\label{sec:found_second}

\paragraph{No body is isolated.} The many-body levels of a macroscopic body are
spaced by about $ e^{-S/k_B} $ in natural units, i.e.\ by a quantity of order $ e^{-N} $.
Any coupling to the outside (e.g., a thermal photon scattered by the walls, the
vibrations of the container, the cosmic radiation, the gravitational field of distant masses)
has matrix elements between these levels that exceed their spacing by a factor
exponential in $ N $. The estimate is a sum rule. Let $ \hat H' $ be a coupling of
energy scale $ \epsilon $, as small as one likes but independent of $ N $, and let
$ |a\rangle $ be an eigenstate of $ \hat H $. The row sum
$ \sum_b|H'_{ab}|^{2} = \langle a|\hat H'^{2}|a\rangle \sim \epsilon^{2} $ is shared among
the states $ b $ that $ \hat H' $ connects to $ a $, of which there are of order
$ e^{S/k_B} $ within the energy range that a local coupling can reach, so a
typical matrix element is $ |H'_{ab}| \sim \epsilon\,e^{-S/2k_B} $, while the level
spacing is $ \sim \epsilon_0 e^{-S/k_B} $ with $ \epsilon_0 $ a microscopic energy. Their
ratio, $ (\epsilon/\epsilon_0)\,e^{S/2k_B} $, is astronomically large for any
$ \epsilon $ that is not itself exponentially small in $ N $. Such a coupling mixes
the eigenstates of the model Hamiltonian $ \hat H $ over an energy range that contains
astronomically many of them, and the resulting state depends on details of $ \hat H' $
that are neither known nor controlled. The eigenstates of an ``isolated'' macroscopic
body are therefore an idealisation: they are sharp only on a scale that the real body
never respects.

The sensitivity is amplified by chaos. Classically, nearby trajectories of a chaotic
system separate exponentially, so that an initially negligible perturbation soon
dominates; Berry (1978) estimated that neglecting the gravitational pull of a single
electron at the edge of the observable universe makes the trajectory of a molecule in
a gas unpredictable after about fifty collisions. The quantum counterpart is the
\emph{Loschmidt echo}
\[
  M(t) = \big|\langle\psi|\,e^{i\hat Ht/\hbar}\,e^{-i(\hat H + \hat H')t/\hbar}\,|\psi\rangle\big|^{2} ,
\]
the fidelity with which a state evolved forward under the true Hamiltonian
$ \hat H + \hat H' $ is returned to its start by reversing only the model Hamiltonian
$ \hat H $. For systems whose classical limit is chaotic, $ M(t) $ decays exponentially,
and in a broad range of perturbation strengths at a rate set by the Lyapunov exponent
of the unperturbed dynamics and independent of $ \hat H' $ (Jalabert and Pastawski,
2001). A coupling too weak to be measured thus suffices to make the microscopic
history unrepeatable and irreversible in practice.

\paragraph{Brownian motion: the random kicks as a device.} A colloidal particle of
mass $ m $ moves in an external potential $ \mathcal{V}(x) $ inside a fluid. The known
Hamiltonian is $ p^{2}/2m + \mathcal{V}(x) $; the unknown part is the interaction with some
$ 10^{20} $ fluid molecules, whose positions and velocities nobody knows. Langevin's
equation (1908) replaces that part by two terms,
\[
  m\ddot x = -\mathcal{V}'(x) - \gamma\dot x + \zeta(t) , \qquad
  \langle\zeta(t)\rangle = 0 , \qquad
  \langle\zeta(t)\,\zeta(t')\rangle = 2\gamma k_BT\,\delta(t - t') :
\]
a friction $ -\gamma\dot x $, the average effect of the kicks, and a random force
$ \zeta(t) $, their fluctuation. The two are not independent. The strength of the noise
must be $ 2\gamma k_BT $ for a fluid at temperature $ T $ to bring the kinetic energy of
the particle to its equipartition value $ k_BT/2 $; this is the
fluctuation--dissipation relation, in Einstein's form (1905) $ D = k_BT/\gamma $ for
the diffusion constant. The whole Hamiltonian of the fluid and of its coupling to the
particle has been replaced by two numbers, $ \gamma $ and $ T $.

On time scales long compared with $ m/\gamma $ the inertia can be dropped,
$ \gamma\dot x = -\mathcal{V}'(x) + \zeta(t) $, and the probability density $ P(x, t) $ of
the particle obeys the Smoluchowski equation
\[
  \frac{\partial P}{\partial t}
  = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\bigg[\frac{1}{\gamma}\Big(\mathcal{V}'P + k_BT\,\frac{\partial P}{\partial x}\Big)\bigg]
  = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\bigg[\frac{P}{\gamma}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\big(\mathcal{V} + k_BT\ln P\big)\bigg] ,
\]
the drift coming from the force and the diffusion from the noise. Define
\[
  F[P] \equiv \int\!dx\;P\,\big(\mathcal{V} + k_BT\ln P\big)
  = \langle\mathcal{V}\rangle - T\,S[P] , \qquad
  S[P] = -k_B\!\int\!dx\;P\ln P .
\]
With $ \psi \equiv \mathcal{V} + k_BT\ln P $, the functional derivative is
$ \delta F/\delta P = \psi + k_BT $, and an integration by parts (the boundary terms
vanish for a confining potential) gives
\[
  \frac{dF}{dt} = \int\!dx\;\frac{\partial P}{\partial t}\,(\psi + k_BT)
  = -\int\!dx\;\frac{P}{\gamma}\Big(\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial x}\Big)^{2} \;\leq\; 0 ,
\]
with equality only if $ \psi $ is constant, i.e.\ for $ P \propto e^{-\mathcal{V}/k_BT} $. (With
inertia kept, the same holds for the density in $ (x, p) $, with
$ \langle p^{2}/2m + \mathcal{V}\rangle $ in place of $ \langle\mathcal{V}\rangle $.)

This calculation shows exactly where the entropy comes from. The known force alone,
$ T \to 0 $, would minimise $ \langle\mathcal{V}\rangle $ and send the particle to the bottom
of the well. The noise alone would spread it without limit. The term $ -T\,S[P] $ in
the functional that the motion minimises is the entire imprint of the ``unknown kicks'':
it vanishes with them. The friction $ \gamma $ fixes how fast the minimum is reached,
not where it lies, and the equilibrium state retains $ T $ alone. The entropy enters
not from the known dynamics but as the representation of the unknown part.

\paragraph{The unknown part need not be external.} Nothing in the device requires the
kicks to come from outside the body, nor any external time-dependent drive. Every
model resolves the degrees of freedom of a body only within a window of length and
energy scales. Beyond its ultraviolet cutoff (short lengths, high energies) and its
infrared cutoff (long lengths, low energies) lie states that could be strongly
interacting and far too complex to be treated microscopically, and they are related to
$ \hat H' $. Their effect on the retained degrees of freedom is modelled, crudely, by a
stochastic force or potential, as the fluid was for the Brownian particle. The
conduction electrons of a metal damp a local magnetic moment and make its orientation
fluctuate (Korringa relaxation, Gilbert damping); the lattice is the bath of the
nuclear spins (the spin--lattice relaxation time $ T_1 $) and the phonons are a bath
for the conduction electrons.\footnote{\label{fn:morizwanzig}\textbf{Mori--Zwanzig
projection.} The exact form of this elimination is the projection-operator
formalism of Zwanzig (1960) and Mori (1965). Projecting the equations of motion
onto a chosen set of slow variables gives for them a \emph{generalised Langevin
equation}: a systematic (mean-field) force, a friction term with a memory kernel
built from the correlations of the eliminated fast variables, and a residual
force that is orthogonal to the slow variables and is treated as noise. The
memory kernel and the noise correlation are related by a fluctuation--dissipation
relation at the temperature of the bath, as $ \gamma $ and $ 2\gamma k_BT $ are in
Langevin's equation. Nothing is approximated in the derivation; the
approximation enters when the memory kernel is replaced by a constant friction
(Markov limit) and the orthogonal force by white noise.}
The short-wavelength fluctuations of an order parameter
$ \phi $, once eliminated, leave on the long-wavelength ones a damping and a noise,
\[
  \frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t} = -\Gamma\,\frac{\delta F[\phi]}{\delta\phi} + \zeta ,
  \qquad
  \langle\zeta(\mathbf{r}, t)\,\zeta(\mathbf{r}', t')\rangle
  = 2\Gamma k_BT\,\delta(\mathbf{r} - \mathbf{r}')\,\delta(t - t') ,
\]
the field-theoretic Langevin equation (model~A of Hohenberg and Halperin, 1977), whose
stationary distribution is $ \propto e^{-F[\phi]/k_BT} $ by the same calculation as above.
In this example the ``known Hamiltonian'' $ F[\phi] $ of the retained modes is itself a
coarse-grained free energy, of Landau--Ginzburg form, which already contains the
entropy of the eliminated modes: the device is applied scale by scale. In all these
cases the total Hamiltonian may be time-independent and the body isolated; seen from
the retained variables, the eliminated ones act as a random force all the same.
Isolation from the outside therefore does not exempt a macroscopic body from the
second law: its own unresolved degrees of freedom suffice.

\paragraph{Averaging over the unknown Hamiltonian.} The same mechanism, stated for a
quantum system without any model of the noise, is an average over what is not known.
Let the realisations of $ \hat H' $ that cannot be excluded be labelled by $ k $, with
weights $ q_k > 0 $, $ \sum_kq_k = 1 $, and let each generate, together with $ \hat H $,
the unitary $ \hat U_k $ over some interval of time. Each $ \hat U_k $ alone is reversible
and conserves the entropy, but not knowing which one acted, the description after the
interval is the mixture
\[
  \rho' = \sum_k q_k\,\rho_k , \qquad \rho_k \equiv \hat U_k\rho\,\hat U_k^\dagger .
\]
Its entropy cannot be lower than that of $ \rho $. Applying Klein's inequality
(Appendix~\ref{app:gibbsvar}) to each $ \rho_k $ against $ \rho' $ (the support of
$ \rho_k $ lies in that of $ \rho' $, since $ \rho' \geq q_k\rho_k $) and using
$ \sum_kq_k\rho_k = \rho' $,
\[
  0 \;\leq\; \sum_kq_k\,D(\rho_k\,\|\,\rho')
  = \sum_kq_k\Tr(\rho_k\ln\rho_k) - \Tr(\rho'\ln\rho')
  = \frac{1}{k_B}\Big(S[\rho'] - \sum_kq_k\,S[\rho_k]\Big) ,
\]
and $ S[\rho_k] = S[\rho] $ for every $ k $, so that
\[
  S[\rho'] \;\geq\; S[\rho] ,
\]
with equality only if every $ \rho_k $ equals $ \rho' $, i.e.\ only if all the
realisations of $ \hat H' $ that cannot be excluded leave the state the same. The
microscopic processes are reversible one by one; the entropy of the description grows
because it is not known which of them took place.

For a body that exchanges nothing with its surroundings, each $ \hat U_k $ conserves the
energy to within a macroscopically small width and maps an energy shell into itself.
If the unknown couplings are generic enough to connect every pair of states in the
shell, the only state on which all of them act identically is the uniform one,
proportional to the projector onto the shell, which commutes with every unitary
acting within it. Repeated averaging therefore drives the description to the
microcanonical state, and stops there, with $ S = k_B\ln W $ for $ W $ states in the
shell. The energy survives because $ \hat H' $ conserves it; everything else about the
microstate is erased.

\paragraph{The master equation.} The mechanism can be made explicit when $ \hat H' $ is
weak. Let $ |a\rangle $, $ E_a $ be the eigenstates of the model Hamiltonian $ \hat H $
and $ p_a = \langle a|\rho|a\rangle $ their occupations. Over a time $ \Delta t $, second-order
perturbation theory in $ \hat H' $, averaged over the unknown phases of the matrix
elements $ H'_{ab} = \langle a|\hat H'|b\rangle $ (which removes the interference terms
between different transitions and keeps $ \rho $ diagonal in $ |a\rangle $), gives Fermi's
golden rule,
\[
  \frac{dp_a}{dt} = \sum_b W_{ab}\,(p_b - p_a) , \qquad
  W_{ab} = \frac{2\pi}{\hbar}\,\overline{|H'_{ab}|^{2}}\;\delta_{\Delta t}(E_a - E_b) ,
\]
with $ W_{ab} $ the rate from $ b $ to $ a $ and $ \delta_{\Delta t} $ a peak of width
$ \hbar/\Delta t $. Hermiticity of $ \hat H' $ makes the rates symmetric,
$ W_{ab} = W_{ba} \geq 0 $. The entropy $ S = -k_B\sum_ap_a\ln p_a $ then obeys
Boltzmann's $ H $-theorem in Pauli's form (1928). Since $ \sum_a\dot p_a = 0 $,
\[
  \begin{aligned}
  \frac{dS}{dt} &= -k_B\sum_a\dot p_a\ln p_a
  = -k_B\sum_{a,b}W_{ab}\,(p_b - p_a)\ln p_a \\
  &= \frac{k_B}{2}\sum_{a,b}W_{ab}\,(p_b - p_a)(\ln p_b - \ln p_a) \;\geq\; 0 ,
  \end{aligned}
\]
the last form following by exchanging $ a \leftrightarrow b $ and averaging, with
$ W_{ab} = W_{ba} $. Each term is non-negative because the logarithm is increasing, and
the sum vanishes only if $ p_a = p_b $ for every pair connected by a transition, i.e.\
for the uniform distribution on the energy shell. The irreversibility enters at one
identifiable step: the phases of $ \rho $ and of $ \hat H' $ are discarded at every
interval $ \Delta t $ (Pauli's repeated random-phase assumption).\footnote{\label{fn:pauli}%
\textbf{Pauli's random-phase assumption.} In deriving the master equation (1928)
Pauli assumed that at the start of every interval $ \Delta t $ the off-diagonal
elements of $ \rho $ in the eigenbasis of $ \hat H $ have random phases, so that
their contribution averages to zero and only the occupations $ p_a $ evolve; the
assumption is \emph{repeated} at each step because unitary evolution would
otherwise rebuild the phases. It is the quantum form of Boltzmann's
Stosszahlansatz. The \emph{random-phase approximation} of many-body perturbation
theory (Bohm and Pines, 1953; the summation of ring diagrams for the dielectric
function) shares the name and the idea of discarding terms whose phases are
uncorrelated, but it is an approximation to the equilibrium correlation
functions of an interacting system and not a statement about irreversibility;
the two should not be confused.} Van~Hove (1955)
showed that this step becomes exact for a macroscopic system in the weak-coupling
limit, $ \hat H' \to 0 $ and $ t \to \infty $ with $ \|\hat H'\|^{2}t $ fixed. With $ \hat H' $
known and the phases kept, the same calculation would give unitary evolution and a
constant entropy. The master equation is the evolution of the description, not of the
microstate.

\paragraph{In contact with a bath.} A bath is the extreme case of an unknown
$ \hat H' $. If each transition $ b \to a $ of the system takes the energy
$ E_a - E_b $ from a large bath at temperature $ T $, the rates are no longer symmetric:
the bath has fewer states at lower energy, by the factor
$ \Omega_{\rm B}(E_{\rm tot} - E_a)/\Omega_{\rm B}(E_{\rm tot} - E_b) = e^{-\beta(E_a - E_b)} $
(Section~\ref{sec:ensembles}), and the rates obey \emph{detailed balance},
$ W_{ab}\,p_b^{\rm eq} = W_{ba}\,p_a^{\rm eq} \equiv K_{ab} = K_{ba} $, with
$ p_a^{\rm eq} = e^{-\beta E_a}/Z $. The master equation becomes
$ \dot p_a = \sum_b(W_{ab}p_b - W_{ba}p_a) = \sum_bK_{ab}(x_b - x_a) $ with
$ x_a \equiv p_a/p_a^{\rm eq} $, and the relative entropy
$ D(p\,\|\,p^{\rm eq}) = \sum_ap_a\ln x_a $ obeys
\[
  \frac{dD}{dt} = \sum_a\dot p_a\ln x_a + \sum_a\dot p_a
  = -\frac{1}{2}\sum_{a,b}K_{ab}\,(x_b - x_a)(\ln x_b - \ln x_a) \;\leq\; 0 ,
\]
where the second sum vanished and the same exchange of $ a $ and $ b $ was used. Since
$ k_BT\,D(p\,\|\,p^{\rm eq}) = F[p] - F $ with $ F[p] = U[p] - TS[p] $
(Appendix~\ref{app:gibbsvar}), the free energy of the system decreases monotonically
to its equilibrium value, as it did for the Brownian particle. The bath enters only
through the number $ T $ in the ratio of the rates; all other details of
$ \hat H_{\rm B} $ and $ \hat H_{\rm int} $ are absorbed in the symmetric $ K_{ab} $, which, like
$ \gamma $, sets how fast the system relaxes but not where. The minimum principle of
Section~\ref{sec:minimum} appears here as the end point of the action of the unknown
part of the Hamiltonian.

\paragraph{Dephasing is not loss.} The model Hamiltonian $ \hat H $ by itself also
makes the state look more disordered, but reversibly. For non-degenerate energy
differences the matrix elements in the eigenbasis are
$ \langle a|\rho(t)|b\rangle = \rho_{ab}(0)\,e^{-i(E_a - E_b)t/\hbar} $. The off-diagonal elements
oscillate, and their time average vanishes,
$ \tau^{-1}\int_0^\tau e^{-i\omega t}dt \to 0 $ for $ \omega \neq 0 $; hence
\[
  \bar\rho \equiv \lim_{\tau\to\infty}\frac{1}{\tau}\int_0^\tau\!\rho(t)\,dt
  = \sum_a\rho_{aa}\,|a\rangle\langle a| ,
\]
the \emph{diagonal ensemble}. A measurement averaged over long times, or of a
few-body observable at a typical late time in a macroscopic system (whose level
spacings are so dense that the off-diagonal contributions add with effectively
random phases), sees $ \bar\rho $ rather than $ \rho(t) $. Because $ \ln\bar\rho $ is
diagonal in the eigenbasis, $ \Tr[\rho(0)\ln\bar\rho] = \sum_a\rho_{aa}\ln\rho_{aa} =
\Tr(\bar\rho\ln\bar\rho) $, and
\[
  S[\bar\rho] - S[\rho(0)] = k_B\,D\big(\rho(0)\,\|\,\bar\rho\big) \;\geq\; 0 ,
\]
with equality only if $ \rho(0) $ was already diagonal. This increase is only apparent.
The information is still there, in the phases of $ \rho_{ab} $, and an operation that
reverses the sign of $ \hat H $ recovers it. The nuclear spin echo (Hahn, 1950) does
exactly this: nuclear spins precessing in an inhomogeneous field dephase and their
net magnetisation disappears; a $ \pi $ pulse reverses the precession, and the
magnetisation reappears, although the inhomogeneity was never known in detail ---
only static. What the echo cannot recover decays with the time $ T_2 $, set by the
interactions among the spins and by fluctuating fields, which the pulse does not
reverse. The magic echo (Rhim, Pines and Waugh, 1971) reverses the dipolar
interaction among the spins as well, and still recovers the signal only partially;
the residual decay comes from the terms in the Hamiltonian that the reversal does not
control. Dephasing under the known Hamiltonian displaces information; the unknown
part of the Hamiltonian removes it beyond recovery.

\paragraph{Correlations with the environment.} The unknown $ \hat H' $ couples the
system to degrees of freedom that nobody monitors. Including those degrees of freedom
makes the composite closed again, and then the information is not destroyed but
transferred into correlations. Let a system $ A $ interact with an environment $ B $,
the composite evolving unitarily, and let $ \rho_A = \Tr_B\rho_{AB} $,
$ \rho_B = \Tr_A\rho_{AB} $ be the reduced states, which are all that measurements on one
part can access. The mutual information
\[
  I(A{:}B) \equiv S[\rho_A] + S[\rho_B] - S[\rho_{AB}] \;\geq\; 0
\]
is non-negative (subadditivity of the entropy, itself a case of Klein's inequality:
$ I = k_B D(\rho_{AB}\,\|\,\rho_A\otimes\rho_B) $). Since $ S[\rho_{AB}] $ is conserved,
$ S[\rho_A] + S[\rho_B] = S[\rho_{AB}] + I(A{:}B) $ grows by exactly the information
stored in the correlations, including entanglement, that the interaction has built
up. Recovering it would require a joint measurement on $ A $ and on every degree of
freedom of $ B $ that $ A $ has touched; once these include the walls and the radiation
field, the information is lost as surely as if it had been destroyed. For a small
system and a large environment more is true (canonical typicality: Goldstein,
Lebowitz, Tumulka and Zangh\`\i; Popescu, Short and Winter, 2006): for almost every
\emph{pure} state of $ A + B $ in a narrow energy shell, $ \rho_A $ is close to
$ e^{-\beta\hat H_A}/Z_A $, with $ \beta $ set by the shell. The canonical state of a
subsystem is what remains of its description once the environment is traced out.

\paragraph{Why the energy suffices: thermalisation.} The diagonal ensemble still
remembers every $ \rho_{aa} $, i.e.\ exponentially many conserved quantities, and it is
not the microcanonical state. That few-body observables nevertheless take their
microcanonical values, before $ \hat H' $ has had time to equalise the $ \rho_{aa} $, is
the content of the \emph{eigenstate thermalisation hypothesis} (Deutsch, 1991;
Srednicki, 1994): the diagonal matrix elements $ \langle a|\hat O|a\rangle $ of a
few-body observable are a smooth function $ O(E_a) $ of the energy, up to fluctuations
exponentially small in the system size. Then
$ \Tr(\bar\rho\hat O) = \sum_a\rho_{aa}O(E_a) \simeq O(U) $ whenever $ \rho_{aa} $ is
concentrated in an energy window narrow on the scale over which $ O $ varies, which
is the case for any physically preparable state of a macroscopic system; and $ O(U) $
is also the microcanonical value. The information carried by the $ \rho_{aa} $ beyond
the energy is real, but invisible to few-body observables. The hypothesis is a
property of the Hamiltonian, well supported numerically for generic (chaotic)
many-body systems. It fails for integrable systems,\footnote{\label{fn:integrable}\textbf{Integrable systems.} A
classical system with $ f $ degrees of freedom is integrable (in the sense of
Liouville) if it has $ f $ independent conserved quantities whose mutual Poisson
brackets vanish; its motion is then confined to $ f $-dimensional invariant tori and is
quasi-periodic, so it cannot explore the energy surface. In quantum mechanics there is
no single accepted definition. In practice a many-body system is called integrable if
it has an extensive set of local or quasi-local conserved operators that commute with
$ \hat H $ and with each other: the occupations $ \hat n_\lambda $ of a quadratic
Hamiltonian, or the hierarchies of interacting models solvable by the Bethe ansatz,
such as the Heisenberg (XXZ) chain, the Lieb--Liniger Bose gas and the one-dimensional
