\caption{Shapes of the two histograms. \textbf{(a)} At a continuous transition
$P(E)$ stays unimodal at all $T$, because $s(e)$ is strictly concave; at $T_c$ its
curvature vanishes at the single point $e_c$, which broadens the peak
($\Var(E)\sim L^{d+\alpha/\nu}$) without splitting it. \textbf{(b)} At a
first-order transition $s(e)$ is affine between $e_o$ and $e_d$; the interfacial
cost penalises the interior of that interval and leaves exactly two peaks, with a
dip of depth $2\sigma L^{d-1}$ in $\ln P$ --- the Lee--Kosterlitz barrier.
\textbf{(c)}--\textbf{(e)} $P(\phi)$ counts the degenerate minima of the
constrained free energy, i.e.\ the order-parameter manifold $G/H$. For Ising
($\mathbb{Z}_2$) that is two points; at $T_c$ the peaks merge into the universal
critical distribution (dashed). For the $q=3$ Potts model at a first-order $T_t$
it is $q$ ordered states plus the disordered one, $q+1$ maxima in the
two-component plane. For the XY model it is a continuous circle, with no discrete
peaks at all --- ``bimodal'' is a statement about $\mathbb{Z}_2$, not a general
rule.}
\label{fig:histograms}
\end{figure}


\section{Fisher renormalisation}
\label{app:fisher}

At the level of the potentials, equivalence survives a continuous transition: for
short-range interactions $s(e)$ remains strictly concave, there is no affine
segment, the Legendre transform stays invertible, and both ensembles yield the
same free energy. At the level of \emph{critical behaviour} it does not, and the
effect --- \emph{Fisher renormalisation} --- is the critical-point face of the
$C_{V,\mu}$ versus $C_{V,N}$ distinction of Section~\ref{sec:respdiffer}.

\paragraph{Mechanism.} Suppose the system carries a hidden constraint --- fixed
particle number, fixed alloy composition, fixed concentration of an annealed
component --- where the unconstrained version would let the conjugate field
float. Fixing the density forces the conjugate field to adjust as the temperature
is swept, and the adjustment is proportional to the singular energy density. The
reduced temperature actually felt by the critical degrees of freedom is then not
the experimental $t$ but $\tilde t$, with
\[
t=\tilde t-a\,u_{\rm sing}(\tilde t),
\qquad u_{\rm sing}\sim|\tilde t|^{1-\alpha},
\]
since $u=\partial f_{\rm sing}/\partial\tilde t$ and $f_{\rm sing}\sim|\tilde
t|^{2-\alpha}$.

\paragraph{Consequence.} If $\alpha>0$ then $1-\alpha<1$, so $|\tilde
t|^{1-\alpha}$ dominates $\tilde t$ itself as $\tilde t\to0$. Hence $t\approx
-a|\tilde t|^{1-\alpha}$, i.e.
\[
\boxed{\;|\tilde t|\sim|t|^{1/(1-\alpha)}\;}
\]
Every power law is re-expressed through this map. From $f_{\rm sing}\sim
|\tilde t|^{2-\alpha}\sim|t|^{(2-\alpha)/(1-\alpha)}$ and the definition
$f_{\rm sing}\sim|t|^{2-\alpha_c}$,
\[
\boxed{\;\alpha_c=\frac{-\alpha}{1-\alpha},\quad
\beta_c=\frac{\beta}{1-\alpha},\quad
\gamma_c=\frac{\gamma}{1-\alpha},\quad
\nu_c=\frac{\nu}{1-\alpha}\;}
\]
If $\alpha<0$ the correction is subleading, $t\approx\tilde t$, and there is no
renormalisation --- Fisher's criterion.

\paragraph{Reading the result.} Note the sign: $\alpha_c<0$ whenever $\alpha>0$,
so the constraint \emph{converts a divergence into a cusp}. For the
three-dimensional Ising class, $\alpha\simeq0.110$ gives
$\alpha_c\simeq-0.124$. This is exactly what the relation
$C_{V,N}=C_{V,\mu}-T(\partial N/\partial T)^2/(\partial N/\partial\mu)$ does at
criticality: the subtracted term diverges at the same rate as $C_{V,\mu}$, the
leading singularities cancel, and weaker behaviour is left. Physical
realisations include order--disorder transitions in binary alloys at fixed
composition, ${}^3$He--${}^4$He mixtures at fixed concentration, and systems with
annealed mobile impurities.


\section{Finite-size scaling}
\label{app:fss}
\label{sec:fss}

\subsection{Absence of a transition in a finite system}
\label{sec:fss:problem}

For any finite system $Z$ is a finite sum of positive exponentials, hence entire
in $\beta$ with no zeros on the real axis, so $F=-k_BT\ln Z$ is analytic in $T$
and every derivative of it is finite. The consequences are not minor:
$c(T,L)$ and $\chi(T,L)$ have finite maxima at every $L$;
$\langle|m|\rangle(T,L)>0$ at every temperature, so there is no sharp point below
which order switches on; and there is strictly no $T_c$; the maximum of $c$,
the maximum of $\chi$ and the inflection of $\langle|m|\rangle$ are three
different temperatures. So the naive programme, simulating near $T_c$ and fitting
$c\sim|t|^{-\alpha}$, fits a power law to a function that has none; what it
measures is the \emph{rounding} of the singularity, an artifact whose magnitude
depends on $L$, and no amount of statistics repairs the resulting drift.

Finite-size scaling turns the obstacle into the instrument. The rounding is
controlled by one mechanism, it is universal, and its $L$-dependence contains
exactly the exponents wanted. And $L$ is an integer the simulator chooses
exactly, whereas $t=(T-T_c)/T_c$ requires prior knowledge of $T_c$, which is
what is not known. Most of the accuracy of modern exponent determinations comes
from this exchange of an unknown continuous variable for a known discrete one.

\subsection{The rounding-and-shifting estimate}
\label{sec:fss:estimate}
In the infinite system $\xi(t)\simeq\xi_0|t|^{-\nu}$; in a box of side $L$
correlations cannot extend further than the system. There are two ways to see
this. Directly: $ \xi $ is defined by the decay of the connected correlator
$ G(r) = \langle\phi(0)\phi(\mathbf{r})\rangle - \langle\phi\rangle^{2} $ with
distance, and in a box of side $ L $ no two points are further apart than $ O(L) $
--- and a decay length longer than every available distance cannot be realised.
With periodic boundary conditions the precise statement is as follows. $ G $ is
periodic in each direction with period $ L $ and, by reflection symmetry, even,
so a separation $ r $ along an axis is equivalent to $ L - r $; the distinct
separations along an axis are $ 0 \leq r \leq L/2 $ (the minimum-image distance),
and along the body diagonal of a $ d $-dimensional box they reach $ \sqrt{d}\,L/2 $.
The numerical factor is immaterial: in every case the largest distance available
is of order $ L $, which is all the argument uses. In Fourier
language: the divergence of $ \xi $ is the growth of long-wavelength fluctuations,
the correlations at distance $ \xi $ being carried by modes of wavevector
$ k \lesssim 1/\xi $. In the box the allowed wavevectors are quantised,
$ \mathbf{k} = 2\pi\mathbf{n}/L $, so there is no non-zero mode longer than $ L $,
and the modes that would carry correlations beyond $ L $ simply do not exist. The
susceptibility, $ \chi \propto \int d^{d}r\, G(r) $, is likewise cut off at
$ r \sim L $. So there are two regimes, separated by $\xi(t)\approx L$, i.e.\ by
\[
\boxed{\;|t^{\ast}(L)|\;\approx\;\left(\frac{\xi_0}{L}\right)^{1/\nu}
\;\sim\;L^{-1/\nu}\;}
\]
For $|t|\gg|t^{\ast}|$ the correlated regions fit inside the box, the system
cannot tell it is finite, and bulk power laws are observed: a finite sample does
reproduce thermodynamic-limit behaviour, just not arbitrarily close to $T_c$. For
$|t|\lesssim|t^{\ast}|$, $L$ takes over as the only length and every singular
quantity saturates at roughly the value it had when $\xi\approx L$. Saturation
converts a divergence in $t$ into a power of $L$ by substitution,
$\chi\sim|t^{\ast}|^{-\gamma}\sim L^{\gamma/\nu}$, and identically
$c_{\max}\sim L^{\alpha/\nu}$ and
$\langle|m|\rangle\big|_{T_c}\sim L^{-\beta/\nu}$; the rounded peak has width
$\Delta T\sim L^{-1/\nu}$ in temperature and is displaced from $T_c$ by the same
order, there being no other scale to set it.

The estimate delivers the exponents of $L$ and nothing else: no amplitudes, no
peak shape, no size of corrections and it is the corrections, not the leading
powers, that set the achievable accuracy.

\subsection{The five assumptions}
\label{sec:fss:assumptions}

Finite-size scaling rests on five assumptions, set out in full in
Section~\ref{app:fssassumptions}. In brief:
\begin{itemize}\setlength{\itemsep}{0pt}
\item[(S1)] a \emph{single} divergent length, $\xi(t)\simeq\xi_0|t|^{-\nu}$, with
the order-parameter correlator $G(r)\simeq A\,r^{-(d-2+\eta)}g(r/\xi)$ and every
other length like lattice spacing, interaction range, interface width,
staying finite as $t\to0$;
\item[(S2)] one $k_BT$ of singular free energy per correlation volume,
$f_{\rm sing}\approx k_BT\,\xi^{-d}$, which together with (S1) is \emph{exactly}
hyperscaling,
\[
f_{\rm sing}\sim|t|^{d\nu}\ \ \text{and}\ \ f_{\rm sing}\sim|t|^{2-\alpha}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\boxed{\;2-\alpha=d\nu\;}
\]
\item[(S3)] $L$ enters only through the ratio $L/\xi$. Half of this is exact:
coarse-graining by $b$ turns a box of $L^{d}$ sites into one of $(L/b)^{d}$, so
$L^{-1}$ has scaling eigenvalue exactly $y_L=1$, with no anomalous dimension,
because $L$ is a geometric parameter and not a coupling that gets renormalised.
The assumption is that no \emph{new} singular $L$-dependence appears beyond it;
\item[(S4)] fixed geometry, aspect ratio and boundary conditions --- the scaling
function below is universal only \emph{given} these, and periodic boundaries are
standard because they remove the surface term entirely;
\item[(S5)] corrections come from irrelevant scaling fields, contributing
relative $L^{-\omega}$ ($\omega\simeq0.83$ for three-dimensional Ising, hence
still $6\%$ at $L=32$), and from a smooth analytic background that matters for
$c$ and not for $\chi$.
\end{itemize}
Each fails somewhere, and Appendix~\ref{sec:fss:failures} collects those places.

\subsection{The fundamental scaling relation}
\label{sec:fss:fundamental}

Assemble (S1)--(S3). By (S2) the singular free energy density is $\xi^{-d}$ times
a dimensionless function; by (S3) the only dimensionless variable $L$ can supply
is $L/\xi$; and it is convenient to pull out $L^{-d}$ rather than $\xi^{-d}$, the
two differing by a power of $L/\xi$ absorbed into the function. Including the
field, with scaling eigenvalue $y_h$,
\[
\boxed{\;
f_{\rm sing}(t,h,L)\;=\;L^{-d}\,\mathfrak{F}\!\left(t\,L^{1/\nu},\;h\,L^{y_h}\right)
\;}
\tag{FSS}
\]
with $\mathfrak{F}$ a universal function fixed by dimensionality, symmetry,
geometry and boundary conditions and by nothing else. This one equation is the
method.

\paragraph{The bulk limit and hyperscaling.} Fix $t\neq0$ and let $L\to\infty$;
$f_{\rm sing}$ must become $L$-independent. Writing $x=tL^{1/\nu}$, so
$L^{-d}=(t/x)^{d\nu}$,
\[
f_{\rm sing}=t^{d\nu}\Big[x^{-d\nu}\,\mathfrak{F}\big(x,\;h\,(x/t)^{\nu y_h}\big)\Big],
\]
and $L$-independence requires
$\mathfrak{F}(x,y)\to|x|^{d\nu}\Psi\big(y/|x|^{\nu y_h}\big)$ at large $|x|$,
whence
\[
f_{\rm sing}(t,h)=|t|^{d\nu}\,\Psi\!\left(\frac{h}{|t|^{\nu y_h}}\right)
\;\equiv\;|t|^{2-\alpha}\,\Phi\!\left(\frac{h}{|t|^{\Delta}}\right),
\]
recovering the homogeneous form of Section~\ref{sec:critical} and identifying
$2-\alpha=d\nu$ and $\Delta=\nu y_h$. So the $L^{-d}$ prefactor in (FSS)
\emph{is} hyperscaling: assuming one is assuming the other, and wherever
hyperscaling fails --- above the upper critical dimension, most importantly ---
(FSS) must be modified.

Two exponents suffice. Taking $\nu$ and $\eta$ as primary --- they are the pair
measurable with the highest accuracy --- the scaling and hyperscaling relations
give
\[
\boxed{\;\frac{\gamma}{\nu}=2-\eta,\qquad
\frac{\beta}{\nu}=\frac{d-2+\eta}{2},\qquad
y_h=\frac{d+2-\eta}{2},\qquad
y_t=\frac{1}{\nu},\qquad
\frac{\alpha}{\nu}=\frac{2}{\nu}-d\;}
\]
the first of these being the Fisher relation; Section~\ref{app:fssrelations}
derives them. Every exponent appearing below is one of these combinations, so
there are no free parameters beyond $\nu$ and $\eta$ --- plus the correction
exponent $\omega$ and the non-universal amplitudes.

\subsection{The observables}
\label{sec:fss:observables}

Everything follows by differentiating (FSS). Throughout $x\equiv tL^{1/\nu}$, all
relations hold at $h=0$ unless stated, and script letters denote universal
functions of $x$, each carrying one non-universal amplitude that is not
predicted.

\paragraph{Order parameter.} Differentiating with respect to $h$ at $h=0$ gives
$m=L^{\,y_h-d}\,\mathfrak{M}(x)=L^{-\beta/\nu}\,\mathfrak{M}(x)$, since
$y_h-d=-(d-2+\eta)/2=-\beta/\nu$. One caution is essential: in a finite system
with exact $\mathbb{Z}_2$ symmetry and $h=0$, $\langle m\rangle=0$ identically,
the two ordered states being sampled with equal weight. The measurable surrogates
are $\langle|m|\rangle$ and $\langle m^{2}\rangle^{1/2}$; both scale as
$L^{-\beta/\nu}$ with the same exponent but different amplitudes and different
corrections, and must not be interchanged within one analysis.

\paragraph{Susceptibility.} Differentiating twice,
$\chi=L^{2y_h-d}\mathfrak{X}(x)=L^{\gamma/\nu}\mathfrak{X}(x)$ with
$2y_h-d=2-\eta=\gamma/\nu$. At $h=0$ with exact symmetry the correct fluctuation
formula is
$\chi=\beta V\langle m^{2}\rangle$, since $\langle m\rangle=0$ makes
$\Var(m)=\langle m^{2}\rangle$; the variant
$\chi'=\beta V(\langle m^{2}\rangle-\langle|m|\rangle^{2})$ in circulation is a
different finite-size quantity with the same exponent but a different amplitude.

\paragraph{Specific heat.} Differentiating twice with respect to $t$ and
restoring the background of (S5),
\[
c=c_{\rm reg}(T)+L^{\alpha/\nu}\,\mathfrak{C}(x) .
\]
If $\alpha>0$ the singular term grows and eventually dominates. If $\alpha=0$ the
power law degenerates and the correct form is $c=c_{\rm reg}+A\ln L+\dots$ (the
two-dimensional Ising case). If $\alpha<0$ the singular term \emph{shrinks} and
the constant dominates at every accessible $L$, so a log--log fit of $c_{\max}$
against $L$ measures the background, not the exponent.

\paragraph{Correlation length and dimensionless ratios.} $\xi$ is itself a
length, so $\xi_L/L=\mathfrak{Z}(x)$: a dimensionless quantity with \emph{no}
power of $L$ in front. Likewise, since $\langle
m^{k}\rangle=L^{-k\beta/\nu}\mathfrak{M}_k(x)$, any ratio in which the powers of
$L$ cancel is a pure function of $x$. The canonical example is the fourth-order
Binder cumulant
\[
U_4\;\equiv\;1-\frac{\langle m^{4}\rangle}{3\langle m^{2}\rangle^{2}}
=\mathfrak{U}(x) .
\]
Three consequences form the backbone of the method. First, $\mathfrak{U}$ is
universal outright, not up to a non-universal factor, so nothing must be fitted
away. Second, at $t=0$ one has $x=0$ for every $L$, so
$U_4(T_c,L)=\mathfrak{U}(0)\equiv U_4^{\ast}$ independently of $L$: plotting
$U_4$ against $T$ for several $L$ gives a common crossing that locates $T_c$
\emph{without} prior knowledge of it, and delivers $U_4^{\ast}$ as a
universality-class fingerprint (Figure~\ref{fig:fss}c). This is the single most
useful consequence of (FSS). Third, $\partial U_4/\partial
T=L^{1/\nu}\mathfrak{U}'(x)/T_c$, so $[\partial U_4/\partial T]_{\max}\sim
L^{1/\nu}$ gives $\nu$ from a pure power of $L$. The same logic applies to
$\xi_L/L$ and to any RG-invariant ratio, and checking that two or three of them
agree is the standard test that corrections are under control.

\paragraph{Peak scaling without $T_c$.} Consider $A(t,L)=L^{\rho}\mathfrak{a}(x)$
whose scaling function has a maximum at $x=x_{\max}$, a pure number fixed by
$\mathfrak{a}$ and hence universal. Then
\[
\boxed{\;
A_{\max}(L)=\mathfrak{a}(x_{\max})\,L^{\rho},
\qquad
T_{\max}(L)=T_c\Big(1+x_{\max}L^{-1/\nu}\Big)
\;}
\]
The \emph{height} of the peak is a pure power of $L$, requiring no knowledge of
$T_c$ whatsoever: one locates the maximum, an operational procedure, and plots
its height against $L$. The \emph{position} approaches $T_c$ as $L^{-1/\nu}$, so
a two-parameter fit of $T_{\max}(L)$ yields $T_c$ and $\nu$ simultaneously. Thus
$\chi_{\max}\sim L^{\gamma/\nu}$ and $[\partial U_4/\partial T]_{\max}\sim
L^{1/\nu}$ are exponent measurements insensitive to where $T_c$ is. Note that
$x_{\max}$ differs from observable to observable: the maxima of $\chi$, of $c$
and of $\partial U_4/\partial T$ occur at three \emph{different} pseudo-critical
temperatures, converging to the same $T_c$ at rate $L^{-1/\nu}$ but from
different directions. Treating them as one temperature is a frequent error.

grows as $L^{\gamma/\nu}$. Both the height and the shift are exponent
measurements; the height needs no knowledge of $T_c$. \textbf{(b)} The same data
plotted as $L^{-\gamma/\nu}\chi$ against $x=tL^{1/\nu}$ collapse onto the single
universal function $\mathfrak{X}$ --- an excellent diagnostic, a poor estimator.

its scaling function: $U_4=\mathfrak{U}(tL^{1/\nu})$. Hence all curves pass
through the same point at $t=0$, locating $T_c$ and the universal value
$U_4^{\ast}$ without any fit, while the slope at the crossing grows as

from a real universality class.}
