  \foreach \t in {-6,-4,-2,2,4,6}
    \draw (\t,0) -- (\t,-0.009) node[below,font=\scriptsize]{$\t$};
  \draw[very thick,red!30,domain=-6.5:6.5,samples=200,smooth,variable=\e]
       plot ({\e},{0.303964*(\e/2)^2/(cosh(\e/2)^2)});
  \draw[very thick,red!55!black,domain=-4.3:4.3,samples=200,smooth,variable=\e]
       plot ({\e},{0.607927*(\e)^2/(cosh(\e)^2)});
  \draw[very thick,red!85!black,domain=-2.15:2.15,samples=200,smooth,variable=\e]
       plot ({\e},{1.215854*(2*\e)^2/(cosh(2*\e)^2)});
  \node[anchor=west,font=\scriptsize,red!85!black] at (0.75,0.55) {$T = T_0/4$};
  \node[anchor=west,font=\scriptsize,red!55!black] at (1.45,0.29) {$T = T_0/2$};
  \node[anchor=west,font=\scriptsize,red!40] at (3.1,0.15) {$T = T_0$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The filter of Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_integrals} taken apart, against the
symmetric variable $x=(\varepsilon-\mu)/2k_BT$.
(a)~The two factors. The fluctuation window
$ \delta_T = \operatorname{sech}^{2}x/4k_BT = -\partial f/\partial\varepsilon $
(black) is common to every observable: even, non-negative, single-peaked at $\mu$,
of unit area and of width set by $T$ alone. The amplitudes,
Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_amplitudes}, are shown in dimensionless form: $ A_\chi $ is
constant (a unit change of an occupation moves one particle), $ A_c \propto x^{2} $
(the energy it moves, squared), and $ A_{N,T} \propto x $ is odd, so that
$ (\partial N/\partial T)_\mu $ has no normalised weight of its own.
(b)~The normalised weights of Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_kernels}, $ \hat w_o = 2k_BT\,w_o $,
each of unit area. The entropy weight peaks at $\mu$ ($ \sigma(0) = \ln2 $: an
orbital with $ n = \tfrac12 $ carries one bit). The heat-capacity weight \emph{vanishes} at
$\mu$, where the amplitude does, and peaks at $|\varepsilon-\mu|=2.3994\,k_BT$.
(c)~The delta limit: $ w_c $ at $ T_0 $, $ T_0/2 $ and $ T_0/4 $, on a fixed energy
axis. The area stays one while the width shrinks $ \propto T $, so
$ w_c \to \delta(\varepsilon - \mu) $; $ w_\chi $ and $ w_S $ do the same.}
\label{fig:filt_kernels}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Why the heat capacity is blind to the levels at \texorpdfstring{$\mu$}{mu}}
\label{sec:filt_blind}

The vanishing of $w_c$ at $\varepsilon=\mu$ --- visible in
Fig.~\ref{fig:filt_kernels}, and easy to miss because it contradicts the usual
picture of $c_V$ as a count of thermally ``available'' states --- is a statement
about \emph{energy fluctuations}. By Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_C_fluct}, $ k_BT^{2}c_\mu $ is
the variance of $ E - \mu N $ per unit volume, and for independent orbitals it is a
sum of one-orbital energy variances,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_product}
\Var\big[(\varepsilon_\lambda-\mu)\,\hat n_\lambda\big]
\;=\;
\underbrace{(\varepsilon_\lambda-\mu)^{2}}_{\text{energy carried}^{2}}
\;\times\;
\underbrace{n_\lambda(1-n_\lambda)}_{\text{occupancy variance}}\,.
\end{equation}
An orbital contributes to the energy variance only if its occupation fluctuates
\emph{and} each fluctuation moves energy. A level far below $ \mu $ is full with
certainty and a level far above is empty with certainty; neither fluctuates, so
neither contributes, however much energy it would carry. A level exactly at $ \mu $
fluctuates maximally, but a change of its occupation costs zero energy relative
to the reservoir: it moves particles, and so contributes fully to $ \chi_c $, but it
moves no energy, and so contributes nothing to the energy variance or to $ c_\mu $.

The blindness is the additive form of an exact statement. By
Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_mb_response}, $ \chi_c $ and $ c_\mu $ are the variances of two
different conserved quantities, $ \hat N $ and $ \hat{\mathcal{H}} $, over the same distribution
$ p_a $ of eigenstates. Equilibrium mixing among eigenstates that differ in $ N $ but
have the same $ \xi_a $ --- a particle added or removed at no cost relative to the
reservoir --- contributes to $ \Var(\hat N) $ and nothing to $ \Var(\hat{\mathcal{H}}) $. For independent
fermions those are precisely the fluctuations of the levels at $ \mu $.

\begin{quote}
The heat capacity measures the energy variance of the levels in the fluctuation
window; it sees only the levels whose occupation simultaneously \emph{fluctuates}
and which are \emph{displaced} from $ \mu $, and $|\varepsilon-\mu|=2.4\,k_BT$ is where the
product of the two is largest.
\end{quote}

Availability alone is therefore not sufficient. Each observable asks a different
question of the same window: $ s $ asks how much information is missing about
the occupations of the fluctuating levels, $ \chi_c $ how many particles their fluctuations move, and $ c_\mu $ how
much energy. The same structure explains the exponential decay on both sides,
which is that of the window, $ e^{-|\varepsilon-\mu|/k_BT} $: a level a few $ k_BT $
from $ \mu $ is frozen, and the polynomial prefactors ($ x^{2} $ for $ w_c $, $ 2|x| $
for $ w_S $) only decide how the weights approach that common fate.

\paragraph{The microscopic view of a phase transition.} The variance form says at
once what a divergent response requires. For independent orbitals with a fixed
spectrum each term of Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_product} is bounded, by
$ (k_BT)^{2}\times0.4392 $ for fermions (Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_peak}), so
$ \Var(E - \mu N) $ grows at most like the \emph{number} of orbitals: independent
fluctuations add like the steps of a random walk, and the response per unit volume
stays finite (Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_bound} below). The same holds for $ \Var(N) $ and
$ \Var(M) $. The exact decomposition of Section~\ref{sec:filt_mb} (paragraph
``Correlations'') shows what is missing: a divergence requires covariances between
the fluctuations of macroscopically many degrees of freedom, and the additive
spectrum, in which the occupations of different orbitals are independent, has none.
In a self-consistent quasiparticle description, where the orbitals remain formally
independent, the same physics appears instead as a spectrum
$ \{\varepsilon_\lambda\} $ that itself moves with $ T $ (Appendix~\ref{sec:filt_shape}).

\subsection{A power-law density of states}
\label{sec:filt_power}
The value of the filter form is that scale covariance
turns a scale-free spectrum into an exponent by inspection. Let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_powerlaw}
\rho(\varepsilon)=A\,|\varepsilon-\mu|^{r},\qquad r>-1,
\end{equation}
over a window wide compared with $k_BT$; $r=0$ is a flat band, $r=1$ a Dirac
point in two dimensions, $r=2$ in three, $-1<r<0$ a divergent van Hove-like
enhancement. Substituting into Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_integrals} with
$\varepsilon-\mu=2k_BTx$ and $d\varepsilon=2k_BT\,dx$, the temperature comes out
of the integral entirely:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_power_C}
c_\mu=k_B A\,(2k_BT)^{r+1}\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\!|x|^{r}
x^{2}\operatorname{sech}^{2}x\,dx
\;=\;2k_B A\,(k_BT)^{r+1}\,\Gamma(r+3)\,\eta(r+2),
\end{equation}
where the moment integral is evaluated by expanding
$\operatorname{sech}^{2}x=4\sum_{n\ge1}(-1)^{n-1}n\,e^{-2nx}$ term by term for
$x>0$, giving $\int_0^\infty x^{s-1}\operatorname{sech}^2x\,dx=
4\,\Gamma(s)\,\eta(s-1)/2^{s}$ with $\eta(s)=(1-2^{1-s})\zeta(s)$ the Dirichlet
eta function. At $r=0$ this returns
$2k_BA\,k_BT\,\Gamma(3)\eta(2)=\tfrac{\pi^2}{3}k_B^{2}AT$, i.e.\
Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_sommerfeld}. So
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_exponent}
\boxed{\;\rho\sim|\varepsilon-\mu|^{r}\;\Longrightarrow\;c_\mu\sim T^{\,r+1},
\qquad \frac{c_\mu}{T}\sim T^{\,r}\;}
\end{equation}
The entropy carries the \emph{same} exponent with a different amplitude, and the
ratio is exact. Integrating $\int_0^\infty x^{r}\sigma\,dx$ by parts with
Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_sprime} gives
$\int_0^\infty x^{r}\sigma\,dx=(r+1)^{-1}\int_0^\infty x^{r+2}
\operatorname{sech}^2x\,dx$, so that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_SC}
\boxed{\;\frac{s}{c_\mu}=\frac{1}{r+1}\qquad\text{exactly, for }
\rho\propto|\varepsilon-\mu|^{r}\;}
\end{equation}
The Sommerfeld coincidence $s=c_\mu$ is the special case $r=0$ and nothing more.
In the language of Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_norm}, $ \Lambda_S = \Lambda_c $ and
$ s/c_\mu $ is the ratio of the $ r $-th absolute moments of the two normalised
weights; at $ r = 2 $ it is $ M_S/M_c = \tfrac13 $, as the table of
Appendix~\ref{sec:filt_kernels} requires.
Three readings of Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_SC} are worth having.

For $r>0$ (a semimetal) $s<c_\mu$: states are scarce at $\mu$, and the few that
the window catches are far enough out to be heavily energy weighted.

For $-1<r<0$ the Sommerfeld ratio $c_\mu/T\sim T^{r}$ \emph{diverges} as
$T\to0$, while $c_\mu$ itself still vanishes. This is worth separating, because
the two are routinely conflated: a diverging $\gamma=c/T$ is a statement about
the enhancement of the density of states at $\mu$, not about a divergent heat
capacity, and it is entirely compatible with the third law.

As $r\to-1^{+}$, $s/c_\mu\to\infty$: the entropy stops vanishing as $T\to0$ and
tends to a residual value fixed by the lower cutoff of the scale-free window.
For $r\le-1$ the integral $\int d\varepsilon\,\rho$ diverges at $\mu$: there would
be infinitely many thermally active levels at arbitrarily low energy, and both
the third law and the normalisability of $\rho$ fail. The realisable extreme is
therefore $r\to-1$, i.e.\ a residual entropy, and not a divergent $c$.

\paragraph{Metals, Fermi liquids, bad metals.} The exponent $ r $ is a compact
classification of what $ c(T) $ says about the electronic states at the Fermi
level, and it is worth placing the familiar cases on it.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{llll}
\toprule
System & effective $ \rho $ near $ \mu $ & $ c_{\rm el}(T) $ & $ s/c $ \\
\midrule
simple metal & $ \rho(\mu) $, band value & $ \gamma T $ & $ 1 $ \\
Fermi liquid & $ (m^\ast/m)\,\rho_{\rm band}(\mu) $ & $ \gamma^\ast T $ & $ 1 $ \\
marginal Fermi liquid & $ \propto\ln(\Lambda/|\varepsilon-\mu|) $ & $ \propto T\ln(\Lambda/k_BT) $ & $ \simeq 1 $ \\
non-Fermi liquid & $ |\varepsilon-\mu|^{r} $, $ -1<r<0 $ & $ T^{1+r} $ & $ 1/(1+r) > 1 $ \\
2D Dirac (graphene) & $ |\varepsilon-\mu| $ & $ T^{2} $ & $ \tfrac12 $ \\
3D Dirac/Weyl semimetal & $ (\varepsilon-\mu)^{2} $ & $ T^{3} $ & $ \tfrac13 $ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
In an ordinary metal $ \rho $ varies on the scale of the bandwidth, $ r = 0 $ to
excellent accuracy, and the filter returns the Sommerfeld law with the band
density of states. In a Landau Fermi liquid the interactions are absorbed into
quasiparticles which are, at low energy, independent orbitals of exactly the type
assumed here, with a renormalised density of states $ \propto m^\ast $; the filter
then measures $ m^\ast $, and in heavy-fermion compounds $ \gamma $ is enhanced by
factors $ 10^{2} $--$ 10^{3} $. What distinguishes a Fermi liquid from free electrons
is not $ c $ alone but its comparison with other windows: the spin susceptibility
sees the same states with amplitude $ m_0^{2} $ instead of $ k_Bx^{2} $, and the
Wilson ratio, $ 1 $ for independent electrons, becomes $ 1/(1 + F_0^{a}) $ with
$ F_0^{a} $ the Landau parameter, typically of order $ 2 $ in heavy fermions. A
\emph{bad metal} --- one whose resistivity exceeds the Mott--Ioffe--Regel limit, so
that no quasiparticle has a mean free path longer than its wavelength --- has no
well-defined orbitals, and Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_integrals} does not strictly apply.
It remains useful as a definition: a measured $ c(T) $ defines an effective
thermodynamic density of states by inverting the filter (below), and the
characteristic signatures of strange and bad metals, $ c/T \sim \ln(1/T) $ or
$ c/T \sim T^{r} $ with $ r < 0 $, are then read as $ \rho $ logarithmically or
power-law enhanced at $ \mu $ on the scale of $ k_BT $ --- i.e.\ as the absence of any
energy scale below which the electronic states settle into a Fermi liquid.

\subsection{Inverting the filter}
\label{sec:filt_invert}

The exact inversion of $ c(T) $ for the many-body density of states was given in
Section~\ref{sec:filt_lowenergy}; what follows is the additive case.
Since a measured $ c(T) $ is then a linear integral transform of
$ \rho $, it is natural to ask whether the transform can be inverted to recover $ \rho $,
especially at low energy, where the window is narrow. The answer has three parts.

\emph{What can be recovered.} The weight $ w_c $ is even, so $ c_\mu $ determines
only the symmetrised density of states $ \rho_s(\omega) =
\tfrac12[\rho(\mu + \omega) + \rho(\mu - \omega)] $, $ \omega > 0 $; the
antisymmetric part is invisible to it and must be obtained from an odd response, such
as $ (\partial N/\partial T)_\mu $ of Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_amplitudes} or, in practice, the
thermopower. Combining several even weights cannot recover the antisymmetric part,
since they all see the same $ \rho_s $; but, as shown at the end of this
subsection, it does help to determine $ \rho_s $ itself.

\emph{Exact inversion.} Write $ \tau = 2k_BT $ and $ g(y) = y^{2}\operatorname{sech}^{2}y $,
so that, folding the integral over $ \varepsilon $ onto $ \omega = |\varepsilon - \mu| $,
\[
  c_\mu(\tau) = 2k_B\int_0^{\infty}\! d\omega\;\rho_s(\omega)\,g(\omega/\tau) .
\]
Scale covariance makes this a convolution on a \emph{logarithmic} energy axis, which
the Mellin transform diagonalises. Multiplying by $ \tau^{-s-1} $, integrating over
$ \tau $ and substituting $ y = \omega/\tau $ in the inner integral,
\[
  \int_0^{\infty}\! d\tau\;\tau^{-s-1}c_\mu(\tau)
  = 2k_B\,G(s)\int_0^{\infty}\! d\omega\;\omega^{-s}\rho_s(\omega),
\]
with
\[
  G(s) = \int_0^{\infty}\! dy\; y^{s-1}g(y) = \frac{4\,\Gamma(s+2)\,\eta(s+1)}{2^{s+2}} ,
\]
the last by the series used in Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_power_C}. The Mellin transform of
$ \rho_s $ is thus the Mellin transform of the data divided by the known function
$ G(s) $, and an inverse Mellin transform returns $ \rho_s(\omega) $. In principle,
then, $ c(T) $ over all temperatures determines $ \rho_s $ uniquely.

\emph{Why it is ill-posed in practice.} Along the line $ s = \sigma + it $, the
factor $ \Gamma(s+2) $ in $ G $ decays as $ e^{-\pi|t|/2} $. Dividing by $ G $ therefore
amplifies the components of the data that oscillate rapidly in $ \ln T $
exponentially in their frequency, and any noise there swamps the reconstruction.
The resolution is fixed on a logarithmic scale: at temperature $ T $ the weight
samples $ \rho $ around $ |\omega| \approx 2.4\,k_BT $ with a width of the same order, so
structure at energy $ \omega $ can be resolved only to within a factor of order one
in $ \omega $ itself. Lowering $ T $ improves the \emph{absolute} resolution, since the
window shrinks, but not the \emph{relative} one. What survives robustly is the
low-temperature asymptotic series. Matching Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_power_C} term by term,
\[
  c_\mu(T) \simeq \sum_r a_r T^{r+1}
  \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  \rho_s(\omega) \simeq \sum_r A_r\,\omega^{r},
  \qquad
  A_r = \frac{a_r}{2k_B^{r+2}\,\Gamma(r+3)\,\eta(r+2)} ,
\]
so the leading power and amplitude of $ c(T) $ fix the leading behaviour of $ \rho_s $
at low energy exactly, and each further term the next correction. Fine structure
in $ \rho $ narrower than a few $ k_BT $ at the lowest measured temperature is beyond the
reach of thermodynamics and requires a spectroscopy (tunnelling, photoemission)
whose resolution is not tied to $ T $.

\emph{Combining observables at low temperature.} A single observable measured at a
single temperature returns one number, and Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_delta_limit} shows
which combination of the Taylor coefficients of $ \rho $ at $ \mu $ it is. Different
observables return \emph{different} combinations, because their weights have
different moments, and a set of observables measured at the same temperature can
therefore be solved for the coefficients individually. Write
$ \rho_k \equiv \rho^{(k)}(\mu) $ for the $ k $-th derivative and divide each observable
by its flat-band prefactor, $ \hat o \equiv o/\Lambda_o $. Taylor-expanding $ \rho $ about
$ \mu $ under the integral of Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_norm}, and using that the even weights
have vanishing odd moments,
\[
  \hat o(T) = \sum_{k\geq0}\frac{\rho_{2k}}{(2k)!}\,(2k_BT)^{2k}\,m^{(2k)}_o ,
  \qquad
  m^{(2k)}_o \equiv \int\! dx\; x^{2k}\,\hat w_o(x) ,
\]
an exact linear system in the unknowns $ \rho_0, \rho_2, \rho_4, \dots $, with
coefficients $ m^{(2k)}_o $ known once and for all. To the first non-trivial order the
second moments of the table above give, for the three normalised observables
\[
  \hat\chi \equiv \chi_c = \frac{\chi_s}{m_0^{2}} , \qquad
  \hat c \equiv \frac{c_\mu}{\pi^{2}k_B^{2}T/3} , \qquad
  \hat s \equiv \frac{s}{\pi^{2}k_B^{2}T/3} ,
\]
the system
\[
  \begin{pmatrix} \hat\chi \\ \hat c \\ \hat s \end{pmatrix}
  = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & \pi^{2}/6 \\ 1 & 7\pi^{2}/10 \\ 1 & 7\pi^{2}/30 \end{pmatrix}
  \begin{pmatrix} \rho(\mu) \\ (k_BT)^{2}\rho''(\mu) \end{pmatrix}
  + O(T^{4}) .
\]
Any two rows determine both unknowns. From the compressibility and the heat
capacity,
\[
  \rho(\mu) \simeq \frac{21\,\hat\chi - 5\,\hat c}{16} ,
  \qquad
  \rho''(\mu) \simeq \frac{15}{8\pi^{2}}\,\frac{\hat c - \hat\chi}{(k_BT)^{2}} ,
\]
so that the curvature of the density of states at the Fermi level is read off from
the \emph{difference} between the normalised heat capacity and the normalised
compressibility (or spin susceptibility) at one temperature, without fitting a
temperature dependence; for independent electrons the Wilson ratio
$ R_W = \hat\chi/\hat c $ deviates from $ 1 $ by $ -(8\pi^{2}/15)(k_BT)^{2}\rho''/\rho $.
The third row is then a consistency check: the residual
$ \hat s - \tfrac18(7\hat\chi + \hat c) $, which vanishes to this order, measures the
$ O(T^{4}) $ terms.
The antisymmetric part follows from the odd observable: with
$ A_{N,T} = (\varepsilon - \mu)/T $ and the second moment of the window,
\[
  \frac{1}{V}\left(\frac{\partial N}{\partial T}\right)_{\!\mu}
  = \frac{\pi^{2}}{3}k_B^{2}T\,\rho'(\mu) + O(T^{3}) ,
\]
which fixes $ \rho'(\mu) $. With $ J $ even observables and the expansion truncated
after $ J $ even coefficients, the system is square, and it is solvable whenever the matrix
of moments is non-singular; including several temperatures enlarges the system and
over-determines it.

Two limitations should be kept in view. First, the moment matrix is ill-conditioned:
the weights are all of similar shape, their moments differ by factors of order one,
and the information about $ \rho_2 $ enters at relative order
$ (k_BT)^{2}\rho''/\rho $, so the differences of observables must be measured to that
relative accuracy; each further coefficient costs another factor $ (k_BT)^{2} $. This
is the same ill-posedness as that of the Mellin inversion, now in a finite-dimensional
form. Second, the combination presupposes that all observables see the same
independent levels. In an interacting system they need not: in a Fermi liquid
$ \chi_s $ and $ c_\mu $ are renormalised differently, the Wilson ratio departs from $ 1 $
already at $ T = 0 $ (Appendix~\ref{sec:filt_power}), and the difference $ \hat c - \hat\chi $
then measures the Landau parameter $ F_0^{a} $ rather than $ \rho''(\mu) $. The
procedure is exact for quadratic Hamiltonians and should be read, otherwise, as a
definition of effective densities of states, one per observable, whose differences
are themselves informative.

\subsection{Reading the shape of \texorpdfstring{$c(T)$}{c(T)}}
\label{sec:filt_shape}

The elementary reading of the specific heat is calorimetric: $c=\dbar Q/dT$, so
$\Delta T\simeq\Delta Q/c$, and a large $c$ means that a given amount of heat
raises the temperature little because the system has somewhere to put it. The
filter form makes this quantitative and corrects it in one respect: ``somewhere
to put it'' is neither the total number of states nor the number below $k_BT$,
but the number that $w_c$ actually weighs. With that correction, the standard
readings of the \emph{shape} of $c(T)$ follow, and they are worth listing because
the practical use of a measured $c(T)$ consists in running them backwards.

\paragraph{Rise.} An increase of $c$ with temperature means that the weight is
dilating into a region of higher level density, so that each further unit of
temperature activates more excitations than the last. Equation~\eqref{eq:filt_exponent}
is the quantitative version: a pure power $c\sim T^{r+1}$ is the case in which the
spectrum presents no scale for the dilation to encounter, and the measured
exponent returns $r$ directly.

\paragraph{Plateau.} A temperature-independent $c$ is the marginal case
$r+1=0$ of Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_exponent}, i.e.\ $\rho\propto|\varepsilon-\mu|^{-1}$,
for which the power-law integrals fail logarithmically and the level count in a
window of width $\Delta$ grows as $\ln\Delta$ rather than as a power. Since
Appendix~\ref{sec:filt_power} showed $r\le-1$ to be non-normalisable, a plateau
cannot extend to $T\to0$: it is necessarily the behaviour \emph{between} the two
cutoffs of a scale-free window, which is what a geometric hierarchy of levels
produces, and it terminates at the bottom in the residual entropy that the lower
cutoff fixes. A plateau is thus considerably more informative than it looks --- it
identifies logarithmic counting, and it comes with a prediction for where it must
end.

\paragraph{Peaks, and the staircase caveat.} A peak is the generic shape whenever
the spectrum has a largest relevant scale. Its rising side is the filling of the
window; its falling side is \emph{exhaustion} --- once $k_BT$ exceeds the largest
splitting in the group of levels concerned, they are all equally populated, their
occupations stop changing with temperature, and by
Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_product} the sector ceases to absorb heat. The bookkeeping is
the entropy sum rule, which follows immediately from $c=T(\partial s/\partial T)$ and
holds for any Hamiltonian (Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_mb_sumrule}):
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_sumrule}
\int_0^{\infty}c(T)\,\frac{dT}{T}=s(\infty)-s(0).
\end{equation}
The area under $c/T$ \emph{counts the states released}, and a peak is the only
way a finite count can be released over a finite range.

The caveat concerns the frequent claim that a tiered spectrum gives a staircase
in $c$. It does not. A set of \emph{well-separated} tiers gives a staircase in
$s(T)$, whose step heights are the logarithms of the tier multiplicities, and a
\emph{sequence of peaks} in $c(T)$, one per tier, with $c$ returning towards zero
between them. A staircase in $c$ requires the tiers to overlap on a logarithmic
temperature axis, i.e.\ to be dense enough to produce the logarithmic counting of
the previous paragraph.

\paragraph{High temperature.} Whether $c$ must vanish as $T\to\infty$ is settled
by the same sum rule, and the answer is conditional. If the spectrum is bounded
and the degrees of freedom have finite dimension, then $s(\infty)$ is bounded by
$k_B\ln(\dim\mathcal{H})/V$ and Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_sumrule} forces $c\to0$. The
explicit form, for any Hamiltonian, is the $ T^{-2} $ tail of
Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_mb_hiT}; in the additive case it is the Schottky tail obtained from
Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_C} at $x_\lambda\to0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_schottky}
c_\mu\;\xrightarrow[T\to\infty]{}\;\frac{k_B}{V}\sum_\lambda x_\lambda^{2}
=\frac{\Var_\infty(E-\mu N)}{Vk_BT^{2}}\;\propto\;\frac{1}{T^{2}},
\end{equation}
with a finite coefficient. Degrees of freedom of unbounded spectrum and unbounded
occupation --- harmonic modes --- instead settle at the equipartition value $k_B$
per mode, with $S$ growing as $\ln T$ without bound. That value is not an
exception to the framework but a reading of it: the per-orbital Bose heat capacity of
Appendix~\ref{sec:filt_twolevel} is $k_Bu^{2}n(1+n)$, and since $n\simeq1/u$ as
$u\to0$ it tends to $k_B$ rather than vanishing there, which is equipartition. A vanishing
high-temperature $c$ is thus a statement about the finite dimension of the local
Hilbert space, not a thermodynamic requirement.

\paragraph{Divergence.} This is the informative case, and the filter form settles
what it can and cannot mean. Since Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_C} is a sum of terms each
bounded by Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_peak}, the bound holds term by term:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:filt_bound}
\boxed{\;c_\mu\;\leq\;0.4393\,k_B\times(\text{number of orbitals per unit volume})\;}
\end{equation}
at \emph{every} temperature. The constant is the fermionic one; the same argument
bounds the per-orbital Bose heat capacity by its $u\to0$ value $k_B$ and the Boltzmann one by
$4e^{-2}k_B=0.5413\,k_B$, so in every case the bound is $\mathcal{O}(k_B)$ per
mode. A lattice model with finitely many modes per site and a
temperature-independent spectrum therefore has an $\mathcal{O}(k_B)$ heat
capacity per site and cannot diverge at all. (Without additivity the bound is
replaced by the exact decomposition of Section~\ref{sec:filt_mb}: $ \Var(\hat{\mathcal{H}})/V $ stays
finite unless correlations become long-ranged.) Every divergence is consequently a
statement that the spectrum itself \emph{moves}: with temperature, through
self-consistency --- an order parameter that reconstructs the band as it
re-equilibrates --- or with a control parameter, through the accumulation of
level crossings at a transition. And a divergence as $T\to0$ is excluded on the
second, independent ground of Appendix~\ref{sec:filt_power}: it would require
$r<-1$, hence a non-normalisable $\rho$ and a violation of the third law. What
diverges in a non-Fermi-liquid regime is the Sommerfeld ratio $c/T\sim T^{r}$,
not $c$.

\begin{remark}
Equation~\eqref{eq:filt_bound} is a statement about non-interacting levels, and
the escape clause in it --- ``temperature-independent spectrum'' --- is where all
the interesting physics of strongly correlated systems lives. It is nevertheless
the right null hypothesis: any claim of an anomalously large $c$ carries the
burden of identifying either the extra orbitals or the moving spectrum that
produces it.
\end{remark}

\section{Worked example: the mean-field Ising model}
\label{sec:appising}

The table under ``The instances, collected'' in Appendix~\ref{app:histograms}
asserts a list of modalities for $P(E)$ and $P(\phi)$ without computing any of
them. This appendix computes all of them, in the simplest model that has both a
continuous and a first-order transition, using nothing but the standard mean-field
decoupling on the ordinary nearest-neighbour lattice. Exactly one approximation
is made --- the decoupling itself, in Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:model} --- and
everything after it is arithmetic, so there is never more than one error to
track.

Three things make the exercise worth the space. The distribution $P(m)$ is not a
schematic here but a closed-form expression, so ``unimodal'' and ``bimodal'' can be
verified rather than asserted. The Binder cumulant acquires an explicit universal
value at criticality, $U_4^{\ast}=0.270520\ldots$, together with explicit
approach laws on both sides. And the model turns out to satisfy the finite-size
scaling relations of Appendix~\ref{sec:fss} exactly, with $N$ playing the role of
$L^{d}$ at $d=4$, which is a useful check on that machinery.

\subsection{The model and the Weiss decoupling}
\label{sec:appising:model}

Take Ising spins $s_i=\pm1$ on a lattice of $N$ sites with $z$ nearest neighbours
each,
\[
\hat H=-J\sum_{\langle ij\rangle}s_is_j-h_{\rm ext}\sum_i s_i ,
\qquad J>0 ,
\]
and write $h\equiv h_{\rm ext}$ from here on. The obstruction to solving this is
the product $s_is_j$, which couples sites. The standard decoupling removes it by
writing each spin as its mean plus a fluctuation,
\[
s_i=m+\delta s_i,\qquad m\equiv\langle s_i\rangle,\qquad \delta s_i=s_i-m ,
\]
so that
\[
s_is_j=m^{2}+m\big(\delta s_i+\delta s_j\big)+\delta s_i\,\delta s_j
=-m^{2}+m\big(s_i+s_j\big)+\delta s_i\,\delta s_j ,
\]
and \emph{dropping the last term}. That single step is the whole approximation: it
discards the correlation between fluctuations on neighbouring sites, and it is
exactly what will fail near a critical point in low dimension, where those
correlations are the entire story. Using $\sum_{\langle ij\rangle}(s_i+s_j)=z\sum_i
s_i$ and $\sum_{\langle ij\rangle}1=Nz/2$,
\[
\hat H_{\rm MF}=\frac{NzJ}{2}m^{2}-\big(zJm+h\big)\sum_i s_i .
\]
The spins are now independent in an effective field $h_{\rm eff}=zJm+h$, so the
partition function factorises,
\[
Z_{\rm MF}=e^{-\beta NzJm^{2}/2}\Big[2\cosh\big(\beta(zJm+h)\big)\Big]^{N} ,
\]
and the free energy per site is
\[
\boxed{\;
f(m)=\frac{zJ}{2}m^{2}-k_BT\,\ln\!\Big[2\cosh\big(\beta(zJm+h)\big)\Big]\;}
\]
It remains to close the loop, and it is worth being explicit about how, since
the usual instruction --- ``require $\partial f/\partial m=0$'' --- looks like a
second postulate arriving from nowhere. It is not. The quantity $m$ was
\emph{defined} as $\langle s_i\rangle$ at the start of the decoupling, so the
calculation must reproduce it: in $\hat H_{\rm MF}$ the spins are independent in the
field $h_{\rm eff}=zJm+h$, whence $\langle s_i\rangle=\tanh(\beta h_{\rm eff})$,
and demanding that this agree with the $m$ that was put in gives
\[
\boxed{\;m=\tanh\!\big[\beta(zJm+h)\big]\;}
\]
That is self-consistency, and no variational principle is involved in it.

What \emph{is} worth noticing is that the same equation is the stationarity
condition on $f$. Differentiating the boxed $f(m)$,
\[
\frac{\partial f}{\partial m}
=zJm-k_BT\cdot\tanh\big(\beta(zJm+h)\big)\cdot\beta zJ
=zJ\Big[m-\tanh\big(\beta(zJm+h)\big)\Big],
\]
so $\partial f/\partial m=0$ and the Weiss equation are the same statement. This
is not an accident of the algebra. Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:exact} shows that
$f(m)$ is the \emph{constrained} free energy of Section~\ref{sec:minimum} --- the
free energy of the system with its magnetisation held at the value $m$ --- and
for such a function the minimum principle of that section is exactly what says
that the equilibrium state sits where it is stationary.

Two things follow from taking the variational reading seriously, and both matter
below. First, the equilibrium state is at a \emph{minimum}, not at any stationary
point: below $T_c$ the Weiss equation is solved by $m=0$ as well as by
$m=\pm m_s$, but $m=0$ is a local maximum of $f$ and is not a state of the
system. Second, when several minima compete it is the one of lowest $f$ that is
realised, the others being metastable --- which is the entire content of
Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:caseB}.

With the loop closed, the transition temperature follows at once. Linearising the
Weiss equation at $h=0$ gives $m=\beta zJ\,m$, so a nonzero solution first
appears at
\[
\boxed{\;k_BT_c=zJ\;}
\]
It is convenient throughout to measure temperature and field in these units,
\[
\theta\equiv\frac{T}{T_c},\qquad
\mathfrak{h}\equiv\frac{h}{k_BT_c},\qquad
t\equiv\theta-1=\frac{T-T_c}{T_c},
\]
so the self-consistency equation reads $m=\tanh\big[(m+\mathfrak{h})/\theta\big]$,
and the energy per site is
\[
u=\frac{\langle\hat H\rangle}{N}=-\frac{k_BT_c}{2}m^{2}-h\,m .
\]

\subsection{The constrained free energy, and the distribution of \texorpdfstring{$m$}{m}}
\label{sec:appising:exact}

Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:model} delivers $\langle m\rangle$ and nothing more. It
says nothing about the \emph{distribution} of $m$, which is what the table of
Appendix~\ref{app:histograms} is about, and an approximation to an average is not
by itself an approximation to a distribution. The object that carries the
distribution is the constrained free energy of Section~\ref{sec:minimum}, and
this subsection constructs it for the nearest-neighbour model of
Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:model}. Two steps are involved, and it matters that
they are kept apart: an exact identity, and then one approximation --- the same
one already made, and no other.

\paragraph{Step 1: the identity, which is exact.} Let
\[
\hat m=\frac{1}{N}\sum_i s_i
\]
be the \emph{instantaneous} magnetisation --- a function on configuration space,
not an average. Every configuration has some value of $\hat m$, so the partition
function can be sorted into groups by that value without changing anything,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ising_sort}
Z=\sum_{\rm configs}e^{-\beta\hat H}
=\sum_{m}\;\underbrace{\sum_{\rm configs}\delta_{\hat m,m}\,e^{-\beta\hat H}}
_{\textstyle \equiv\;Z_N(m)}\,,
\end{equation}
where $m$ runs over the $N+1$ attainable values $-1,-1+2/N,\dots,1$. Define
$f_N(m)$ as the free energy of the group,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ising_fNdef}
\boxed{\;N f_N(m)\;\equiv\;-k_BT\ln Z_N(m)
=-k_BT\ln\Tr\big[\delta(\hat m-m)\,e^{-\beta\hat H}\big]\;}
\end{equation}
which is precisely the constrained free energy $F(T,V,N;\phi)$ of
Section~\ref{sec:constrained}, here with $\phi=m$. Now ask for
the probability that a configuration drawn from the Boltzmann distribution has
magnetisation $m$. It is the weight of the group divided by the total weight,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ising_PN}
\boxed{\;P_N(m)=\frac{Z_N(m)}{Z}
=\frac{e^{-\beta N f_N(m)}}{\sum_{m'}e^{-\beta N f_N(m')}}
\;\propto\;e^{-\beta N f_N(m)}\;}
\end{equation}
That is the whole derivation: Eq.~\eqref{eq:ising_sort} is a regrouping of a
sum, Eq.~\eqref{eq:ising_fNdef} is a definition, and the denominator of
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ising_PN} does not depend on $m$. No approximation has been made
and no property of the Ising model has been used --- the relation
$P\propto e^{-\beta N f_N}$ holds for any Hamiltonian and any order parameter,
and it is the reason the constrained free energy is worth constructing at all.
The factor $N$ in the exponent is what makes it powerful: free-energy
differences of order $k_BT$ \emph{per site} become probability ratios
exponentially large in $N$.

\paragraph{Step 2: evaluating $f_N$, where the approximation enters.} With
$\hat H=-J\sum_{\langle ij\rangle}s_is_j-h\sum_is_i$, every configuration in the
group has $\sum_is_i=Nm$, so the field term contributes $-Nhm$ to all of them
\emph{exactly}. The interaction term is not constant on the group, and this is
the one place an approximation is needed. It is the same one as in
Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:model} --- drop the correlation between neighbouring
fluctuations, $\langle\delta s_i\,\delta s_j\rangle\to0$ --- which on the
constrained set reads
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ising_mfstep}
\sum_{\langle ij\rangle}s_is_j\;\longrightarrow\;\frac{Nz}{2}\,m^{2}.
\end{equation}
The Boltzmann factor is then the same for every configuration in the group, and
$Z_N(m)$ reduces to \emph{counting} them. That count is exact combinatorics on
the lattice: with $N_\pm=N(1\pm m)/2$ spins up and down,
\[
Z_N(m)\;\simeq\;\binom{N}{N_+}\,
\exp\!\Big[\beta N\Big(\tfrac{zJ}{2}m^{2}+hm\Big)\Big] .
\]
Using Stirling's formula, $\ln\binom{N}{N_+}=N\,\mathfrak{s}(m)+O(\ln N)$ with
\[
\boxed{\;
\mathfrak{s}(m)=-\frac{1+m}{2}\ln\frac{1+m}{2}-\frac{1-m}{2}\ln\frac{1-m}{2}\;}
\]
the entropy per site in units of $k_B$ (so $\mathfrak{s}(0)=\ln2$ and
$\mathfrak{s}(\pm1)=0$), and writing $zJ=k_BT_c$ as in
Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:model},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ising_fN}
\boxed{\;
f_N(m)=-\frac{k_BT_c}{2}m^{2}-h\,m-k_BT\,\mathfrak{s}(m)\;}
\end{equation}
The energy is approximate and the entropy is exact --- a division worth
remembering, because it is the energy term that fails in low dimension while the
counting never does.\footnote{Step~\eqref{eq:ising_mfstep} is exact if every pair
of spins interacts with strength $zJ/N$ instead of only neighbours, since
then $\hat H$ depends on the configuration through $m$ alone. That
infinite-range model is often used to present this argument for exactly that
reason; nothing below requires it, and the nearest-neighbour reading is kept
throughout.}

\paragraph{Two checks.} First, $\mathfrak{s}'(m)=-\operatorname{artanh}m$, so
stationarity of Eq.~\eqref{eq:ising_fN} reads
\[
-k_BT_c\,m-h+k_BT\operatorname{artanh}m=0
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
m=\tanh\big[\beta(k_BT_cm+h)\big],
\]
the Weiss equation of Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:model} again. Second, $\min_mf_N(m)$ reproduces the
$f(m)$ of that section up to an additive constant. The two routes are the same
theory, as they must be, and Eq.~\eqref{eq:ising_fN} is the Landau function of
this model: not a phenomenological guess with undetermined coefficients, but a
partial trace with the coefficients computed.

\paragraph{Why minimising is the right operation.} Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:model}
justified $\partial f/\partial m=0$ by the minimum principle applied to
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ising_fNdef}, and Eq.~\eqref{eq:ising_PN} now makes that concrete:
$P_N$ is largest where $f_N$ is smallest, by a margin exponential in $N$, so in
the thermodynamic limit the equilibrium state \emph{is} the minimum. There is
also a variational statement, which is stronger and independent of the
foregoing. The Gibbs--Bogoliubov inequality $F\leq F_0+\langle\hat H-\hat
H_0\rangle_0$, applied with the trial Hamiltonian $\hat H_0=-\lambda\sum_is_i$ of
independent spins in a field $\lambda$ and reparametrised by
$m=\tanh(\beta\lambda)$, returns the right-hand side
\[
\frac{1}{N}\Big[F_0+\langle\hat H-\hat H_0\rangle_0\Big]
=-\frac{k_BT_c}{2}m^{2}-hm-k_BT\,\mathfrak{s}(m)
=f_N(m)
\]
--- the same function. So $F/N\leq f_N(m)$ for \emph{every} $m$, and minimising
over $m$ is not merely natural but optimal: it is the best bound the trial family
can give. This is the sense in which mean-field theory is a controlled
approximation from one side, and it is why the minimum, rather than any other
stationary point, is the object of interest.

\paragraph{What is exact and what is not.} Collecting: the identity
$P_N\propto e^{-\beta Nf_N}$ is exact; the entropy in Eq.~\eqref{eq:ising_fN} is
exact; $f_N$ is a variational upper bound on the true free energy per site; and
the only approximation is Eq.~\eqref{eq:ising_mfstep}, whose error is the
neglected nearest-neighbour correlation. Everything computed below is therefore a
calculation rather than a sketch, but it is a mean-field calculation, and where
that matters --- the exponents, the value of $T_c$, the behaviour in low
dimension --- is set out in Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:caveats}.

\subsection{Landau expansion}
\label{sec:appising:landau}

Expanding $\mathfrak{s}$ for small $m$: from
$\mathfrak{s}'(m)=-\operatorname{artanh}m=-(m+\tfrac13m^{3}+\tfrac15m^{5}+\dots)$
one integrates to
\[
\mathfrak{s}(m)=\ln2-\frac{m^{2}}{2}-\frac{m^{4}}{12}-\frac{m^{6}}{30}-\dots ,
\]
whence
\[
\boxed{\;
f_N(m)=\underbrace{-k_BT\ln2}_{f_0(T)}
\;-\;h\,m\;+\;\frac{a}{2}\,t\,m^{2}\;+\;\frac{b}{4}\,m^{4}+O(m^{6}),
\quad\;
a=k_BT_c,\;\; b=\frac{k_BT}{3}\simeq\frac{k_BT_c}{3}\;}
\]
This is the standard Landau form, and the two coefficients decide everything:
$a>0$ makes the $m^{2}$ term change sign at $t=0$, and $b>0$ makes the transition
\emph{continuous}. (Had the quartic coefficient come out negative --- as it does
for the Blume--Capel model at large crystal field, or effectively for the
$q$-state Potts model at $q>2$ --- a sextic term would be needed to stabilise $f$
and the transition would be first order in temperature. That case is discussed in
Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:table}.) Note also $b/a=1/3$, a relation used
repeatedly below.

The background $f_0=-k_BT\ln2$ is \emph{linear} in $T$, so it contributes nothing
whatever to the specific heat. That is a peculiarity of this model and a piece of
good luck: the analytic background $c_{\rm reg}$ that Appendix~\ref{sec:fss:corrections}
warns about is absent here, and the specific heat below is purely singular.

\subsection{Case A: $h_{\rm ext}=0$ --- the second-order transition}
\label{sec:appising:caseA}

\paragraph{Order parameter.} At $h=0$ the Weiss equation $m=\tanh(m/\theta)$ has
only $m=0$ for $\theta>1$, and additionally $m=\pm m_s$ for $\theta<1$. Near
$T_c$, expanding $\tanh$ to cubic order gives $1=\theta^{-1}-m^{2}/3\theta^{3}$,
i.e.\ $m_s^{2}=3\theta^{2}(1-\theta)$, so
\[
\boxed{\;m_s=\sqrt{3}\,|t|^{1/2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\beta_{\rm exp}=\tfrac12\;}
\]
(the subscript distinguishes the exponent from the inverse temperature). The same
result follows from the Landau form: $atm+bm^{3}=0$ gives
$m_s^{2}=a|t|/b=3|t|$.

\paragraph{Specific heat.} With $u=-\tfrac12k_BT_c\,m^{2}$ at $h=0$, the specific
heat per site is $c=du/dT$. Implicit differentiation of the Weiss equation gives,
for general $\mathfrak{h}$,
\[
\frac{dm}{d\theta}=-\frac{(1-m^{2})(m+\mathfrak{h})}
{\theta\big[\theta-(1-m^{2})\big]} ,
\]
so that $c=-k_B(m+\mathfrak{h})\,dm/d\theta$ becomes the compact exact result
\[
\boxed{\;
\frac{c}{k_B}=\frac{(1-m^{2})\,(m+\mathfrak{h})^{2}}
{\theta\big[\theta-(1-m^{2})\big]}
=\frac{(m+\mathfrak{h})^{2}}{\theta}\;\big(k_BT_c\,\chi\big)\;}
\]
the second form using the susceptibility computed next. Reading off the two
regimes at $h=0$:
\begin{itemize}
\item $T>T_c$: $m=0$ identically, so $c=0$. The mean-field paramagnet has
\emph{no} specific heat at all --- the spins are free and the entropy $N k_B\ln2$
is temperature-independent.
\item $T<T_c$: substituting $m_s^{2}=3|t|$, $1-m_s^{2}\simeq1$,
$\theta-(1-m_s^{2})=t+3|t|=2|t|$ and $\theta\simeq1$,
\[
\frac{c}{k_B}\;\longrightarrow\;\frac{3|t|}{2|t|}=\frac{3}{2}
\qquad (T\to T_c^{-}).
\]
\end{itemize}
So the specific heat has a finite \emph{discontinuity}
\[
\boxed{\;\Delta c=c(T_c^{-})-c(T_c^{+})=\tfrac32 k_B\ \ \text{per site}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \alpha=0\ \text{(jump, not divergence)}\;}
\]
Per unit volume, $c_V=\tfrac32k_B/a^{d}$ with $a$ the lattice constant. Away from
$T_c$ the exact curve is plotted in Figure~\ref{fig:appising2}a; it falls to zero
as $T\to0$ (where $m_s\to1$ and $dm_s/dT$ is exponentially small) and rises
monotonically to $\tfrac32k_B$ at $T_c^{-}$. This $\alpha=0$-with-a-jump is the
mean-field prediction; it is qualitatively wrong in $d=2$ (logarithmic divergence)
and in $d=3$ (weak power-law divergence, $\alpha\simeq0.11$), for the reason given
in Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:caveats}.

\paragraph{Susceptibility.} Differentiating $m=\tanh[\beta(k_BT_cm+h)]$ with
respect to $h$ and using $\operatorname{sech}^{2}=1-m^{2}$,
\[
\chi=\frac{\partial m}{\partial h}=(1-m^{2})\,\beta\big(k_BT_c\chi+1\big)
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\boxed{\;k_BT_c\,\chi=\frac{1-m^{2}}{\theta-(1-m^{2})}\;}
\]
which is exact for all $T$ and $h$. The two regimes at $h=0$:
\[
T>T_c:\quad m=0\ \Rightarrow\ \chi=\frac{1}{k_BT_c}\,\frac{1}{t}
\qquad\text{(the Curie--Weiss law)},
\]
\[
T<T_c:\quad m^{2}=3|t|\ \Rightarrow\
\theta-(1-m^{2})=2|t|\ \Rightarrow\
\chi=\frac{1}{k_BT_c}\,\frac{1}{2|t|} .
\]
Hence
\[
\boxed{\;\chi\sim C_{\pm}|t|^{-1},\qquad \gamma=1,\qquad
\frac{C_+}{C_-}=2\;}
\]
The susceptibility diverges from \emph{both} sides with the same exponent and an
amplitude ratio of exactly $2$ --- the classic mean-field value, and an example of
the universal amplitude ratios of Appendix~\ref{app:cusp}.
Note how the second boxed form of $c$ above now reads: $c$ and $\chi$ are
proportional, with proportionality $m^{2}/\theta$, so above $T_c$ the diverging
$\chi$ is exactly cancelled by the vanishing $m^{2}$ and leaves $c=0$, while below
$T_c$ the product $3|t|\times1/2|t|$ is the finite jump $3/2$. The whole structure
of Case A is in that one line.

\paragraph{Distribution of the order parameter.} Now use
Eq.~\eqref{eq:ising_PN}, $P_N(m)\propto e^{-\beta Nf_N(m)}$ --- the exact
identity of Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:exact}, which said that the probability of
a value of $m$ is the Boltzmann weight of the configurations carrying it divided
by the total --- with $f_N$ from Eq.~\eqref{eq:ising_fN}. Since $f_N$ is even in $m$ at $h=0$, so is
$P_N$, and $\langle m\rangle=0$ identically at every temperature --- the point
emphasised in Appendix~\ref{sec:fss:observables}. What changes with temperature is
the \emph{shape}:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{$T>T_c$: unimodal.} $f_N$ has its single minimum at $m=0$ with
$f_N''(0)=at=k_BT_c\,t>0$, so
\[
P_N(m)\propto\exp\!\left[-\frac{N\,t\,m^{2}}{2\theta}\right],
\qquad
\Var(m)=\frac{k_BT}{N f_N''(0)}\simeq\frac{1}{N t} ,
\]
a Gaussian of width $(Nt)^{-1/2}$ centred on zero. Consistency check:
$\chi=\beta N\Var(m)$ gives $\chi=1/(k_BT_ct)$, the Curie--Weiss law again.
\item \textbf{$T=T_c$: unimodal but \emph{not} Gaussian.} The quadratic term
vanishes identically and the quartic takes over,
\[
P_N(m)\propto\exp\!\left[-\frac{N\,b\,m^{4}}{4k_BT_c}\right]
=\exp\!\left[-\frac{N m^{4}}{12}\right],
\]
a flat-topped quartic distribution of width $\sim(12/N)^{1/4}$. This is the
mean-field version of the ``universal critical distribution'' of
Appendix~\ref{app:histograms}: broader than a Gaussian, single-peaked, and with a
non-Gaussian fourth moment --- which is precisely what the Binder cumulant will
detect.
\item \textbf{$T<T_c$: bimodal.} $f_N$ has two degenerate minima at $\pm m_s$
separated by a barrier at $m=0$ of height
\[
\Delta f=f_N(0)-f_N(\pm m_s)=\frac{a^{2}t^{2}}{4b}=\frac{3}{4}k_BT_c\,t^{2} ,
\]
so
\[
\frac{P_N(0)}{P_N(\pm m_s)}=e^{-\beta N\Delta f}
=\exp\!\left[-\frac{3}{4}N t^{2}\right],
\]
two Gaussian peaks at $\pm m_s$ of width $(2N|t|)^{-1/2}$, separated by a dip that
deepens \emph{exponentially in $N$}. This is the $\mathbb{Z}_2$ row of the table in
Appendix~\ref{app:histograms}, now computed. One caveat belongs with it. The
mean-field step~\eqref{eq:ising_mfstep} admits only spatially \emph{uniform} $m$,
so the barrier it produces is extensive, $\propto N$. A real short-range system
crosses between the two peaks the cheap way instead, by nucleating a domain wall
whose cost is only $\propto L^{d-1}$, and that is the Lee--Kosterlitz scaling.
The discrepancy is not a detail of the counting but a genuine failure of
mean-field theory \emph{between} the peaks, where the configurations that matter
are the non-uniform ones it has discarded; the peaks themselves are unaffected.
\end{itemize}
Figure~\ref{fig:appising1}a,b shows $f_N$ and $P_N$ in all three regimes.

\paragraph{Distribution of the energy.} This is the point the table in
Appendix~\ref{app:histograms} makes and does not prove. At $h=0$ the energy per
site is $u=-\tfrac12k_BT_c\,m^{2}$, which depends on $m$ \emph{only through
$m^{2}$}. The two ordered states $\pm m_s$ therefore have \emph{identical} energy,
and $P(E)$ --- which is $P_N(m)$ pushed forward through $m\mapsto
-\tfrac12k_BT_cm^{2}$ --- has a single peak at $u=-\tfrac12k_BT_cm_s^{2}$ even
though $P_N(m)$ has two. So
\[
\boxed{\;T<T_c,\ h=0:\qquad P(m)\ \text{bimodal},\qquad P(E)\ \text{unimodal}\;}
\]
exactly as the table claims. The general reason, now visible, is that the two
coexisting phases are related by a \emph{symmetry} that leaves the energy
invariant; a symmetry-related pair of phases can never split $P(E)$.

\paragraph{Binder cumulant.} Because $P_N(m)$ is now explicit, so is
$U_4=1-\langle m^{4}\rangle/3\langle m^{2}\rangle^{2}$.

\emph{At $T=T_c$.} With $P\propto e^{-cm^{4}}$ and
$\int u^{k}e^{-u^{4}}du=\tfrac12\Gamma\!\big(\tfrac{k+1}{4}\big)$, all powers of
$c$ cancel in the ratio and
\[
\boxed{\;
U_4^{\ast}=1-\frac{\Gamma(1/4)\,\Gamma(5/4)}{3\,\Gamma(3/4)^{2}}
=1-\frac{\Gamma(1/4)^{2}}{12\,\Gamma(3/4)^{2}}
=0.270520\ldots\;}
\]
a pure number, independent of $N$, of $J$, and of the lattice --- a genuinely
universal quantity, and the mean-field value of a universal amplitude ratio
(Appendix~\ref{app:cusp}).

\emph{Above $T_c$.} Treating the quartic term as a perturbation about the Gaussian
of width $\sigma^{2}=1/(\beta N a t)$, the Gaussian moments
$\mu_4=3\sigma^{4},\mu_6=15\sigma^{6},\mu_8=105\sigma^{8}$ give
$\langle m^{2}\rangle\simeq\sigma^{2}(1-12\lambda\sigma^{4})$ and
$\langle m^{4}\rangle\simeq3\sigma^{4}(1-32\lambda\sigma^{4})$ with
$\lambda=\beta Nb/4$, whence
\[
U_4\simeq8\lambda\sigma^{4}=\frac{2b}{\beta N a^{2}t^{2}}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\boxed{\;U_4\simeq\frac{2}{3N t^{2}}\;\longrightarrow\;0
\qquad(T>T_c)\;}
\]
using $b/a=1/3$. So $U_4\to0$, the Gaussian value, as it must.

\emph{Below $T_c$.} Two Gaussians of width $\sigma$ at $\pm m_s$ give
$\langle m^{2}\rangle=m_s^{2}+\sigma^{2}$ and
$\langle m^{4}\rangle=m_s^{4}+6m_s^{2}\sigma^{2}+3\sigma^{4}$, so with
$\varepsilon=\sigma^{2}/m_s^{2}\ll1$
\[
U_4=1-\frac{1+6\varepsilon+3\varepsilon^{2}}{3(1+\varepsilon)^{2}}
\simeq\frac{2}{3}-\frac{4}{3}\,\frac{\sigma^{2}}{m_s^{2}} .
\]
Inserting $\sigma^{2}=k_BT\chi/N=1/(2N|t|)$ and $m_s^{2}=3|t|$,
\[
\boxed{\;U_4\simeq\frac{2}{3}-\frac{2}{9N t^{2}}\;\longrightarrow\;\frac{2}{3}
\qquad(T<T_c)\;}
\]
the two-delta-function value of Appendix~\ref{app:histograms}, approached from
below.

\emph{The scaling variable.} All three results depend on $N$ and $t$ only through
the combination $Nt^{2}=(\sqrt{N}\,t)^{2}$, i.e.
\[
U_4=\mathfrak{U}\big(N^{1/2}t\big),
\]
which is exactly the form (FSS) of Appendix~\ref{sec:fss:fundamental} predicts,
$U_4=\mathfrak{U}(L^{1/\nu}t)$, once one identifies $N\leftrightarrow L^{d}$ with
$d=4$ and $\nu=1/2$: then $L^{1/\nu}=L^{2}=N^{1/2}$. The identification is not a
coincidence --- $d=4$ is the upper critical dimension, where mean-field theory
becomes correct --- and the other exponents check too:
\begin{align*}
\eta=0,\qquad
\frac{\gamma}{\nu}&=2-\eta=2
&&\Longrightarrow&& \chi\sim L^{2}=N^{1/2},\\
\frac{\beta_{\rm exp}}{\nu}&=\frac{d-2+\eta}{2}=1
&&\Longrightarrow&& m_{\rm rms}\sim L^{-1}=N^{-1/4} ,
\end{align*}
and indeed the quartic distribution above has width $N^{-1/4}$ and gives
$\Var(m)\sim N^{-1/2}$, hence $\chi=\beta N\Var(m)\sim N^{1/2}$. So this appendix
also serves as an exactly solvable test of Appendix~\ref{sec:fss}.

Table~\ref{tab:appising} collects the three regimes, and
Figure~\ref{fig:appising2}c shows the exact finite-$N$ curves crossing at
$U_4^{\ast}$.

\subsection{Spectral view: configurations, levels, and three different gaps}
\label{sec:appising:spectrum}

Everything above is written in terms of $m$ and $f(m)$. It is worth once
translating back into the language of Section~\ref{sec:filters} --- microstates,
energy levels, degeneracies --- because the translation exposes a distinction
that the order-parameter language hides. ``The gap closes at $T_c$'' is said
routinely about a continuous transition, and in this model there are \emph{three}
candidate gaps with three different exponents, only one of which closes as
$|t|^{1/2}$, and none of which is the splitting between the two ordered states.

\paragraph{The spectrum is an inverted parabola.} At $h=0$ the mean-field
energy~\eqref{eq:ising_mfstep} depends on the configuration only through $m$, so
the $2^{N}$ configurations collapse onto $N+1$ distinct levels --- the
\emph{mean-field} spectrum, coarse-grained from the exact one by the single
approximation of Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:exact}:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:isspec_levels}
E(m)=-\frac{N k_BT_c}{2}\,m^{2},
\qquad
g(m)=\binom{N}{N(1+m)/2}=e^{N\mathfrak{s}(m)+O(\ln N)},
\end{equation}
with $m$ running over the $N+1$ values $\{-1,-1+\tfrac2N,\dots,1\}$.
The structure is the exact inverse of the familiar one. The \emph{ground} level
$E_0=-Nk_BT_c/2$ at $m=\pm1$ is a doublet --- two configurations out of $2^{N}$ ---
and the \emph{top} level $E=0$ at $m=0$ carries $\binom{N}{N/2}\simeq2^{N}$ of
them. Degeneracy therefore increases monotonically with energy across the whole
spectrum, whose total width is $Nk_BT_c/2$ and whose total entropy span is
$Nk_B\ln2$. The level spacing is not uniform either: adjacent levels differ by
$\delta m=2/N$, hence by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:isspec_spacing}
\delta E=2k_BT_c\,|m| ,
\end{equation}
so the levels are widely spaced at the bottom of the band and accumulate at the
top. In particular the gap above the ordered ground state, obtained by flipping
one spin, is $2k_BT_c(1-1/N)$: an $O(1)$ gap that does not depend on temperature
at all. Indeed nothing in Eqs.~\eqref{eq:isspec_levels}--\eqref{eq:isspec_spacing}
does: the Hamiltonian does not know about $T_c$. What depends on temperature is
\emph{where on this fixed ladder the thermal state sits}, and because the spacing
$\delta E$ is itself a function of $m$, that turns out to be enough to produce
the closing gap of mean-field theory --- as the next paragraphs make precise.

\paragraph{The whole paramagnet is one point of the spectrum.} The microcanonical
route makes this concrete. With $u=E/N=-\tfrac12k_BT_cm^{2}$ and
$s=k_B\mathfrak{s}(m)$, using $\mathfrak{s}'(m)=-\operatorname{artanh}m$ and
$du=-k_BT_c m\,dm$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:isspec_micro}
\frac{1}{T}=\frac{ds}{du}
=\frac{k_B\operatorname{artanh}m}{k_BT_c\,m}
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
m=\tanh\!\Big(\frac{m}{\theta}\Big),
\end{equation}
which is the Weiss equation again --- a third derivation, and a check on the
first two. But read it as it stands: $\operatorname{artanh}(m)/m\geq1$ for all
$m$, with equality only at $m=0$, so $T\leq T_c$ over the \emph{entire} energy
range $-\tfrac12k_BT_c\leq u<0$. The top of the spectrum is at $T_c$, not at
$T=\infty$. Every temperature above $T_c$ maps to the same point $u=0$, and the
paramagnetic phase occupies a single level.

That is the spectral content of a fact recorded in Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:caseA}
and usually stated apologetically: mean-field theory gives the paramagnet
$c=0$. It is not that the calculation is crude, but that there is no energy left
to absorb --- above $T_c$ the system already sits at the top of its own spectrum.
The residue at finite $N$ is exactly the Schottky tail of
Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_schottky}: at $T=\infty$ the spins are independent, so
$\Var(m^{2})=2/N^{2}$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:isspec_schottky}
\Var_\infty(E)=\frac{(k_BT_c)^{2}}{2}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
C=\frac{\Var_\infty(E)}{k_BT^{2}}=\frac{k_B}{2}\Big(\frac{T_c}{T}\Big)^{2},
\end{equation}
a heat capacity of order $k_B$ for the \emph{whole system}, i.e.\ $O(1/N)$ per
site, which vanishes in the thermodynamic limit.

\paragraph{Three gaps.} Now the transition. Three quantities compete for the name
``the gap'', and they are genuinely different.

\emph{(1) The splitting between the two ordered configurations is exactly zero,
at every temperature.} The states $m=+1$ and $m=-1$ are exchanged by the
$\mathbb{Z}_2$ symmetry of $\hat H$, so they are degenerate at $T=0$, at $T_c$,
and everywhere between. Nothing closes because nothing was ever open. What
separates the ordered states is not an energy difference but a \emph{path}: to
carry the system from one to the other, $m$ must pass through $0$, and the cost of
the crossing is the object that changes with temperature.

\emph{(2) The energy barrier along that path.} At temperature $T$ the thermally
selected ordered states sit at $m=\pm m_s(T)$, and the crossing point is the
maximum-entropy level $m=0$. The energetic cost is read straight off
Eq.~\eqref{eq:isspec_levels},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:isspec_dE}
\Delta E(T)=E(0)-E(m_s)=\frac{N k_BT_c}{2}\,m_s(T)^{2}
\;\simeq\;\frac{3}{2}\,N k_BT_c\,|t| ,
\end{equation}
using $m_s^{2}\simeq3|t|$ from Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:caseA}. This closes
\emph{linearly} in $|t|$.

\emph{(3) The single-spin (Weiss) gap.} In the decoupled description each site is
a two-level system in the field $h_{\rm eff}=k_BT_c\,m_s$, with levels at
$\mp h_{\rm eff}$ and splitting
\begin{equation}\label{eq:isspec_D1}
\Delta_1(T)=2k_BT_c\,m_s(T)\;\simeq\;2k_BT_c\sqrt{3|t|} ,
\end{equation}
closing as $|t|^{1/2}$. This is the gap that people usually mean when they say
that the gap protecting the ordered state vanishes at $T_c$, and the first two
are related by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:isspec_relation}
\Delta E=\frac{N\,\Delta_1^{2}}{8k_BT_c} ,
\end{equation}
which is the whole content of ``the collective barrier is the square of the
single-particle gap''.

\paragraph{The closing gap is a spectral statement after all.} It is worth
spelling out why Eq.~\eqref{eq:isspec_D1} is not in conflict with the fixed
spectrum of Eq.~\eqref{eq:isspec_levels}, because the two are usually presented
as if one had to choose between them. Flip one spin in a configuration of
magnetisation $m$, so that $m\to m-2/N$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:isspec_flip}
E(m-2/N)-E(m)=2k_BT_c\,m-\frac{2k_BT_c}{N} ,
\end{equation}
which is the local level spacing $\delta E$ of Eq.~\eqref{eq:isspec_spacing}.
Evaluated at the thermally selected magnetisation $m=m_s(T)$ this is
$\Delta_1(T)$ up to $O(1/N)$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:isspec_identify}
\boxed{\;\Delta_1(T)=\delta E\big|_{m=m_s(T)}\;}
\end{equation}
So the gap that protects the ordered state \emph{is} an energy gap --- the cost
of the cheapest excitation available to the system at the place where it actually
sits. In the nearest-neighbour model the exact cost of flipping spin $i$ is
$2Js_i\sum_{j\in{\rm nn}(i)}s_j$, and $\Delta_1$ is precisely its mean value once
the neighbours are replaced by $m$; the identification is therefore as exact as
the decoupling itself, and no more. With that said, and the familiar statement that it closes as
$|t|^{1/2}$ is correct as it stands. What has to be said carefully is only the
mechanism. No level moves. The ladder~\eqref{eq:isspec_levels} is fixed, its
rungs crowd together towards the top of the band, and heating slides the thermal
state up the ladder towards the accumulation point at $m=0$. The gap closes
because of where the state is, not because of where the levels are.

Two riders belong with this. First, ``protects'' is a statement about $\Delta_1$
measured \emph{against temperature}, not in absolute units. Both $\Delta_1$ and
$k_BT$ are finite at $T_c$; what decides the fate of the order is the ratio,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:isspec_ratio_T}
\frac{\Delta_1}{k_BT}=\frac{2m_s}{\theta}=2\operatorname{artanh}m_s ,
\end{equation}
which falls monotonically from $\infty$ at $T=0$ to $0$ at $T_c$, and which fixes
the fraction of overturned spins $n=(1+e^{\Delta_1/k_BT})^{-1}$ through
$m_s=1-2n$. Order is destroyed when the cheapest excitation stops being expensive
\emph{on the scale of $T$}, which is the content of the Weiss equation read
backwards. Second, $\Delta_1$ is a one-spin quantity, and it is not the splitting
between the two ordered states, which is zero at every temperature by item (1).
Conflating the two is what makes the closing gap sound paradoxical; kept apart,
the $\tfrac12$, $1$, $2$ of Eq.~\eqref{eq:isspec_ratio} are three different
questions with three different answers.

\paragraph{What the Weiss field does and does not break.} The effective field
$h_{\rm eff}=zJm$ of Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:model} is temperature dependent
and it does split levels, so it is tempting to read the whole of symmetry
breaking off it. Three statements have to be separated, because only two of them
are right.

It is true that $\hat H_{\rm MF}$ is not $\mathbb{Z}_2$ symmetric: once $m\neq0$
the single-site levels sit at $\mp h_{\rm eff}$ instead of at a common value, and
the ground state of $\hat H_{\rm MF}$ is the unique configuration aligned with
$h_{\rm eff}$. It is also true --- and this is the standard statement of
spontaneous symmetry breaking --- that each individual ground state of the
\emph{exact} $\hat H$ fails to have the symmetry of $\hat H$: at $h=0$ the ground
configurations are $\uparrow\cdots\uparrow$ and $\downarrow\cdots\downarrow$, and
the $\mathbb{Z}_2$ operation maps each to the \emph{other} rather than to itself.
The set of ground states is symmetric; no member of it is.

What is \emph{not} true is that the Weiss field protects the up-ordered state
against the down-ordered one. At $h=0$ the free energy is exactly even,
$f_N(m)=f_N(-m)$, so the two self-consistent solutions $\pm m_s$ have identical
free energy, identical energy and identical entropy at every temperature, and the
splitting between them is zero --- item~(1) above, which no amount of
self-consistency alters. The Weiss field does not lower $+m_s$ relative to
$-m_s$; it lowers \emph{both}, by the same $\Delta F$, relative to the disordered
level. What it protects each ordered state against is \emph{local} decay, by
making the cheapest excitation cost $\Delta_1$; and what stops the system from
visiting the other sector is not an energy difference but the extensive barrier
$N\Delta F$ between them, i.e.\ the loss of ergodicity recorded in
Section~\ref{sec:firstorder}, not a level splitting. A genuine external field
\emph{does} split the two sectors, by $2Nhm_s$, and that is exactly the
first-order line of Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:caseB}. The operational difference
between a real field and a Weiss field is precisely this.

The third statement needs the most care: mean-field theory does not \emph{derive}
the symmetry breaking, it assumes it. The number $m$ is chosen by hand at the
decoupling step and the theory then checks that the choice reproduces itself. Had
one set $m=0$ the algebra would have been equally consistent, and for $T>T_c$
that is the only consistent choice. The non-vacuous content is therefore not
``the symmetry is broken'' but the \emph{range} over which a broken solution
exists at all, together with the $T_c$ that bounds it. The corresponding exact
statement is weaker still, and belongs with Appendix~\ref{sec:fss:problem}: at
finite $N$ nothing is broken, $\langle m\rangle=0$ identically at every
temperature, and a nonzero magnetisation survives only in the order
$N\to\infty$ first and $h\to0^{+}$ second. Taken the other way round the limits
give zero at every temperature. Symmetry breaking in this model is a statement
about barriers and the order of two limits, not about a level splitting between
up and down.

\paragraph{The free energy closes the barrier one power faster.} Against
Eq.~\eqref{eq:isspec_dE} set the free-energy barrier computed in
Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:caseA}, $\Delta F=\tfrac34Nk_BT_c\,t^{2}$. The two
differ by the entropy released at the crossing point, $T\Delta S=\Delta E-\Delta
F$ with $\Delta S=Nk_B[\ln2-\mathfrak{s}(m_s)]$, and expanding
$\mathfrak{s}(m)=\ln2-m^{2}/2-m^{4}/12-\dots$ shows exactly how the cancellation
works:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:isspec_cancel}
\frac{\Delta F}{N}
=\underbrace{\frac{k_BT_c}{2}m_s^{2}}_{\Delta E/N}
-\underbrace{k_BT\Big(\frac{m_s^{2}}{2}+\frac{m_s^{4}}{12}+\dots\Big)}_{T\Delta S/N}
=\frac{k_BT_c}{2}m_s^{2}\,(1-\theta)-\frac{k_BT}{12}m_s^{4}+\dots
\end{equation}
The leading terms cancel \emph{identically} at $\theta=1$; what survives is
$O(m_s^{2}|t|)=O(t^{2})$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:isspec_ratio}
\boxed{\;
\Delta_1\sim|t|^{1/2},
\qquad
\Delta E\sim|t|,
\qquad
\Delta F\sim t^{2},
\qquad
\frac{\Delta F}{\Delta E}\simeq\frac{|t|}{2}\;}
\end{equation}
Near $T_c$ the entropy of the disordered level pays for all but a fraction
$|t|/2$ of the energetic cost of crossing it. That is the quantitative version of
the statement that a continuous transition is driven by entropy: the energy
barrier is still there, and still extensive, but it has been almost exactly
refunded.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccccc}
\toprule
$\theta$ & $|t|$ & $m_s$ & $\Delta_1/k_BT_c$ & $\Delta E/Nk_BT_c$
& $\Delta F/Nk_BT_c$ \\
\midrule
$0.99$ & $0.01$ & $0.17251$ & $0.34502$ & $0.014881$ & $0.0000747$ \\
$0.95$ & $0.05$ & $0.37949$ & $0.75897$ & $0.072005$ & $0.0018560$ \\
$0.90$ & $0.10$ & $0.52543$ & $1.05086$ & $0.138038$ & $0.0073432$ \\
$0.80$ & $0.20$ & $0.71041$ & $1.42082$ & $0.252342$ & $0.0286816$ \\
$0.60$ & $0.40$ & $0.90733$ & $1.81466$ & $0.411630$ & $0.1082834$ \\
$0.20$ & $0.80$ & $0.99991$ & $1.99982$ & $0.499909$ & $0.3613797$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Both barriers are extensive, which is why symmetry breaking survives the
thermodynamic limit at every $T<T_c$: the ratio $P_N(0)/P_N(\pm m_s)=e^{-\beta
N\Delta F}$ of Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:caseA} is exponentially small in $N$ for
any fixed $t<0$. The $t^{2}$ rather than $|t|$ is what decides how \emph{close}
to $T_c$ a finite system still looks bimodal, namely $N t^{2}\gtrsim1$; and
$Nt^{2}\sim1$ is precisely the finite-size scaling combination $L^{1/\nu}t$ with
$N=L^{d}$, $d=4$ and $\nu=\tfrac12$, so the spectral bookkeeping and the scaling
analysis of Appendix~\ref{sec:fss} agree, as they must.
