  \node[anchor=west,font=\scriptsize,align=left,inner sep=2pt] at (1.12,0.60)
       {finite \emph{and}\\ continuous at $T_t$};
  \draw[thin,gray!60] (1.11,0.55) -- (1.01,0.27);
  \node[anchor=east,font=\scriptsize,blue!60!black,inner sep=2pt] at (0.62,1.10) {metastable};
  \draw[blue!60!black,thin] (0.635,1.08) -- (0.72,0.90);
  \node[anchor=north,font=\small] at (0.92,-0.30) {(c) susceptibility};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Observables of the mean-field $q=10$ Potts model across its first-order
transition; dashed curves are metastable branches, terminating at the spinodals
$T_0$ and $T_1$. \textbf{(a)} The order parameter jumps discontinuously by
$m_t=(q-2)/(q-1)$ at $T_t$ --- the defining first-order signature. The disordered
branch survives, metastable, down to $T_0$; the ordered branch up to $T_1$.
\textbf{(b)} The specific heat is identically zero above $T_t$, jumps to a finite
value below, and carries in addition a delta function of weight $L$ (arrow); at
finite $N$ that delta becomes a peak of height $\propto N$ and width
$\propto1/N$, which is the $c_{\max}\sim L^{d}$ entry of
Appendix~\ref{sec:fss:firstorder}. \textbf{(c)} The susceptibility stays
\emph{finite} through the transition --- and in this model is even continuous
there, since $D(0)=D(m_t)$ exactly. The Curie--Weiss divergence at $T_0$, and the
matching one at $T_1$, lie on the metastable continuations and are never reached:
a first-order transition has no critical fluctuations.}
\label{fig:apppotts2}
\end{figure}


\section{The generic structure of a mean-field theory}
\label{sec:mfgeneric}

Appendices~\ref{sec:appising} and~\ref{sec:apppotts} worked two mean-field
theories in full, and the Bogoliubov--de Gennes (BdG) treatment of
superconductivity is a third of the same kind. Read side by side they invite a
contradiction. In the superconducting case one says without hesitation that a gap
opens and \emph{protects} the condensate, and that the gap closes as $T\to
T_c^-$; in Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:spectrum} it was insisted that the Weiss
field does \emph{not} protect the up-ordered Ising state against the down-ordered
one, which stays exactly degenerate with it at every temperature. Both statements
are correct, and they are about different things. This section sets out the
structure common to all three so that which is which can be read off, once, in
general.

\subsection{The construction}
\label{sec:mfgeneric:construction}

Every case has the same four steps.

\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
\item A Hamiltonian $\hat H$ invariant under a symmetry group $G$:
$U_g\hat HU_g^{\dagger}=\hat H$ for all $g\in G$.
\item A choice of order parameter: an operator $\hat\Phi$, transforming in some
representation of $G$, whose expectation value is expected to be nonzero in the
ordered phase.
\item The decoupling. The interaction is replaced by a coupling of $\hat\Phi$ to
its own average, leaving a Hamiltonian that is \emph{one-body} in the elementary
degrees of freedom and therefore exactly solvable,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mfg_HMF}
\hat H\;\longrightarrow\;\hat H_{\rm MF}[\Phi]
=\hat H_{\rm one\text{-}body}[\Phi]\;+\;\mathcal{C}(\Phi),
\end{equation}
with $\mathcal{C}$ a $c$-number that corrects the double counting of the
interaction. Its presence is not decorative: without it the free energy is wrong,
as the $\tfrac{zJ}{2}m^{2}$ of Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:model} shows.
\item Self-consistency, $\Phi=\langle\hat\Phi\rangle_{\hat H_{\rm MF}[\Phi]}$,
equivalently stationarity of $f[\Phi]=-k_BT\ln\Tr\,e^{-\beta\hat H_{\rm
MF}[\Phi]}$, evaluated per site or per unit volume.
\end{enumerate}

The three instances, in one table:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
& Ising (Weiss) & $q$-state Potts & BCS/BdG \\
\midrule
symmetry $G$ & $\mathbb{Z}_2$ & $S_q$ & $U(1)$ \\
order parameter $\Phi$ & $m=\langle s_i\rangle$ & $\{x_a\}$ & $\Delta=g\sum_k\langle c_{-k\downarrow}c_{k\uparrow}\rangle$ \\
$G$ acts by & $m\mapsto-m$ & permuting $x_a$ & $\Delta\mapsto e^{2i\theta}\Delta$ \\
one-body term & $-zJm\sum_is_i$ & $-zJ\sum_{ia}x_an_i^{a}$
  & $-\sum_k(\Delta c^{\dagger}_{k\uparrow}c^{\dagger}_{-k\downarrow}+{\rm h.c.})$ \\
double counting $\mathcal{C}$ & $\tfrac{N zJ}{2}m^{2}$
  & $\tfrac{NzJ}{2}\sum_ax_a^{2}$ & $|\Delta|^{2}/g$ \\
spectrum of $\hat H_{\rm MF}$ & $\mp zJm$ per site & $-zJx_a$, $q$ per site
  & $\pm\sqrt{\xi_k^{2}+|\Delta|^{2}}$ \\
local gap $\Delta_{\rm loc}$ & $2zJm$ & $zJ\,m$ & $2|\Delta|$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
In each row of the last three lines the spectrum of the solvable Hamiltonian
depends on $\Phi$, and hence --- once self-consistency has fixed $\Phi=\Phi(T)$
--- on temperature. This is the ``moving spectrum'' of
Appendix~\ref{sec:filt_shape}, and it is what every mean-field theory has in
common: an exactly solvable one-body problem whose levels are placed by the
state they are supposed to describe.

\subsection{The orbit is exactly flat}
\label{sec:mfgeneric:orbit}

Now the point that causes the trouble. Let $g\in G$ and let $g\!\cdot\!\Phi$
denote the transformed order parameter. Because $\hat\Phi$ transforms in a
representation of $G$ and $\mathcal{C}$ is built from $G$-invariants of $\Phi$
--- $m^{2}$, $\sum_ax_a^{2}$, $|\Delta|^{2}$ respectively --- the decoupling is
\emph{covariant},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mfg_covariance}
\hat H_{\rm MF}[g\!\cdot\!\Phi]=U_g\,\hat H_{\rm MF}[\Phi]\,U_g^{\dagger}.
\end{equation}
Unitary equivalence preserves the spectrum, hence the partition function, hence
everything computed from it:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mfg_flat}
\boxed{\;f[g\!\cdot\!\Phi]=f[\Phi],\qquad u[g\!\cdot\!\Phi]=u[\Phi],\qquad
s[g\!\cdot\!\Phi]=s[\Phi]
\qquad\text{for every }g\in G\text{ and every }T .\;}
\end{equation}
The free energy is \emph{constant on the orbit} $G\!\cdot\!\Phi$. This is a
one-line theorem, not a property of any particular model, and it has no
exceptions within mean-field theory: a mean field can never split states related
by a symmetry of $\hat H$, because the mean field is itself carried along by the
transformation. Applied to the three instances it says that $f(+m_s)=f(-m_s)$
identically, that the $q$ ordered Potts rays are exactly degenerate, and that all
phases of $\Delta$ are exactly degenerate. The last of these nobody ever doubts,
because the orbit is visibly a circle; the first is the same statement about a
two-element orbit, and reads as surprising only because with $|G|=2$ the phrase
``the ordered state'' sounds like the name of one of them.

\subsection{Three energies, not one}
\label{sec:mfgeneric:three}

With Eq.~\eqref{eq:mfg_flat} in hand, three distinct scales can be kept apart.
Every mean-field theory has all three, and confusion between the first and the
second is the whole of the apparent paradox.

\paragraph{(1) The local gap $\Delta_{\rm loc}$.} The cost of the cheapest
excitation of $\hat H_{\rm MF}[\Phi]$ \emph{at fixed $\Phi$}. This is what
``protects'' the ordered state, and it is the last line of the table: flipping one
spin against the Weiss field, moving one site out of the favoured Potts state,
breaking one Cooper pair. Since $\hat H_{\rm MF}$ depends on $\Phi$ through a
term linear in it, $\Delta_{\rm loc}\propto|\Phi|$, so at a continuous transition
with $\beta_{\rm exp}=\tfrac12$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mfg_gap}
\Delta_{\rm loc}(T)\;\propto\;|\Phi(T)|\;\sim\;|t|^{1/2}.
\end{equation}
The exponent is universal within mean-field theory; the amplitude is not. In
units of $k_BT_c$ it is $2\sqrt3\,|t|^{1/2}=3.46\,|t|^{1/2}$ for the Ising spin
flip (Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:spectrum}) and
$2\sqrt{8\pi^{2}/7\zeta(3)}\,|t|^{1/2}=6.13\,|t|^{1/2}$ for BCS pair breaking.
This is the gap that ``closes at $T_c$'', in both models, and the statement is
correct in both.

\paragraph{(2) The splitting within the orbit: exactly zero.} By
Eq.~\eqref{eq:mfg_flat}. There is nothing to close, at any temperature, in any
mean-field theory, and $\Delta_{\rm loc}$ has no bearing on it. A \emph{genuine}
external field --- one added to $\hat H$, thereby lowering the symmetry --- does
split the orbit: a field $h$ splits the two Ising sectors by $2Nhm_s$
(Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:caseB}), and a supercurrent or an external phase
gradient does the analogous thing to $\Delta$. That is the operational difference
between a real field and a self-consistent one.

\paragraph{(3) The cost of moving along the orbit.} Here the models part company,
and the division is by the topology of $G$, not by anything dynamical.

\emph{$G$ discrete} (Ising $\mathbb{Z}_2$, Potts $S_q$). The orbit is a finite
set of isolated points. $f$ is equal at all of them, but any path joining two of
them must leave the orbit, and the free energy along that path is higher. The
cost is the barrier of Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:caseA}, $N\Delta F$, which is
\emph{extensive}. In the thermodynamic limit it is infinite, the two sectors
decouple, and the state must be chosen by hand --- which is spontaneous symmetry
breaking in its precise sense, and is the ergodicity statement of
Section~\ref{sec:firstorder}. There is no soft mode.

\emph{$G$ continuous} (BCS $U(1)$, Heisenberg $O(3)$). The orbit is a connected
manifold, and by Eq.~\eqref{eq:mfg_flat} $f$ is constant \emph{along} it. There
is no barrier at all: a uniform rotation of the phase of $\Delta$ costs exactly
nothing. That flat direction is the Goldstone mode, and a slow spatial twist of
it costs only gradient energy, $\propto(\nabla\theta)^{2}$, vanishing as the
wavelength grows. The ordered state is therefore \emph{not} protected against
phase fluctuations at all; only the amplitude $|\Delta|$ is stiff. This is why a
continuous symmetry is destroyed by fluctuations in $d\leq2$ at $T>0$ while a
discrete one is not, and it is a distinction invisible in the local gap, which
looks the same in both cases.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,font=\small,x=1.55cm,y=1.30cm]
  \draw[->] (-0.25,0) -- (3.45,0) node[below right,font=\scriptsize]{path along/between minima};
  \draw[->] (-0.25,0) -- (-0.25,1.45) node[above left,font=\scriptsize,inner sep=1pt]{$f$};
  \draw[dashed,gray!70] (-0.25,0.30) -- (3.35,0.30);
  \draw[very thick,blue!60!black,domain=0.25:3.05,samples=100,smooth,variable=\x]
       plot ({\x},{0.30+0.85*exp(-((\x-1.65)*(\x-1.65))/0.52)});
  \fill[blue!60!black] (0.25,0.3057) circle (1.9pt);
  \fill[blue!60!black] (3.05,0.3057) circle (1.9pt);
  \draw[<->,thick,orange!85!black] (1.65,0.33) -- (1.65,1.12);
  \node[anchor=west,font=\scriptsize,orange!85!black,inner sep=2pt] at (1.72,0.74)
       {$N\Delta F$};
  \node[anchor=north,font=\scriptsize,align=center] at (0.30,-0.06) {$-m_s$};
  \node[anchor=north,font=\scriptsize,align=center] at (3.00,-0.06) {$+m_s$};
  \node[anchor=south,font=\scriptsize,align=center,inner sep=2pt] at (1.65,1.18)
       {the path \emph{leaves} the orbit};
  \node[anchor=north,font=\small,align=center] at (1.55,-0.48)

\hfill
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,font=\small,x=1.55cm,y=1.30cm]
  \draw[->] (-0.25,0) -- (3.45,0) node[below right,font=\scriptsize]{phase $\theta$};

\textbf{(a)} For a discrete $G$ the orbit is a set of isolated points, so the
connecting path must leave it and pay the extensive barrier $N\Delta F$; this is
ergodicity breaking, and there is no soft mode. \textbf{(b)} For a continuous $G$

\subsection{What ``the gap protects the ordered state'' means}
\label{sec:mfgeneric:reconcile}

The reconciliation is now a matter of naming. In BCS the statement is that
breaking a pair costs $2|\Delta|$, so the condensate is stable against
quasiparticle excitation, and that this protection weakens as $|\Delta|\to0$ at
$T_c$. That is item~(1), and nobody reads it as a claim that $\Delta$ and
$-\Delta$ differ in energy --- the $U(1)$ degeneracy is manifest. In the Ising
model the statement is that flipping a spin costs $2zJm_s$, so the ordered state
is stable against local excitation, and that this protection weakens as
$m_s\to0$. That is the same item~(1), and it is equally correct.

What does \emph{not} transfer is the reading of ``the ordered state'' as ``this
one rather than that one''. In neither model does the gap do that work. In BCS
nothing does it: the phases are degenerate and freely connected. In Ising the
work is done by item~(3), an extensive barrier that has nothing to do with the
gap and a different exponent, $t^{2}$ against $|t|^{1/2}$
(Eq.~\eqref{eq:isspec_ratio}). The two models differ in item~(3) and agree in
items~(1) and~(2); the superconducting intuition is sound, and transfers to Ising
exactly as far as the local gap, which is all it was ever about.

\subsection{The heat capacity, uniformly}
\label{sec:mfgeneric:heat}

One quantitative consequence ties the three cases and
Section~\ref{sec:filters} together. In every mean-field theory $\hat H_{\rm MF}$
is one-body, so its entropy is a sum of the two-level entropies $ k_B\sigma $ of
Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_S} evaluated on its own levels. Differentiating, and
using that those levels depend on $T$ both explicitly and through $\Phi(T)$,
gives the decomposition already found for the Ising model in
Eq.~\eqref{eq:isspec_csplit}, in general:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mfg_csplit}
\boxed{\;c\;=\;\underbrace{c_{\rm frozen}[\Phi(T)]}_{\text{the filter }w_c
\text{ on the instantaneous spectrum}}
\;+\;\underbrace{c_{\rm motion}}_{\propto\;\mathrm{d}|\Phi|^{2}/\mathrm{d}T}\;}
\end{equation}
At a continuous transition $|\Phi|\to0$, so the instantaneous spectrum tends to
the disordered one and $c_{\rm frozen}$ is \emph{continuous} across $T_c$, while
$\mathrm{d}|\Phi|^{2}/\mathrm{d}T$ stays finite and $c_{\rm motion}$ delivers the
entire jump. This is one statement, and it reproduces the standard numbers.

For the Ising model the disordered phase has $c=0$ identically
(Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:spectrum}), so $c_{\rm frozen}\to0$ and the whole jump
$\tfrac32k_B$ per site is spectrum motion --- which is what
Eq.~\eqref{eq:isspec_jump} found. For BCS the disordered phase is a metal, so
$c_{\rm frozen}\to C_n=\tfrac{2\pi^{2}}{3}N(0)k_B^{2}T$, and the motion term
gives $\Delta C=-N(0)\,\mathrm{d}\Delta^{2}/\mathrm{d}T|_{T_c}$; with the
BCS result $\Delta^{2}\simeq\tfrac{8\pi^{2}}{7\zeta(3)}(k_BT_c)^{2}(1-T/T_c)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mfg_bcsjump}
\frac{\Delta C}{C_n}\bigg|_{T_c}
=\frac{8\pi^{2}/7\zeta(3)}{2\pi^{2}/3}=\frac{12}{7\zeta(3)}=1.426\ldots,
\end{equation}
the textbook value, obtained here as a special case of
Eq.~\eqref{eq:mfg_csplit}. For the Potts model at $q>2$ the transition is first
order, $|\Phi|$ jumps rather than vanishing, and $\mathrm{d}|\Phi|^{2}/\mathrm{d}T$
acquires a delta function: $c_{\rm motion}$ then carries the latent heat instead
of a jump, which is the $L\,\delta(T-T_t)$ of Appendix~\ref{sec:apppotts:c}.

The bound of Eq.~\eqref{eq:filt_bound} applies to $c_{\rm frozen}$ in all three
cases, since that term is the filter on a one-body spectrum, and to none of the
jumps. Every mean-field anomaly at a transition lives in $c_{\rm motion}$, i.e.\
in the self-consistency, which is exactly the escape clause named at the end of
Appendix~\ref{sec:filt_shape}.

\subsection{Three caveats}
\label{sec:mfgeneric:caveats}

\paragraph{A complex order parameter has two kinds of fluctuation.} Where $\Phi$
is complex, the amplitude and the phase behave differently: the amplitude mode is
gapped (it climbs the wall of the potential) and the phase mode is the gapless
Goldstone mode of item~(3). Ising has a single real component and no phase, so
this distinction is absent there; transferring superconducting intuition to Ising
one must remember that the flat direction has no Ising counterpart.

\paragraph{Gauge ``symmetry'' is not a symmetry.} For a \emph{neutral} superfluid
the $U(1)$ above is a genuine global symmetry and the Goldstone mode is physical
(the Anderson--Bogoliubov mode). For a \emph{charged} superconductor the $U(1)$
is local, Elitzur's theorem forbids its spontaneous breaking, and the would-be
Goldstone mode is absorbed by the electromagnetic field and pushed to the plasma
frequency (the Anderson--Higgs mechanism). The BdG calculation is unaffected ---
it is a variational treatment of a definite Hamiltonian --- but the words
``broken symmetry'' must be used with care in the charged case, in a way that has
no analogue in the Ising and Potts examples.

\paragraph{Mean-field theory assumes what it appears to derive.} As recorded in
Appendix~\ref{sec:appising:spectrum}, $\Phi$ is chosen by hand at step~3 and the
theory then verifies that the choice reproduces itself. Its real content is the
\emph{range} over which a nonzero solution exists, the value of $T_c$ that bounds
it, and the exponents on the ordered side. Whether the resulting picture is
quantitatively right is the separate question of the Ginzburg criterion
(Appendix~\ref{app:mfchi}); BCS satisfies it comfortably, because the
coherence length is large, which is why superconductivity is the one place where
a mean-field theory of a $d=3$ continuous transition is quantitatively accurate.
